# 'Commentary on Precious Qualities: Entering the True Intent of the Buddha''s Mother' — DharmaMitra zero-shot (english) ^0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^0-1]]

Herein is contained the *Commentary on the Precious Qualities: An Eloquent Explanation for Entering the Unmistaken Intent of the Mother of the Victorious Ones*. ^0-1

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^0-2]]

An Explanation According to the Mode of Emptiness as Directly Taught.
The Meaning of the Title.
Herein is contained the *Commentary on the Precious Qualities: An Eloquent Explanation for Entering the Unmistaken Intent of the Mother of the Victorious Ones*.
Oṃ svasti. I respectfully bow to the assembly of spiritual friends who teach the profound path of the Middle Way—the Victorious One, the Kinsman of the Sun, the Sons of the Victorious One, Maitreya, Mañjuśrī, Nāgārjuna, and the other holders of the Victorious One’s lineage.
With a mind of devotion, I shall well express the way to enter into the mode of the supreme Mother, the source of the assembly of the noble ones of the three times, which includes the Victorious Ones, the Sons of the Victorious One, and their disciples, who are as vast as space.
Regarding this, it is taught that among all the inexhaustible treasuries of the teachings of the Victorious Ones of the three times, the *ārya-prajñāpāramitā* (perfection of wisdom) is like the essence and the ultimate. Although it exists in many forms, such as the seventeen known as the Mother and Sons, this *Ratnaguṇasañcayagāthā* (Precious Qualities) perfectly encompasses the meaning of them all. Because of many excellent qualities—such as the clarity of its verse structure and the fact that it is the word spoken by the Buddha himself—engaging in the study and teaching of this noble text is equivalent to engaging in the study and teaching of the entire *prajñāpāramitā*. Therefore, with a pure intention, I shall express the way to enter into its intended meaning, relying upon the oral instructions of the holy ones. ^0-2

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^0-3]]

Regarding the two ways of explaining—explaining according to the manner of emptiness as directly taught, and explaining according to the clear realization of the path as implicitly taught: ^0-3

## དངོས་བསྟན་སྟོང་ཉིད་ཀྱི་ཚུལ་ལྟར་བཤད་པ། ^1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-1]]

Regarding the first, there are four parts: the meaning of the title, the homage of the translator, the meaning of the main text, and the meaning of the conclusion. ^1-1

### མཚན་གྱི་དོན། ^1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-2]]

First, in the Indian language, *ārya* means "noble" in the Tibetan language. Likewise, *prajñā* is "wisdom," *pāramitā* is "perfection," *sañcaya* is "summary," and *gāthā* is "verse." The detailed meaning of the words of the title should be understood from the context of the main text. In brief, because it is a profound dharma that transcends the world, it is called "noble" (*ārya*). Wisdom that realizes suchness, the abiding nature of all phenomena, is the supreme and foremost among all wisdoms, and therefore it is called "perfection of wisdom" (*prajñāpāramitā*). Because this text is taught with that very non-conceptual primordial wisdom as its subject matter, this text also receives the title "perfection of wisdom." Because it gathers and holds all the precious qualities of the paths and fruits, both mundane and supramundane, by relying upon this text, it is called a "summary" (*sañcaya*). It is a "verse" (*gāthā*) composed in the eleven-syllable *indravajrā* meter, bound by noble melody. ^1-2

### འགྱུར་གྱི་ཕྱག ^1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-3]]

The homage of the translator: Second, prior to the great translators of old translating this profound dharma that teaches the ultimate meaning into the Tibetan language, the words "Homage to the noble Mañjuśrī," the supreme deity, were placed there so that there would be no obstacles and to increase merit. The meaning of the main text: ^1-3

### གཞུང་དོན། ^1-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-4]]

Third, regarding the meaning of the main text, there are two parts: the introductory verses of connection and the actual meaning of the text. ༈ The introductory verses of connection: ^1-4

#### མཚམས་སྦྱར་བ་གླེང་གཞིའི་ཚིག ^1-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-5]]

First, regarding the perfect location, it is Vulture Peak Mountain. The perfect teacher, the King of the Śākyas, Śākya Siṃha himself, was in the midst of a vast gathering of the perfect retinue, including śrāvakas and bodhisattvas. At that perfect time, immediately following the completion of the teaching of the Mother scriptures in one hundred thousand verses and so forth, the teacher, the Bhagavān himself, taught this profound perfection of wisdom—the perfect Dharma, which is the subject matter—to those four groups of the retinue, including the monks, in order that they might easily realize the great meaning with little difficulty and be thoroughly delighted by the qualities of the path and fruit of the Great Vehicle. At that time, he spoke these verses of expression. ^1-5

#### གཞུང་དོན་དངོས། ^1-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-6]]

༈ The actual meaning of the text: The second part has three sections: the exhortation to listen to the Dharma, the explanation of the nature of the Dharma, and the conclusion thereof. ^1-6

##### ཆོས་ལ་ཉན་པར་བསྐུལ་བ། ^1-3-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-7]]

༈ The exhortation to listen to the Dharma. The first part has two sections: the actual exhortation and the incidental explanation of the teaching of the Dharma as the enlightened activity of the Buddhas. ^1-7

###### དངོས། ^1-3-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-8]]

༈ The actual meaning. The first part is taught in a single verse, beginning with "joy and respect." One should set aside the supreme faith that trusts in the benefits of hearing the Great Vehicle and so forth, along with the joy of having a clear mind and a longing for the holy Dharma, and the respect that includes reverence for those who speak the Dharma. Having eliminated the five obscurations—such as the desire for things that act as obstacles to contemplating or grasping the Dharma correctly—which are called the defilements, and having transcended the stains such as stinginess, which are the discordant factors of the six perfections and act as obstacles to practicing or experiencing the Dharma, that noble person who has truly entered into accomplishing the temporary and ultimate goals of all beings through great compassion should listen. "Listen" means that those great beings whose continuums have become gentle—meaning they are of a gentle or peaceful nature, having united the realization of emptiness with the mind of compassion—should listen to this profound perfection of wisdom, which is the path of the great beings, which transcends the sphere of activity of the śrāvakas and others, and which is the sphere of activity of the heroic bodhisattvas in both meditative equipoise and subsequent attainment. In general, since the fruit of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas also arises from realizing emptiness in various ways, such as realizing the selflessness of the person, when speaking in general of the greatness of the qualities of realizing emptiness, it is taught as the sole Mother that gives birth to the four noble ones. Furthermore, the perfection of wisdom, which realizes the equality of all phenomena, is the unique cause of Buddhahood itself; thus, one must understand many key points, such as how it is established that there is ultimately only one vehicle. 
༈ Incidental explanation of the teaching of the Dharma as the enlightened activity of the Buddhas. ^1-8

###### ཞར་བྱུང་དུ་ཆོས་བསྟན་པ་སངས་རྒྱས་ཀྱི་ཕྲིན་ལས་སུ་བསྟན་པ། ^1-3-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-9]]

Regarding the second, there are three—example, meaning, and reason—respectively. ^1-9

###### **དཔེ།** ^1-3-2-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-10]]

Each verse, beginning with "In this Jambudvīpa," indicates this. If, as just explained, the perfection of wisdom to be listened to is the uncommon domain of heroic bodhisattvas, one might wonder how the elder Subhūti and others are able to explain it. As for the example: just as in this Jambudvīpa, all the rivers such as the Gaṅgā that flow and cause all the medicinal plants and forests endowed with flowers and fruits to grow, arise from the power and splendor of the previous aspirations of the lord of the nāgas—the great nāga who dwells in the great lake Anavatapta, who has become the chief or sovereign of the nāgas, and who is free from the fears of hot sand and the like—that is how it is. ^1-10

###### **དོན།** ^1-3-2-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-11]]

In the same way, whatever amount of the Dharma the Victor’s disciples, such as Subhūti and others, teach by summarizing, or explain in detail, or express as being endowed with the four modes of reasoning—such as the method of establishing the definitive meaning—and whatever ultimate, supreme, and exalted bliss of non-abiding nirvāṇa they cause to be attained through that teaching of the Dharma, and furthermore, whatever temporary fruits of that teaching of the Dharma, such as the four fruits beginning with stream-enterer, are attained—all of these arise from the power of the Tathāgata. ^1-11

###### **རྒྱུ་མཚན།** ^1-3-2-1-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-12]]

Why is that? It is because the supreme among men, the śrāvakas who have become disciples of the Buddha, have trained in and directly realized the Dharma-method of selflessness that the Victor has taught, and having done so, they teach it to others just as they themselves have trained in it. All of this is done through the power of the Buddha, not through the power of the śrāvakas’ own strength. Furthermore, one should understand that the enlightened activity of the Buddha is inconceivable, such as when, through the Buddha’s blessings, the sound of the profound Dharma arises even from the insentient sky, whereby those to be tamed become liberated. ༈ The nature of the Dharma that is taught. ^1-12

##### བསྟན་པ་ཆོས་ཀྱི་རང་བཞིན། ^1-3-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-13]]

Second, regarding the nature of the taught Dharma, there are two: the path to be accomplished and the result of the path. ^1-13

###### **རྟེན་ཆོས་འབྲས་བུ་རྒྱས་པར་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-18]]

Regarding the first, there are three: the distinction of realization, the distinction of the spiritual mentor, and the etymological meaning of bodhisattva. ^1-18

###### **རྟོགས་པའི་ཁྱད་པར།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-19]]

༈ The distinction of realization. Regarding the first, there are three modes: the basis as being without a focal object, the path as being without conduct, and the result as not abiding in the two extremes. ^1-19

###### **གཞི་དམིགས་པ་མེད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-20]]

༈ The basis as being without a focal object. Regarding the first, there are three parts: the brief presentation, the detailed explanation, and the summary. ༈ The brief presentation: ^1-20

###### **བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-21]]

The brief presentation. Regarding the first, the meaning of the single verse beginning with "Regarding which, the perfection of wisdom..." is as follows: For a bodhisattva, the perfection of wisdom—which is the supreme object to be taught or practiced—when analyzed by distinguishing the aspects of object and time, such as from which object it originates or which person possesses it, is not found to be established by its own entity; it is established merely as a designation and convention. Likewise, the bodhisattva who is the object of the teaching or the practitioner is merely an aggregation of the five aggregates; in that, one does not find even the slightest entity established as a person or a phenomenon, and it abides merely as an illusory convention. Furthermore, the essence of the path that such a person practices—the ultimate bodhisattva, which is described as being of a luminous nature—when examined and analyzed, does not abide in any extreme, and therefore not the slightest entity established as truly existent by its own nature is found. Having heard this discourse that teaches the nature of the profound reality of phenomena, which does not abide in any phenomenon whatsoever and transcends all objects of focalization, the bodhisattva who is free from the ignorance of grasping at truth—thinking "this is not so"—and who is not terrified by the teaching of emptiness, but who has realized the unity of the two truths, where the nature of emptiness and appearance are inseparable, is one who possesses the fortune to practice the profound perfection of wisdom that has been realized and taught by the Sugata. ༈ The detailed explanation. ^1-21

###### **བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-22]]

Regarding the second, there are two: the non-apprehension of the object of knowledge and the non-apprehension of the subjective wisdom. ^1-22

###### **ཤེས་བྱའི་ཡུལ་མི་དམིགས་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-23]]

Regarding the non-apprehension of the object of knowledge, the first has two parts: the meaning and the example. ^1-23

###### **དོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-1-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-24]]

The first section, "The non-apprehension of form, etc.," consists of one verse. In this regard, when one investigates with the wisdom that examines the ultimate, all that appears as the five aggregates of form—the five sense faculties and the five objects—is not composed of particles, nor is it an accumulation. There is not even the slightest thing that is truly established as a coarse or solid entity upon the accumulation of many. Furthermore, since one investigates whether those minute particles have directional parts or not, the particles themselves are also not truly established. Therefore, one should know that this "form," although it appears, is of a nature that lacks truth. Likewise, all the feelings of pleasure, pain, and equanimity, whether belonging to one's own continuum or another's, when investigated as to their nature of being many and impermanent, do not have even a single extremely subtle part that is truly established. Similarly, the aggregate of perception and the formations, namely the mental factors, also do not have the slightest thing established by their own nature. Regarding such a mode, that which serves as the basis or foundation of consciousness is not even a particle; for since the mind and the phenomena arising from the mind exist as a diversity, it is impossible for them to be truly one, and for entities that arise and cease in every moment, the partless ultimate time is not established when one investigates the parts of before and after. Thus, these five aggregates, beyond merely appearing pleasantly when not examined, are not established as any entity, whether subtle or coarse, when investigated and analyzed. Therefore, one sees them in the manner of dreams, illusions, water-moons, and so forth, appearing while non-existent. In this way, all conditioned and unconditioned phenomena are not established from their own side in the slightest; they appear merely due to the power of causes, conditions, and dependent origination. Even regarding this appearance, which is unerring, when one investigates with the reasoning that seeks the suchness, one becomes certain that, apart from what is merely posited by the mind due to the power of conceptualization as various selves and phenomena, not even a minute particle is found established as this or that. At the time such certainty arises, that bodhisattva does not remain in the manner of fixation upon any of the phenomena of knowledge. Possessing a good view through the certainty of not remaining in any extreme, he practices without remaining in anything, or "practices without remaining." It is said that seeing nothing is the correct seeing; likewise, one should understand the meaning of all such statements as "remaining without remaining" and "practicing without practicing." Thus, knowing all phenomena of cyclic existence and nirvana as lacking inherent existence, in the manner of an illusion, is the "post-meditation illusion-like certainty." This is the pure view that possesses the mode of apprehension of the mind's sphere of activity, which is the conventional ultimate. It is important to become familiar with this for a beginner. As for the ultimate, final meaning of the way things are, all spheres of activity of the mind that follow after words and concepts—such as "lacking inherent existence" and "illusion-like"—are phenomena posited by conceptualization; they are conventional and artificial. They are not the non-artificial, natural state of the reality of the ultimate, final essence. Therefore, the dharma-dhātu, where all dualistic phenomena—such as appearance and emptiness, conventional and ultimate—have become non-dual, is the non-conventional ultimate, to be realized by the pristine cognition of individual self-awareness. It transcends all spheres of activity of words and concepts. In the sphere of meditative equipoise, all phenomena are from the beginning without arising or ceasing, without anything to be adopted or rejected, and by their nature of equality, they appear like the expanse of space. That is the great mother of the victors of the three times and their heirs, the dharma-dhātu, the state of non-arising, the essence of the ultimate bodhicitta, which is naturally luminous and the final heart of all phenomena. Therefore, it is essential to enter into this mode through a certainty that is free from doubt. As it is said in the *Praise to the Mother* by Candrakīrti: "The perfection of wisdom, which is beyond speech, thought, and expression, / Unarisen, unceasing, the nature of space, / The sphere of activity of individual self-awareness, / To the mother of the victors of the three times, I bow." And as stated in the *Mūlamadhyamakakārikā*: "Not known from another, peaceful, / Not proliferated by proliferations, / Without conceptual thought, without distinction, / That is the characteristic of suchness." Because that yogi who is in meditative equipoise on such profound reality does not grasp any phenomenon whatsoever, all nets of fixation and proliferation that are the sphere of activity of consciousness are completely turned back. By relying on the wisdom that lacks any mode of apprehension—which is naturally luminous or the self-arisen pristine cognition, and which possesses the excellence of all aspects—the great enlightenment of the Sugatas is attained. As explained here in some detail, one should understand in this way whatever appears in the following sections, such as the words refuting form and so forth. ^1-24

###### **དཔེ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-1-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-25]]

The second is the example: "Just as..." constitutes one verse. As for the example, when a non-Buddhist *kun tu rgyu ba* (wandering ascetic), known as a *bzo sbyangs* (craft-purifier) or in some places as a *phreng can* (garland-wearer), saw through his own analytical *shes pa* (cognition) that no matter how much effort he put into the *dmigs pa can* (referential) view—such as the belief in the existence of a self held in his own scriptures—it did not act as a cause for liberation from all *’ching ba* (bonds), he came before the Teacher, the Bhagavan, and asked for the path to liberation. When the Teacher taught the dharma of selflessness, he gained supreme faith and, relying on that path of *shes rab* (wisdom) that is *dmigs pa med pa* (non-referential), he attained *mya ngan las ’das pa* (nirvana), which is the *rnam par ’jig pa* (destruction) of the *phung po* (aggregates) that are the appropriation of suffering, or the cessation of the continuum of cyclic existence. Just as that is so, any *byang chub sems dpa’* (bodhisattva) who thus knows *chos* (phenomena) as *dmigs su med pa* (non-referential) does not touch upon the nirvana that leans toward peace, and that *byang sems* (bodhisattva) will abide in the profound *shes rab* (wisdom) of the equality of existence and peace. 
The subject, wisdom, is non-referential. ^1-25

###### **ཡུལ་ཅན་ཤེས་རབ་མི་དམིགས་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-26]]

Regarding the second, there are three parts: teaching from the affirmative perspective, teaching from the negative perspective, and summarizing the meaning thereof. ^1-26

###### **སྒྲུབ་ཕྱོགས་ནས་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-1-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-27]]

Regarding the first, "Again, this perfection of wisdom..." and so forth is a single verse. "Again, this..." refers to the perfection of wisdom, which is the realization that all phenomena are devoid of intrinsic nature; it is the sole source of all the qualities of nirvana and is something to be sought after. However, when one investigates and analyzes, "What is its nature? Whose is it? From what object does it arise?" one realizes that even in that, there is no essence established as truly existent. By extension, one contemplates and analyzes all these phenomena, from form up to the omniscience of a buddha, as being empty of their own essence. A bodhisattva who, possessing such correct reasoning, investigates closely in this way, and who is neither bewildered nor discouraged by the meaning of emptiness, and who is not afraid of the profound reality, is one who has become close to perfect enlightenment. ^1-27

###### **དགག་ཕྱོགས་ནས་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-1-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-28]]

The second, the meaning of the four lines beginning with "If one does not know," is as follows: If one does not know the meaning of all phenomena being empty from the very beginning, one remains in the perception that form is established as having the essence of form. Likewise, one engages with the aggregates of feeling, volition (that is, compositional factors), and consciousness with the perception of clinging to them as true. Even if, based on logical analysis, one thinks that the nature of all these aggregates is merely the emptiness of non-implication—the negation of the object to be negated, which is true existence—and that this is the natural state, such a bodhisattva is not one who has attained the faith of conviction in the profound natural state of the dharma-expanse, which is unborn and devoid of all conceptual elaborations of signs. Because they do not abide in the meaning that transcends the sphere of conceptual activity, which is free from the superimposition and negation of the natural state, they are engaging with signs that have an object of focus; it should be understood that they are bound by the grasping of signs and objects of focus. ^1-28

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-29]]

Summary of the meaning. ^1-29

###### **དེའི་དོན་བསྡུ་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-1-2-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-30]]

The third, the meaning of the four lines beginning with "Whoever does not perceive form," is as follows: The authentic meaning of what is taught in this way is that one does not perceive form as form, and likewise, one does not perceive feeling, nor does one perceive perception and volition as such, and one does not engage with consciousness in a manner that perceives it as established by its own essence. In short, because one does not engage with the signs of objects of focus, such as existence or non-existence, in any phenomenon, including the aggregates, one engages without abiding in anything. This is because one does not perceive, "Wherever I engage, I am engaging," and thus one’s signless perfection of wisdom, which is free from all arrogance, has become stable. One who possesses such a mind that realizes the unborn attains the supreme, peaceful meditative absorption known as "the heroic stride" and the like.
The path of non-engagement. ^1-30

###### **ལམ་སྤྱོད་པ་མེད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-31]]

The second, the path of non-engagement, is the meaning of the two verses beginning with "Whatever bodhisattva." In this context of practicing the path as previously explained, when a bodhisattva’s continuum remains in a state of near-peace, this is because they have been prophesied to unsurpassed, perfect enlightenment by the Tathāgatas of the past. For such a bodhisattva, there is no arising of the arrogant thought, "I am resting in emptiness," or "I have arisen from it." Why is that? Because they have fully understood the equality of the reality of all phenomena, which is the non-dual nature of all things. Engaging in this way is to engage in the wisdom of the Sugatas, for that bodhisattva has thoroughly understood that the supreme way to engage is to engage in the very fact that there is nothing to engage in regarding any phenomenon. Whatever phenomena, such as the path, one engages with, neither that nor the one who engages can be perceived. This way of being is to engage in the supreme perfection of wisdom.
The manner of not abiding in the two extremes of the result. ^1-31

###### **འབྲས་བུ་མཐའ་གཉིས་སུ་མི་གནས་པའི་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-32]]

The third has two parts: the meaning and the analogy. ^1-32

###### **དོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-33]]

The first is the meaning of the single verse beginning with "Whatever exists." When it is taught that all these phenomena do not exist by their own nature, and that as many phenomena as are designated are devoid of inherent existence, those childish beings who are attached to names and characteristics examine and analyze that meaning, yet they are unable to grasp the state of reality that is free from the extremes of grasping at either the extreme of superimposition—that things exist—or the extreme of deprecation—that they do not exist. In the ultimate sense, the two terms "existence" and "non-existence" are phenomena that are equally unperceivable and without foundation. The meaning of non-abiding, which is the union free from all the conceptual elaborations of childish beings such as existence, non-existence, being, or non-being, is a state where all forms of extremes and assertions are completely pacified, to be realized individually by oneself. If a bodhisattva knows this meaning of the dharmadhātu, which does not conceptualize in terms of existence or non-existence, they will certainly emerge from all extremes. ^1-33

###### **དཔེ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-34]]

The second is the meaning of the single verse beginning with "In this context, the aggregates." When one is training on the path, one understands that all phenomena, exemplified by the five aggregates, are like illusions—appearing in whatever way they do, yet untrue. Furthermore, one does not make a distinction between the illusion and the aggregates as being separate, nor does one grasp at a difference in the degree of reality between the two. Having understood that the five aggregates themselves are on a par with illusions, dreams, and the like, one is free from the various perceptions of grasping at a self and phenomena, and one engages in the complete pacification of conceptual elaborations. This is engaging in the supreme perfection of wisdom. ^1-34

###### **བཤེས་གཉེན་གྱི་ཁྱད་པར།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-35]]

The second, regarding the specific qualities of the spiritual friend, consists of two parts: the affirmation and the refutation. ^1-35

###### **སྒྲུབ་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-36]]

First, the meaning of the two lines beginning with "spiritual friend." In this context, because bodhisattvas possess both external and internal guardians, they do not decline from the path of the supreme vehicle. What are these? They possess a spiritual friend who, as an external guardian, teaches that all phenomena are devoid of intrinsic nature through the gateway of wisdom, and who teaches the method of dedicating all roots of virtue toward omniscient wisdom. As for the internal guardian, they themselves possess the mind that aspires to attain omniscience and possess the higher insight that is pure in the three spheres. Such individuals, having heard the profound perfection of wisdom—the mother who gives birth to all the victorious ones—do not become afraid at all, but rather become patient with the meaning of the Mother. ^1-36

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-37]]

The specific qualities of the spiritual friend. ^1-37

###### **དགག་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-38]]

The second, the meaning of the two lines beginning with "whoever." Contrary to what was just explained, a beginner who possesses a "sinful friend" who disparages the meaning of the supreme vehicle, and who, because their own intelligence is immature, "relies on others"—that is, is dependent upon others—will, like an "unfired vessel" that "disintegrates" the moment it is "touched by water," decline from the path of the supreme vehicle. The etymological meaning of "bodhisattva." ^1-38

###### **བྱང་ཆུབ་སེམས་དཔའི་སྒྲ་དོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-39]]

The third, regarding the etymological meaning of "bodhisattva," consists of two parts: the etymological meaning of the term "bodhisattva" and the etymological meaning of "mahasattva." ^1-39

###### **བྱང་ཆུབ་སེམས་དཔའ་ཞེས་པའི་སྒྲ་དོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-40]]

The first, the meaning of the four lines beginning with "For what reason is this..." If one asks, "For what reason is this individual to be designated as a 'bodhisattva'?" it is because, through the gateway of wisdom—such as the hearing [of teachings] that follows upon the nature of the reality of phenomena—they cut off, without remainder, all manifest adherence or "attachment" to all phenomena such as form. They abandon entirely and seek to "exhaust" the conceptualizations, along with their seeds, that are "attached" to all knowable phenomena through the belief in their truth. By ultimately "touching" the great "bodhi" of the "victorious ones," which has become "devoid of attachment," this individual obtains the name "bodhisattva." ^1-40

###### **བྱང་ཆུབ་སེམས་དཔའ་ཆེན་པོའི་སྒྲ་དོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-41]]

The second, regarding the "mahasattva," consists of two parts: being the foremost among sentient beings and possessing great armor. ^1-41

###### **སེམས་ཅན་རྣམས་ཀྱི་མཆོག་ཏུ་གྱུར་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-3-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-42]]

The first, the meaning of the single verse beginning with "For what reason is this..." If one asks, "For what reason is this individual to be designated by the name 'mahasattva'?" it is because, through the greatness of the generation of bodhicitta to protect all sentient beings without remainder and the realization of the profound Dharma, they are the foremost and supreme among the many—that is, the immeasurable and countless—groups and classes of sentient beings residing in the world-realms. Furthermore, they are "great" because the boundless views of self, aggregates, and so forth, held by sentient beings are difficult to destroy due to having been habituated since beginningless time, and are numerous due to being limitless. By teaching the profound Dharma of non-observation, they cut off and eliminate all such views. For that reason, they are highly praised as a "mahasattva." ^1-42

###### **གོ་ཆ་ཆེ་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-3-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-43]]

The second part has two sections: the actual meaning and the explanation of the manner of the armor. ^1-43

###### **དངོས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-3-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-44]]

The first, the meaning of the single verse beginning with "Great generosity..." It is not only for the reason just explained, but also because they have donned the great armor that they are called a "mahasattva." If one asks what that armor is, it is the armor of generosity, which consists of great generosity without attachment to any possessions; the armor of ethical discipline, which is the mind or great intelligence that abandons improper conduct; and the armor of the other four, such as patience, which possesses the great power to overcome the respective opposing forces, such as an agitated mind. Furthermore, it consists of being engaged in the path, along with its fruit, from the generation of bodhicitta up to omniscience, within the supreme Great Vehicle of the victorious ones. In short, by practicing the six perfections, they have donned the great armor of an unyielding spirit for all the non-deficient teachings of the path of the Great Vehicle, which is as limitless as the sky. Because they strive to eliminate the thicket of views throughout all the world-realms of the ten directions and to liberate beings from the lower realms, and so forth, by subduing all the deceptive arts of Māra, for that reason they are highly praised as a "mahasattva." ^1-44

###### **གོ་ཆའི་ཚུལ་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-3-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-45]]

The second part has two sections: the example and the meaning. ^1-45

###### **དཔེ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-3-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-46]]

The first, the single verse beginning with "For example, like an illusion..." Regarding this, the manner in which a bodhisattva dons the armor is of a nature that is fearless and undaunted. Why is that? It is because, for example, just as a magician at a crossroads creates various illusory forms and, when demonstrating the trick of severing the many heads of a great number of people, those who are to be killed are merely illusory emanations—and thus, the more they suffer and the more numerous they become, the more one sees them as false—in that same way, a bodhisattva also thoroughly understands that all these beings, who possess vast and limitless suffering, are likewise merely illusory emanations devoid of true existence. Having understood this, that bodhisattva has not the slightest fear in taking upon themselves the burden of the welfare of limitless sentient beings, right up until the final end of the future. ^1-46

###### **དོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-3-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-47]]

The second, the single verse beginning with "Form and perception..." The reason why sentient beings are like illusory emanations is as follows: although it is said that "sentient beings" are designated in dependence upon these five aggregates, in reality, form, perception, feeling, volition, and consciousness are not bound by anyone, nor are they liberated by anyone. The reason for this is that these five aggregates do not exist in an ultimate sense. That which lacks an intrinsic nature has neither bondage nor liberation, just like the sky. Having understood it in this way, the state of having a mind that is undaunted in engaging in the accomplishment of enlightenment, merely in terms of conventional designation, is the supreme armor of the noble beings. ^1-47

###### **ཉམས་སུ་བླང་བྱ་ཆོས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-48]]

The Dharma to be practiced. Second, regarding the Dharma to be practiced, there are two parts: a brief presentation of the essence of the Great Vehicle, and a detailed explanation of its nature. ^1-48

###### **ཐེག་ཆེན་གྱི་ངོ་བོ་མདོར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-49]]

A brief presentation of the essence of the Great Vehicle. Regarding the first, there are two parts: an explanation of the conventional aspects in terms of their terminology, meaning, essence, and function, and an explanation of how those same things are not observed in the ultimate sense. ^1-49

###### **ཐ་སྙད་དུ་སྒྲ་དོན་ངོ་བོ་བྱེད་ལས་ཀྱི་སྒོ་ནས་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-50]]

The first, the meaning of the single verse beginning with "For what reason is this..." If one asks, "For what reason is this designated by the name 'Great Vehicle' of the bodhisattva?" it is because the Great Vehicle is that upon which any person mounts in order to turn all sentient beings to be tamed away from suffering, that is, to cause them to pass beyond it; thus, it is called a "vehicle" in the sense of a conveyance, for by this path one lifts both oneself and all others to the ultimate result. The essence of this Great Vehicle is that it provides an opportunity for immeasurable sentient beings, and it is of the nature of the profound, being like the sky—the dharmadhātu, which is the nature of the twenty types of emptiness—and it possesses immeasurable, vast arrays, such as samādhi, dhāraṇī, and the perfections, just like a great celestial palace. The function of engaging in such a way is that it causes immeasurable sentient beings to experience, in the temporary sense, a joyful mind and a comfortable body through the bliss of the undefiled, and ultimately, it causes the manifestation of the bliss of the non-abiding nirvāṇa, the ultimate result; therefore, it is called the "supreme vehicle" or the "Great Vehicle." ^1-50

###### **དོན་དམ་པར་དེ་དག་མི་དམིགས་པར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-51]]

The second, the meaning of the single verse beginning with "Whatever person mounts..." In ultimate reality, there is no person whatsoever who mounts the Great Vehicle and proceeds toward the direction of the result that can be apprehended. If one asks, "How then is it said that by relying on this vehicle, one proceeds to the result of nirvāṇa?" that is taught only in terms of conventional designation. In the sense of ultimate reality, there is no such thing as "proceeding to nirvāṇa" that can be apprehended or is an object of apprehension. Just as when fuel is exhausted and a fire dies out, that fire has nowhere else to go, in the same way, because the cause—the conceptualization of inverted, mistaken thoughts—has been exhausted, that person is thoroughly spoken of as having attained nirvāṇa. ^1-51

###### **དེའི་རང་བཞིན་རྒྱས་པར་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-52]]

The detailed explanation of its nature. The second, the detailed explanation, has two parts: the demonstration that the person and the path are without intrinsic nature, and the summary of their meaning. The person. ^1-52

###### **གང་ཟག་དང་ལམ་རང་བཞིན་མེད་པར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-53]]

The first part has two sections: the demonstration that the person who practices the path is not to be apprehended, and the demonstration that the path, the perfection of wisdom, is not to be apprehended. ^1-53

###### **ལམ་སྒྲུབ་པ་པོ་གང་ཟག་མི་དམིགས་པར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-2-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-54]]

The first, the meaning of the single verse beginning with "The bodhisattva..." That person who practices the path, the bodhisattva, is not to be apprehended in any of the following: the prior limit, which is the past or the state of saṃsāra; the posterior limit, which is the future or the state of non-learning; or the present, the time of learning the path. This is because the person is unborn from the very beginning in their own essence and will not be born later; they are pure of the marks of the three times, and thus cannot be distinguished by anything whatsoever. That which is called "the bodhisattva" is, in ultimate reality, unconditioned; therefore, there is no conceptual proliferation of conventional designations such as "one who practices the path" or "one who attains the result." To know it in this way is to engage in the supreme perfection of wisdom.
Demonstrating that the path is without intrinsic nature. ^1-54

###### **ལམ་ཤེར་ཕྱིན་མི་དམིགས་པར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-2-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-55]]

The second part has two sections: what is to be engaged in, and what is to be avoided. ^1-55

###### **འཇུག་བྱ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-2-2-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-56]]

The first is the meaning of the single verse beginning with "The bodhisattva who is skilled and knows..." When a bodhisattva who is skilled in conventional reality and knows the suchness of ultimate reality engages in their practice, they analyze the essence of the causes and results of all phenomena, contemplate the meaning of their being unborn from the very beginning, and thus act and abide. What is that? Even while generating great compassion that is devoid of apprehension, they have no perception of sentient beings; this is to engage in the supreme perfection of wisdom. ^1-56

###### **ལྡོག་བྱ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-2-2-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-57]]

The second is the meaning of the single verse beginning with "If there is a perception of sentient beings..." If a bodhisattva generates a perception of sentient beings as truly existent, and likewise generates a perception of suffering, thinking, "I shall perform the benefit of beings and eliminate their suffering," and thus conceptualizes in a manner of grasping at self and sentient beings, they have not transcended conceptualization. Such a person is not engaging in the supreme perfection of wisdom, but is abiding on the level of those who have apprehension. Summarizing the meaning of that. ^1-57

###### **དོན་བསྡུ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-2-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-58]]

The third is the summary of the meaning. It is the meaning of the single verse beginning with "What is the self like..." A bodhisattva knows all sentient beings to be just as the self is, in that it is not established by its own nature; and having known all phenomena, such as the generation of bodhicitta, to be just as all sentient beings are, in that they are devoid of own-nature, they abide in the meaning of non-elaboration—the state of reality where the two truths are inseparable, without conceptualizing either non-arising or arising. This is to engage in the state of the supreme perfection of wisdom. 
Indicating the result of that. ^1-58

###### **འབྲས་བུ་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-59]]

The third is the result. It is the meaning of the single verse beginning with "In the world, the names of phenomena..." What is the nature of that which is attained by that non-conceptual primordial wisdom? In the world, all the names of phenomena that can be expressed, from form up to the omniscience of the Buddha, are ultimately devoid of the extremes of arising and of truly passing away or ceasing. By abandoning these, one abides in the equality of non-arising and non-ceasing. This is the non-conceptual primordial wisdom of the Buddha’s mind, which never diminishes; thus, it is deathless and supreme, and there is nothing else beyond it. Because it brings about the attainment of the supreme, ultimate, and unsurpassable nature, this path of realizing that all phenomena are without apprehension is called the perfection of wisdom; it is the supreme and unsurpassable of all paths. ^1-59

###### **དེ་དག་གི་སྐབས་བསྡུ་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-60]]

Summarizing these points: Second, the summary of the sections. This is the single verse beginning with "Whatever bodhisattva..." A bodhisattva who, through conviction in their own stage, practices just as it has been taught without doubt or hesitation, is said to possess supreme perfection of wisdom; this should be understood as abiding in the equality of all phenomena. In this way, to have fully understood that all phenomena of the basis, path, and result are of one taste in the equality of being without intrinsic nature—this is what it means to practice the supreme perfection of wisdom. The gate of the medium-length teaching. ^1-60

###### **འབྲིང་དུ་བསྟན་པའི་སྒོ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-61]]

Second, the presentation of the points of training through the gate of the medium-length division, which consists of four parts: identifying the perfection of wisdom as the subject that trains in this way; the accumulation of merit based upon that; and the manner of generating its nature within one's mental continuum. ^1-61

###### **ཇི་ལྟར་བསླབ་པའི་གནས་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-62]]

The presentation of the points of training: the first part consists of two sections, the manner of non-abiding and the explanation of the manner of training in that. ^1-62

###### **མི་གནས་པའི་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-63]]

The manner of non-abiding: the first part consists of two sections, the explanation of abiding in non-abiding and its benefits. ^1-63

###### **མི་གནས་པའི་གནས་པ་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-64]]

The explanation of abiding in non-abiding: the first part consists of two sections, the meaning and the analogy. ^1-64

###### **དོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-65]]

The first part consists of two sections: not abiding in the basis, such as form, and not abiding in the attributes, such as impermanence. ^1-65

###### **ཁྱད་གཞི་གཟུགས་ལ་སོགས་པ་ལ་མི་གནས་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-1-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-66]]

The first part consists of one verse, "Whoever does not abide in form," and so forth. Whoever does not abide by way of grasping at form, and likewise does not abide in feeling, and whoever does not abide in perception, and does not abide in volitional formations, and does not abide in consciousness—that is to abide in the non-graspability of their reality (dharmatā). Such a practice is to engage in the supreme perfection of wisdom. ^1-66

###### **ཁྱད་ཆོས་མི་རྟག་སོགས་སུ་མི་གནས་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-1-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-67]]

The second part consists of one verse, "Permanent and impermanent," and so forth. Furthermore, one does not abide in any of the two extremes or the objects grasped by conceptual thought, such as grasping at phenomena like form as permanent, impermanent, pleasant, painful, agreeable, disagreeable, having a self, or having no self, nor grasping at them as such (tathatā) or as emptiness. One does not abide in the perception of grasping at the attainment of the three fruits, such as the stream-enterer, nor in the stage of an arhat. One does not abide in the stage of a pratyekabuddha, nor likewise in the stage of a buddha. In short, one does not abide through intense attachment to any phenomena of the basis, the path, or the result. ^1-67

###### **དཔེ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-68]]

The second part consists of one verse, "Just as the Guide," and so forth. The example of not abiding in any phenomenon is as follows: the mind of the Buddha, the Bhagavan, does not abide merely in the unconditioned element, the peaceful realm of nirvāṇa. Likewise, it does not abide in the conditioned side of the three realms of cyclic existence. That is to engage without abiding in any extreme of existence or peace. In this way, the bodhisattva, without abiding through attachment to any object of knowledge, abides in a state where there is no abiding in anything that can be designated as an abode. The King of Dharma, the Victorious One, has declared this manner of practice to be the supreme of all modes of abiding. ^1-68

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-69]]

Of that ^1-69

###### **ཕན་ཡོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-70]]

The second part is the benefits. This consists of one verse, "Those who," and so forth. In this way, one who abides with mental attachment to all phenomena of the ground and path, and who possesses an object of observation, will not attain enlightenment. However, by relying on the absence of an object of observation, the three types of enlightenment are attained. This refers to those who think, "I shall become a Hearer of the Well-Gone One," or those who desire to become a Solitary Realizer, or likewise, a King of Dharma, a Buddha. In short, regardless of which of the three enlightenments one desires, it is impossible to attain any of those fruits without relying on this path of patience regarding the meaning of non-abiding or the absence of an object of observation. It is like not seeing the near shore or the far shore of the ocean if one does not rely on a boat. Thus, to the extent that one generates the patience of realizing the lack of intrinsic nature of the self of persons and the self of phenomena, to that extent, the corresponding three types of enlightenment arise. Through the distinction of emptiness, by realizing the selflessness of persons, one attains the enlightenment of a Hearer; by realizing the selflessness of persons and the selflessness of phenomena as a pair, one attains the enlightenment of a Solitary Realizer; and by realizing the ultimate equality where both types of selflessness are perfected, one attains the enlightenment of a Buddha. Thus, all of these arise from the power of the perfection of wisdom. Therefore, it is declared that the path of the three vehicles arises extensively from this perfection of wisdom. Furthermore, other sutras state that if one is arrogant, thinking, "I have attained the fruit of a Stream-Enterer," that very thought becomes a grasping at a self. Also, because Hearers lack skillful means and are of lesser intellect regarding the nature of emptiness—the three doors of liberation—they do not realize it as it is for a time, and although they manifest a lesser nirvāṇa, they eventually realize the equality as it is and attain buddhahood; thus, it is taught that there is ultimately only one vehicle. Just as the perfection of wisdom sutras teach the distinctions of the three—the knowledge of the ground, the knowledge of the path, and the omniscience—this is also clearly explained in the treatises of the Regent (Maitreya) and the bodhisattvas of the ten stages. In general, one who has an object of observation cannot be liberated from cyclic existence, and it is important to know that even when practicing the perfection of wisdom that is without an object of observation, there is a difference of proximity or distance to the Mother (the perfection of wisdom) as the fruit, depending on whether one’s realization is perfected or not; this is mentioned as a related topic. ^1-70

###### **དེ་ལ་སློབ་པའི་ཚུལ་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-71]]

Explaining the manner of training in that. The second part, regarding the manner of training in that, consists of three aspects: the basis of training, the person who trains, and the manner in which to train. ^1-71

###### **བསླབ་པར་བྱ་བའི་གནས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-72]]

The first, "teaching the Dharma" and so forth, is a single verse. In terms of conventional reality, the teacher "teaching the Dharma," the student "listening," the "Dharma" that is to be taught, and the attainment of the "four fruits" of a śrāvaka through hearing the Dharma and practicing diligently, as well as, in the same way, the "pratyekabuddha" attaining the fruit of a solitary realizer, and likewise, the "world protector" manifesting the "enlightenment" of a buddha—all of these, including the "ultimate nirvāṇa" attained by those with clear intellect who are skilled in the methods of the "transcendental path," are taught by the "Tathāgata" as being like a "magical illusion," in that the appearance of dependent origination does not withstand logical analysis. As it is said in the extensive sūtra: Subhūti said, "O sons of the gods, you should seek the hearing of the Dharma from me as being like a magical illusion and a phantom," and so on, up to his statement, "If there were any other dharma superior to nirvāṇa, I would view even that as being like a magical illusion." ^1-72

###### **སློབ་པ་པོ་གང་ཟག** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-73]]

The second part, "the four types of persons" and so forth, is a single verse. Who is it that respects the meaning as explained? These "four types of persons" are those who are "not afraid" of that profound meaning just explained. Which four? They are the "sons of the Victorious One" who are "skilled" in the profound meaning of the truth—that is, those who have seen the truth or have trained in wisdom—the "great bodhisattvas" who are "irreversible," and the "noble arhats." These three have cleared away and exhausted the "stains of the mind," and by abandoning "doubt" regarding the profound meaning, their "intentions are completely fulfilled." The "fourth" is one who, even if they are a "beginner," is "completely seized" by a "spiritual friend" who is capable of correctly teaching the perfection of wisdom in the present, because they have already performed deeds for the Victorious Ones of the past. ^1-73

###### **ཇི་ལྟར་བསླབ་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-74]]

Third, regarding the way of training, there are two: training in the way of not training in inferior results, and training in the way of not training in all phenomena. ^1-74

###### **འབྲས་བུ་དམན་པ་ལ་མི་སློབ་པའི་ཚུལ་ལ་སློབ་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-2-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-75]]

The first point is taught by a single verse, "The bodhisattva who is skilled in knowledge," and so forth. A bodhisattva who is skilled in the stages of the path of the three vehicles, and who knows the system of the single vehicle—the ultimate, indivisible, and equal truth—acts in such a way that all phenomena are without extremes and of a single taste, and teaches the nature of reality as it is designated without contradiction. Such a person does not train in the stages that lead to attaining the state of a śrāvaka arhat, nor do they train in the stages of a pratyekabuddha. Conventionally, however, if one asks in what they do train, it is that they train in the wake of the dharma for which the buddhas have departed and appeared, solely for the sake of omniscience. Nevertheless, in the ultimate sense, since neither the so-called "training" nor its opposite, "not training," can be observed, that which does not train in any phenomenon whatsoever is what is called training in the true path. ^1-75

###### **ཆོས་ཐམས་ཅད་ལ་མི་སློབ་པའི་ཚུལ་ལ་སློབ་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-2-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-76]]

The second point is taught by a single verse, "Form increasing or completely," and so forth. It is not that one trains for the sake of grasping, increasing, or completely diminishing—that is, declining—all phenomena included within the impure and the pure, such as form. Since all these phenomena are from the beginning unobservable and thus pure by nature, in the ultimate sense there is never any increasing, declining, arising, or ceasing, nor any grasping of them. Having seen this nature of reality—the primordial meaning—and abiding in the equality of non-action, the bodhisattva does not train by way of attachment to grasping or accepting any of the various phenomena; rather, they train in non-observation. By way of intention, they hold onto omniscience itself in a way that is indivisible. By the power of the path of profound method and wisdom, they certainly emerge into that ultimate state of attainment they desire. This, which possesses these three qualities, is the way of training for bodhisattvas who delight in the virtues of the supreme vehicle.
Identifying the perfection of wisdom as the object of such training. ^1-76

###### **དེ་ལྟར་བསླབ་པའི་ཡུལ་ན་ཤེས་རབ་ཕར་ཕྱིན་ངོས་བཟུང་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-77]]

The second point, identifying the perfection of wisdom as the object of such training, is taught through four aspects: showing it to be free from oneness and difference, showing it to be infinite, showing it to be inconceivable, and showing it to be pure from the beginning. ^1-77

###### **གཅིག་དང་ཐ་དད་བྲལ་བར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-78]]

Showing it to be free from oneness and difference. The first point, "Form is not the perfection of wisdom," and so forth, is one verse. If one asks what this so-called perfection of wisdom is like: form is not the perfection of wisdom, because those two are not one. Nor does the perfection of wisdom exist as something different from form, because it is not observed as separate. Likewise, consciousness, perception, feeling, and the compositional factors are not the perfection of wisdom, and the perfection of wisdom does not exist as something different from these either. The perfection of wisdom, which is the basis—the nature of phenomena—cannot be expressed as being either one with or different from form and the rest. Therefore, this perfection of wisdom, the sphere of reality, is not one with the phenomena of form and so forth, nor is it different from them; yet, because all phenomena are encompassed within the nature of that single reality, it is, for example, like the expanse of space. It is distinguished solely by the sphere of reality, and there is not even an atom of a dualistic phenomenon that is different from it. Furthermore, if the nature of phenomena or the sphere of reality were to exist as something different from the phenomena themselves, it would imply that there are phenomena that are not its nature and that have not become the essence of the sphere of reality, which is impossible. If these phenomena, such as form, were one with the sphere of reality, then seeing them would be seeing the truth, and there would be the fault of being unable to establish the conventional and the ultimate as different, and so on. As it is said, "The perfection of wisdom should be sought in the chapter of Subhūti," and in that regard, "Kausika, the perfection of wisdom should not be sought in form, nor should it be sought apart from form, because those phenomena have a single characteristic, which is the characteristic of having no characteristics." This is as explained in that teaching. ^1-78

###### **མཐའ་ཡས་པར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-79]]

[Demonstrating the Boundless] The second point is a single verse beginning with "the objects of focus." The nature of the objects of focus or what is to be grasped—such as forms—is boundless because it does not abide as a delimited entity, saying "this is this." The nature of the sentient beings who conceptualize or grasp them is also boundless in its essence as the expanse of phenomena (dharmadhātu). The nature of the sky, which serves as an analogy to illustrate the nature of phenomena (dharmatā), is likewise boundless. The wisdom of the world-knower that realizes all those objects is also boundless to that same extent. As the sutra states, "The perfection of wisdom is great, immeasurable, and boundless," and so forth. Because all phenomena have become the essence of the expanse of phenomena, the expanse of phenomena is all-encompassing and its limits are not observed. Furthermore, because the phenomena that are contained within its state also lack limits of object and limits of time, one should understand them to be of such a nature of great vastness, the nature of phenomena (dharmatā) of equal taste. [Demonstrating the Inconceivable] ^1-79

###### **བསམ་གྱིས་མི་ཁྱབ་པར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-80]]

[Demonstrating the Inconceivable] The third point is a single verse beginning with "perception is the near shore." The Sugata has proclaimed that as long as there is the perception of grasping at signs—such as existence and non-existence—one does not transcend the world, and thus it is the "near shore." He stated that as long as perception arises, to that extent there is cyclic existence. Therefore, by means of the wisdom of discernment, one completely destroys all that is perceived and all that is the object of perception, and abandoning all perceptions that grasp at signs is to go to the "far shore" of all worldly beings. In this way, those who have attained this suchness of phenomena, which is beyond the intellect and free from all perceptions of objects, have gone to the "far shore" of the path and abide in the intent of the definitive-meaning teachings of the Teacher. Although all phenomena are, by their own essence, primordially unborn and abide in a nature free from obscuration, they are merely obscured by conceptualization through falsely imputing them as self and various phenomena. One should understand that the perfection of wisdom, which is the ultimate, is to rest in the expanse of phenomena—the reality limit—by stopping all perceptions through non-conceptual primordial wisdom that accords with the nature of phenomena, thereby maintaining the natural state without any removal or establishment. ^1-80

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-81]]

The teaching of the inconceivable. ^1-81

###### **གཟོད་ནས་དག་པར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-4-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-82]]

Regarding the fourth: the actual primordial purity of all phenomena, as exemplified by sentient beings, and the benefits of realizing it in that way—these two. ^1-82

###### **སེམས་ཅན་གྱིས་མཚོན་པའི་ཆོས་ཀུན་གཟོད་ནས་དག་པ་དངོས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-4-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-83]]

First, the single verse beginning with "If the Teacher..." [is explained]. Even if the Teacher, the Sugata who has become a valid authority, were to proclaim clearly with the sound of his voice for as many eons as there are grains of sand in the Ganges River that "sentient beings exist," how could sentient beings exist or be born, given that they abide in a nature that is primordially unborn and pure in its own essence? Since the suchness of phenomena is immutable, no one is capable of causing birth or the like; therefore, if the Teacher were to teach that they exist, yet they do not function as existing, what need is there to mention others? Seeing the meaning of being primordially unborn in this way is to engage in the supreme perfection of wisdom. By means of these four verses that identify the perfection of wisdom itself, one should determine the yoga of the profound perfection of wisdom, which is the true wisdom that transcends the scope of consciousness and holds the mode of non-dual primordial wisdom. ^1-83

###### **དེ་ལྟར་རྟོགས་པའི་ཕན་ཡོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-4-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-84]]

Second, the single verse beginning with "At the time when I..." [is explained]. The Victorious Śākyamuni himself spoke thus: "At the time when I, the King of the Śākyas, was speaking in complete accordance with this supreme perfection of wisdom, having attained the noble patient acceptance of the non-arising of phenomena on the eighth stage, the supreme being of the past prophesied to me, 'In a future time, you shall become a Buddha.'" In the past, when our Teacher was the young Brahmin youth named Sumati, he scattered five utpala flowers to the Buddha Dīpaṃkara and spread his matted hair, which was the color of gold, as a seat. At that time, the Buddha said, "Brahmin youth, in a future time, after countless eons, you shall become a Tathāgata named Śākyamuni in this very world system," and thus gave the prophecy. At that moment, the Brahmin youth attained the patient acceptance of the non-arising of phenomena, and because he was not separated from the immeasurable qualities of a Buddha through the mode of non-apprehension, he received the prophecy; it is the nature of reality that Buddhas give prophecies to those who have attained the patient acceptance of the non-arising of phenomena. It is said that prior to that, although he had served countless Buddhas for immeasurable eons and had generated the mind of omniscience, he did not receive a prophecy because he had not previously attained such a patient acceptance that abides in the perfection of wisdom in every respect. The accumulation of merit based upon that. ^1-84

###### **དེ་ལ་བརྟེན་པའི་བསོད་ནམས་མངོན་པར་འདུ་བྱ་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-85]]

Third, regarding the accumulation of merit that depends upon that, there are two: the benefits of personally upholding it and so forth, and the benefits of explaining it to others and so forth. ^1-85

###### **རང་གིས་འཆང་བ་སོགས་ཀྱི་ཕན་ཡོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-86]]

The benefits of personally upholding it and so forth. Regarding the first, there are two: being unharmed by obstacles and the accumulation of merit. ^1-86

###### **གནོད་པས་མི་ཚུགས་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-87]]

Being unharmed by obstacles. The first point concerns one verse of the Perfection of Wisdom and so forth: "This Perfection of Wisdom, which the protector, the perfect Buddha, along with his spiritual heirs, practices through meditative equipoise—any son or daughter of noble family who respectfully holds this text in mind and understands its meaning will not be harmed by physical suffering, poison, weapons, fire, or water." Furthermore, they will not be harmed by the poison of intense attachment, the weapon of hatred, fire-like desire, or water-like ignorance. The sinful Māra, who is an obstacle to the path, and the dark retinue of Māra’s faction will not find an opening, because they are protected by the power of the nature of reality, the Buddhas, the Bodhisattvas, and the deities of the white faction. Accumulation of merit. ^1-87

###### **བསོད་ནམས་སྡུད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-88]]

Regarding the second, there are two: the benefits of offering and the benefits of training. ^1-88

###### **མཆོད་པའི་ཕན་ཡོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-89]]

The benefits of offering. The first point concerns three verses of "The Sugata and so forth": regarding the construction of a stūpa containing the relics of a Sugata who has passed into parinirvāṇa, the merit is great due to the excellence of the object of connection. Furthermore, the excellence of the causes of that stūpa—made of the seven precious substances such as vaiḍūrya, red pearls, musāragalva, aśmagarbha, crystal, gold, and silver—and the act of making offerings to it are significant. Regarding the excellence of the vast number, if one were to create such stūpas of the Sugata equal in number to the grains of sand in the river Gaṅgā, and fill the Buddha-fields of a thousand million in number, and—to take an example of what is to be fully examined—if all the beings residing in those countless millions of fields were to do nothing else but make offerings with the excellence of the substances of offering, such as divine flowers, superior incense, and unguents, and if they were to make offerings in that manner without interruption in the three times, day and night, for an aeon or even longer than that aeon, the merit would be immeasurable. However, if someone were to write down this text—which teaches the non-dual perfection of wisdom of the Sugatas, along with skillful means, from which the incomparable qualities such as the ten powers of the Guide and Victorious One arise—into a volume, hold it in their hand, and for a short time make even a slight gesture of respect with flowers and unguents, the merit of that is such that the former merit of building and making offerings to such stūpas cannot even approach it in terms of number, fraction, analogy, or cause. ^1-89

###### **བསླབ་པའི་ཕན་ཡོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-90]]

The benefits of training. Second, there are three parts: how all happiness of cyclic existence and peace arises from training in the Mother; how one attains the merit of making offerings to the relics and so forth by training in the Mother; and how other roots of virtue are transformed into the perfection of wisdom by training in the Mother. ^1-90

###### **ཡུམ་ལ་བསླབ་པས་སྲིད་ཞིའི་བདེ་བ་ཀུན་འབྱུང་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-91]]

How all happiness of cyclic existence and peace arises from training in the Mother. The first has two parts: stating the meaning and providing an example of it. ^1-91

###### **དོན་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-92]]

Stating the meaning. The first has two parts: how the happiness of peace arises, and how the happiness of both cyclic existence and peace arises. ^1-92

###### **ཞི་བའི་བདེ་བ་འབྱུང་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-93]]

How the happiness of peace arises. The first has two parts: how the happiness of mere peace arises, and how the state of great peace, the source of the buddhas of the three times, arises. ^1-93

###### **ཞི་བ་ཙམ་གྱི་བདེ་བ་འབྱུང་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-1-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-94]]

How the happiness of mere peace arises. The first, the two lines beginning with "The perfection of wisdom of the Victorious One," means that this perfection of wisdom of the Victorious One is a great mantra or an unsurpassed mantra. Regarding this, the term "mantra" (vidyā) derives from the word *mantra*, meaning that which protects easily through awareness and knowledge; among all such methods of mantra, this one is great because it permanently pacifies all that is included within the mental sorrow and physical suffering of the immeasurable and manifold elements of sentient beings. It has become the unsurpassed king. ^1-94

###### **ཞི་བ་ཆེན་པོའི་གོ་འཕང་དུས་གསུམ་གྱི་སངས་རྒྱས་འབྱུང་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-1-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-95]]

The second, the two lines beginning with "Those who benefit," means that even those bodhisattvas who engage in the conduct of Samantabhadra, which consists of benefiting all sentient beings through their application and possessing a mind of compassion, will, after training in this great mantra, the perfection of wisdom, attain the supreme, unsurpassed awakening in the future and become fully enlightened. How the happiness of both cyclic existence and peace arises. ^1-95

###### **སྲིད་ཞི་གཉིས་ཀའི་བདེ་བ་འབྱུང་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-96]]

The second, the two lines beginning with "The happiness of the conditioned," refers to the happiness of conditioned cyclic existence and the happiness of unconditioned liberation. One should understand that all such happiness of cyclic existence and nirvana arises from this perfection of wisdom, which is like the ground or essence of all phenomena. Providing an analogy for that. ^1-96

###### **དཔེ་བརྗོད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-97]]

The second, regarding the analogies, consists of two parts: how all qualities are produced, using the analogy of the earth, and how all qualities follow after this, using the analogy of a universal monarch. ^1-97

###### **ས་ཡི་དཔེས་ཡོན་ཏན་ཀུན་སྐྱེད་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-98]]

The first, the single verse beginning with "Having planted the seed," explains the analogy for how the perfection of wisdom is established as the ground for all qualities of the path and the fruit. Just as "having planted the seed, it abides in the earth, and sprouts and so forth arise," and those things, having found the gathering of their respective causes and conditions, are born in many various forms, in the same way, all the qualities of the five perfections of the path and the fruit of awakening arise from the perfection of wisdom. If one is seized by the wisdom that does not conceptualize the three spheres, such as generosity, it becomes a genuine path, and it is established as having a connection such that the path and its ultimate fruit cannot arise without it. Furthermore, from the wisdom that realizes suchness, all-knowledge is attained, and because that knowledge teaches all phenomena of cause and effect without error, all roots of virtue arise from it, either directly or indirectly. ^1-98

###### **འཁོར་སྒྱུར་གྱི་དཔེས་ཡོན་ཏན་ཀུན་འདི་ཡི་རྗེས་སུ་འགྲོ་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-99]]

The second, the single verse beginning with "The universal monarch," explains that just as the universal monarch always travels along the path, and the seven precious royal treasures and the fourfold gathering of the army all travel along that very same path, in the same way, wherever the perfection of wisdom of the Victorious Ones exists or proceeds within the continuum of a person or the state of the path, all the roots of virtue—which are the causes characterized by the purity of the three spheres—and the phenomena of the fruit, namely all the qualities of the path and the fruit, will also proceed. By realizing this, one gathers and increases all white phenomena, and they reach their ultimate fruition. ^1-99

###### **ཡུམ་ལ་བསླབ་པས་སྐུ་གདུང་སོགས་མཆོད་པ་བརྙེས་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-100]]

How one attains the offering of the physical remains and so forth through training in the Mother. The second section, on how one attains the offering of the physical remains through training in the Mother, has two parts: the actual explanation and the explanation of its reason, accompanied by an analogy. ^1-100

###### **དངོས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-101]]

The first, the single verse beginning with "The Victorious One asked Śakra," explains the Victorious One’s inquiry to Śakra and his subsequent reply: If all the Buddha-fields, equal in number to the sands of the Ganges, were to be filled completely—without any gaps and piled up to the very peaks—with the physical remains of the Victorious Ones, and if one were to compare that with the Perfection of Wisdom written in a volume, asking which one would be chosen, [the reply is]: "Śakra, I would choose this very Perfection of Wisdom written in a volume." ^1-101

###### **དེའི་རྒྱུ་མཚན་དཔེ་དང་བཅས་ཏེ་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-102]]

The second section, the three verses beginning with "For what reason," explains that since one attains inexhaustible merit by making such offerings, why would one set aside so many physical remains and instead choose this Perfection of Wisdom? It is not that I lack respect for the physical remains; I certainly do have respect for them. However, the reason for choosing the Perfection of Wisdom is that because it has been fully cultivated by such wisdom, those physical remains themselves become worthy of being offered to. For example, just as people who rely on a king receive honor from others, the physical remains of the Buddha, which possess great qualities, become such because they rely on the Perfection of Wisdom. If one asks, "If that is so, then since the Buddha has passed into parinirvāṇa and is now devoid of present activity, how is it appropriate to offer to the physical remains?" [the answer is]: Just as a precious jewel, which possesses all the qualities of granting one's wishes and is of immeasurable value, is placed in a container such as a casket or a box, that container is worthy of being honored in the world. Even when the jewel is removed from that container, one still rejoices in the casket that was previously used as a container, thinking, "This is the container that held the precious jewel, so it is unlike others." The fact that one obtains such qualities of that container is due to that precious jewel itself. Likewise, through the qualities of that precious jewel, the Perfection of Wisdom, even though the Victorious One has passed into parinirvāṇa, the physical remains—which are like its container—also attain the status of being worthy of offerings. Because of this reason, any person who delights in the immeasurable qualities of the Victorious One and wishes to uphold them should take up this Perfection of Wisdom, for it is what brings liberation from all the bonds and obscurations of signs, and it is the essence of unsurpassed liberation. ^1-102

###### **ཡུམ་ལ་བསླབ་པས་དགེ་རྩ་གཞན་འདས་པའི་ཕར་ཕྱིན་དུ་འགྱུར་ཚུལ** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-103]]

How training in the Mother causes other roots of virtue to become the perfection of wisdom. Third, regarding the transformation of other virtues into the path, there are two parts: making generosity and the rest into a single method, and demonstrating the analogy for that. ^1-103

###### **སྦྱིན་སོགས་ཚུལ་གཅིག་ཏུ་བྱེད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-104]]

The first part consists of one verse on "generosity and the rest." The "generosity that precedes generosity" is the perfection of wisdom, for it must be grasped by the wisdom that does not observe the three spheres; the same applies to discipline, patience, diligence, and meditative concentration. Why is this? Because all such virtuous phenomena as generosity and the rest, when tainted by conceptual observation, are not established as causes that transcend the cycle of existence and peace; therefore, they are held as the very state of the perfectly pure perfection of wisdom. Thus, this wisdom demonstrates the very method of equalizing all phenomena, such as generosity and the rest, into the nature of non-observation. ^1-104

###### **དེའི་དཔེ་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-105]]

The second part consists of two verses on "For example, in Jambudvīpa..." and so forth. If one wishes to illustrate the method by which all other virtues, such as generosity, are of one taste in the nature of the perfection of wisdom, consider this: in Jambudvīpa, there are many thousands of millions of distinct trees, such as sandalwood and agarwood, which possess a variety of forms, such as diverse colors, scents, branches, trunks, and fruits. Yet, regarding their shadows, apart from the mere designation of them as the "shadows of the trees," the shadows themselves do not possess the diverse colors, scents, and so forth, as the trees do; nor do the shadows, from their own side, exist as the distinct particulars of the individual trees. ^1-105

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-106]]

In the same way, these five perfections of the Victorious One, when embraced by the wisdom that realizes that very nature, all obtain the name "perfection of wisdom." The reason they are counted as transcendent perfections is due to the power of wisdom, and all those paths become of one taste in the emptiness endowed with the supreme of all aspects. Not only during the stage of the path, but when all virtues are dedicated for the sake of omniscience, just like water that has reached the ocean, all those virtues become of one taste as the result, called "perfected enlightenment," and the six perfections become spontaneously established as the result—the primordial wisdom of the emptiness endowed with the supreme of all aspects—in the form of the Truth Body. ^1-106

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-107]]

*The benefits of teaching others and so forth. ^1-107

###### **གཞན་པ་འཆད་པའི་ཕན་ཡོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-108]]

Second, regarding the benefits of teaching others, there are two parts: the manner in which one teaches, and the benefits of having taught in that way. ^1-108

###### **ཇི་ལྟར་འཆད་པའི་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-109]]

*The manner in which one teaches. Regarding the first, there are two parts: the manner of teaching the artificial perfection of wisdom, which is to be abandoned, and the manner of teaching the authentic perfection of wisdom, which is to be adopted. ^1-109

###### **སྤང་བྱ་ཤེར་ཕྱིན་བཅོས་མ་འཆད་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-110]]

The first is the single verse beginning with "If a bodhisattva..." Any bodhisattva who, without truly understanding the profound meaning of the Mother as explained—which is the manner of being free from conceptual proliferation—fully teaches these five aggregates of form, perception, feeling, mental formations, and consciousness merely as impermanent, and by that token as suffering, as merely the selflessness of the person, and as merely a temporary emptiness, while declaring, "This is the supreme perfection of wisdom," is engaging in a mere image of the perfection of wisdom, an artificial perfection of wisdom, or a mere semblance. By teaching in a way that does not accord with the nature of reality, such a person causes the intent of the supreme vehicle to be misunderstood.
