Conversely, those learned ones who have realized the meaning of the Mother as it is, always teach the phenomena established by the profound wisdom of the Victorious One in a way that is free from error, being endowed with both meaning and words; thus, they do not cause the profound teaching of the Tathāgatas to decline or perish. ^1-110

###### **བླང་བྱ་ཤེར་ཕྱིན་ཡང་དག་འཆད་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-111]]

The second is the verse beginning with "In which there is no form..." This refers to one who teaches the Dharma without any fixation on form, and likewise does not perceive feeling, does not perceive perception, does not perceive mental formations, and does not perceive consciousness. One who thoroughly understands and teaches others that all phenomena of cyclic existence and nirvana—the essence of form and the rest, and their characteristics such as impermanence—are from the very beginning unborn, empty, and transcend the entire scope of conceptual activity, is truly engaging in the supreme perfection of wisdom. ^1-111

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-112]]

The benefits of what has been explained in this way. ^1-112

###### **ཤེར་ཕྱིན་ཡང་དག་པ་བཤད་པའི་ཕན་ཡོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-113]]

The second part, concerning the benefits of correctly explaining the perfection of wisdom, has two sections: the actual benefits and the reasons for them. ^1-113

###### **དངོས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-114]]

The first is the single verse beginning with "If someone, for as many eons as there are grains of sand in the Ganges..." This refers to a person who establishes or tames all the sentient beings residing in as many world systems as there are grains of sand in the Ganges, bringing them to the ultimate fruit of the śrāvaka or pratyekabuddha, which is the state of an arhat. If a person writes down this perfection of wisdom, understands its meaning—calling it the "supreme sentient being"—and gives that volume to a bodhisattva who is capable of explaining it to others, this merit is far more exalted than the former. ^1-114

###### **རྒྱུ་མཚན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-115]]

The second part, concerning the reasons, has two sections: the actual reasons and the illustration of them through analogies. ^1-115

###### **རྒྱུ་མཚན་དངོས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-116]]

The first is the single verse beginning with "Why is that?" This asks why that merit is so exalted. It is because those supreme speakers, the bodhisattvas, having trained in this path, teach all phenomena as emptiness within this Great Vehicle. Having heard this method of emptiness, those of lesser intellect understand it as, for example, the foam of a bubble, and thus those of the śrāvaka lineage quickly attain liberation from cyclic existence; those of medium faculties see the meaning of dependent origination and attain the enlightenment of a pratyekabuddha; and those of sharp faculties realize the equality of all phenomena and attain the enlightenment of a Buddha. ^1-116

###### **དཔེས་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-117]]

The second part has two sections: a brief presentation using the analogy of a sprout, and a detailed explanation using the analogies of the sun and the nāga. ^1-117

###### **མྱུ་གུའི་དཔེས་མདོར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-2-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-118]]

The first is the single verse beginning with "Without a sprout," and so forth. Just as it is entirely impossible for a tree to appear in the world without a sprout, and if there is no tree, how could branches, leaves, flowers, and fruits appear—they would not appear—in the same way, if the ultimate and conventional bodhicitta, which are like the sprout, do not exist as taught in the Mother of the Victors, then the Victor, who is like the tree, cannot possibly appear in the world. And if that does not exist, then how could the fruits of the teachings given by that Buddha—the branches and leaves, and so forth—such as Śakra, Brahmā, and the śrāvakas, appear, since there would be no cause? ^1-118

###### **ཉི་མ་དང་ཀླུ་ཡི་དཔེས་རྒྱས་པར་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-119]]

The second part has two sections: the example of the sun illustrating the direct correspondence, and the example of the nāga-king illustrating the inverse. ^1-119

###### **རྗེས་འགྲོའི་དཔེ་ཉི་མས་མཚོན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-120]]

The first is the single verse beginning with "The solar disk," and so forth. Just as when the solar disk manifests or radiates its web of light in the ten directions, sentient beings exert themselves in various activities of karma, in the same way, when the aspiration and engagement bodhicitta arises in the world for the sake of the wise attaining the wisdom of omniscience—the perfection of wisdom of the Victors—the unsurpassable wisdom of the Victors arises as the fruit from that. By that very wisdom, through seeing all knowable things without error, they emit the light rays of the Dharma that target the beings to be tamed, whereby sentient beings become endowed with the qualities of the Dharma, both temporary and ultimate. ^1-120

###### **ལྡོག་པའི་དཔེ་ཀླུ་བདག་གིས་མཚོན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-121]]

The second part has two sections, the two verses beginning with "For example, the Anavatapta." For example, if the lord of the nāgas were absent from the Anavatapta Lake, how could the rivers that arise from the power of that nāga flow in this Jambudvīpa? They would not flow, and if there were no rivers, there would be no moisture, and consequently, flowers and fruits would not arise. Furthermore, in the great oceans that arise from the meeting of the rivers, there would also be no variety of precious substances of different forms. Just as in that example, if in this world there were no bodhicitta—the union of emptiness and the essence of compassion, which is like the lord of the nāgas—then how could the wisdom of the Sugatas, which is like the flow of rivers that causes the seeds of enlightenment to flourish, arise anywhere in this world? It would not be appropriate for it to arise. If that wisdom, which is like a river, were absent, then the flowers of virtuous qualities—which serve as the causes for higher status and definite goodness, and which are cultivated and increased by the beings to be tamed through hearing and so forth the Dharma taught by that wisdom—would also be absent. If those were absent, their fruit, the three enlightenments, would also be absent. If those were absent, the immeasurable Buddha-qualities, such as the powers, which are like an ocean filled with precious gems, would also be absent. Therefore, if the precious bodhicitta—the union of emptiness and the essence of compassion, in which the two truths are inseparable—exists, then all the qualities of the path and the fruit follow in its wake; and because all other things are reversed if it is absent, this alone is the root of all white virtues. ^1-121

###### **དེའི་རང་བཞིན་རྒྱུད་ལ་བསྐྱེད་པའི་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-122]]

The manner of generating its nature within the mental continuum. Fourth, regarding the generation of the nature of that perfection of wisdom within the mental continuum, there are two: what is to be done during the post-meditative state and what is to be cultivated during meditative equipoise. ^1-122

###### **རྗེས་ཐོབ་ཏུ་བྱ་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-123]]

What is to be done during the post-meditative state. Regarding the first, there are three: rejoicing, which is the cause for increasing merit; dedication, which is the cause for ensuring it does not go to waste; and aspiration, which is the cause for it to increase ever further. Rejoicing, which is the cause for increasing merit. ^1-123

###### **བསོད་ནམས་སྤེལ་བའི་རྒྱུ་ཡི་རང་བཞིན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-124]]

Regarding the first, there are two: the explanation of the benefits of the bodhisattva’s rejoicing, and the explanation of the common basis for both rejoicing and dedication. ^1-124

###### **བྱང་སེམས་ཀྱི་ཡི་རང་གི་ཕན་ཡོན་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-125]]

Regarding the first, there are two: the example and the meaning. ^1-125

###### **དཔེ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-126]]

Regarding the first, there is one verse beginning with "In this world..." In this world-realm, all the various living beings—such as the stars and fire-torches that provide light, and fireflies and those holding lamps—produce the light of their own bodies and substances in one place in order to illuminate the directions. The light rays of the sun’s disk appearing as one is supreme; all other collections of illuminating lights cannot even begin to compete with the manner of its brilliance and pervasiveness. ^1-126

###### **དོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-127]]

Regarding the second, there is one verse beginning with "The assembly of Śrāvakas..." Just as in the example just explained, even if the assembly of Śrāvakas generates a collection of the three bases of merit—consisting of generosity, discipline, and meditation—it cannot even begin to compete with a single moment of a Bodhisattva’s mind of rejoicing. This is because, no matter how great the objective basis of the Śrāvakas’ roots of virtue may be, their mode of apprehension is narrow, they are not purified of the stains of conceptualization, and they are not supported by the dedication of skillful means, and therefore their fruit is small. ^1-127

###### **ཡི་རང་དང་བསྔོ་བའི་གཞི་ཐུན་མོང་དུ་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-128]]

Regarding the second, the basis for rejoicing and dedication, there are three verses beginning with "In the past time..." This indicates the countless Buddhas of the past who resided in infinite, thousand-fold millions of world-realms. Furthermore, all those protectors of the world who have passed beyond the sorrow of cyclic existence, who are currently living and abiding, and who teach the precious Dharma to exhaust suffering—from the time they first generated the mind set on supreme enlightenment within their own continuums, up until the time when the holy Dharma of those guides vanishes or is exhausted—all the merit of their intention and generation of mind, their application consisting of the six perfections, and the resultant Buddha-qualities such as the powers that they have attained, as well as all the virtuous, contaminated, and uncontaminated states of learning and non-learning of the Buddha’s sons, the Bodhisattvas, and the Śrāvakas and Pratyekabuddhas—all of these, the Bodhisattva gathers together into one with his own intellect, and following them, he rejoices with a sincere heart full of joy. Having generated the mind for unsurpassable enlightenment, and in order to attain that great, unsurpassable enlightenment as a cause for accomplishing the benefit of boundless beings, he gathers all these roots of virtue of self and others into one and dedicates them. The essence of dedication is the act of transforming those roots of virtue, like shooting an arrow, through a mind of aspiration that is focused on the correct basis of conventional dedication. 
Dedication as a cause that is not wasted. ^1-128

###### **ཆུད་མི་ཟ་བའི་རྒྱུ་བསྔོ་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-129]]

The second part, regarding dedication, has two sections: the dedication itself and the benefits of having dedicated in that way. ^1-129

###### **དངོས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-130]]

The first part has two sections: dedication in the manner of non-conceptualization of ultimate reality, and dedication in the manner of following the Buddhas in conventional reality. ^1-130

###### **དོན་དམ་མི་དམིགས་པའི་ཚུལ་གྱིས་བསྔོ་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-131]]

The first part has two sections: the individual explanation of negation and affirmation, and the presentation of their meaning summarized together with an analogy. ^1-131

###### **དགག་སྒྲུབ་སོ་སོར་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-2-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-132]]

The first part has two sections: the dedication of what is to be negated, which involves conceptualization, and the dedication of what is to be affirmed, which is without conceptualization. ^1-132

###### **དགག་བྱ་དམིགས་བཅས་ཀྱི་བསྔོ་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-2-1-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-133]]

The first is the single verse beginning with "If one dedicates..." Regarding that dedication, if there arises a perception of clinging to the dedicating mind as truly existent, and if there is also a perception of clinging to the place to which one dedicates—namely, enlightenment—as well as a perception of the sentient beings who are the object of the dedication, then, because one is bound by the perception of superimposition, one remains in a view that involves conceptualization. Since that mind possesses an apprehension that is attached to the three spheres of dedication through clinging to true existence, it is not understood as a dedication that is of the nature of being non-erroneous, and it is not included in the count of perfectly pure dedications. ^1-133

###### **བསྒྲུབ་བྱ་དམིགས་མེད་ཀྱི་བསྔོ་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-2-1-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-134]]

The second part is the single verse beginning with "If one dedicates in this way..." By reversing that manner of dedication involving conceptualization, one dedicates in this way: the virtue to be dedicated and the mind that dedicates—that is, the phenomena of who dedicates and what is dedicated—are, conventionally, momentary, and thus they cease and are exhausted by their very nature. Furthermore, the place to which one dedicates, namely enlightenment, also does not arise simultaneously with the former, and thus it is exhausted or non-existent. Ultimately, those three phenomena—the object of dedication, the act of dedication, and the dedicator—do not exist even as a moment or a mere particle, and because they have not arisen by their own essence, or have been ceased and exhausted from the very beginning, they have never existed. If one comes to know the manner in which, ultimately, a phenomenon never dedicates a phenomenon to a phenomenon, or that there is nothing to be dedicated, then one does not cling to the three spheres of dedication. Being possessed of such profound knowledge is what is called "perfectly dedicated." ^1-134

###### **དོན་བསྡུ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-2-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-135]]

The third part is the summary of the meaning of those two. It is a single verse beginning with "If one [clings to] signs..." In short, although one may dedicate with aspiration by taking as an object the three phenomena—such as what is dedicated and to whom it is dedicated—one must understand this in the manner of a conventional illusion. If one clings to those three phenomena as "signs" and performs a dedication, that is not a correct dedication. If one understands those three phenomena as having the nature of being "signless" and dedicates them, that is a correct dedication to enlightenment. Just as when one eats good food mixed with poison, even though the food is good, one is harmed by the poison, the Victorious One has taught that even if they are virtuous phenomena, if one takes them as an object through the perception of clinging to them as true, it is just like food mixed with poison. ^1-135

###### **ཀུན་རྫོབ་ཀྱི་བསྔོ་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-136]]

The second part is the conventional dedication. It is a single verse beginning with "Therefore, in this way..." Because one must know whether a dedication is perfectly pure or not, one should train in dedication by understanding the faults and qualities in this way. This refers to the manner in which the Victors have realized or known the unerring virtue of rejoicing and dedication—that is, what the nature or form of conventional dedication is, what kind of unerring result arises from it in terms of worldly convention, and what the characteristic of those things being non-conceptual in the ultimate sense is. In short, having realized the nature of the two truths as inseparable, one thinks, "Just as the Victors have performed rejoicing and dedication, I too shall follow them and perform such rejoicing and dedicate in that way." By thinking, "I dedicate just as the Victors dedicate," even if one is not skilled in the method of dedication oneself, by dedicating in the footsteps of those who are skilled, one’s dedication becomes in accordance with the correct dedication. This is as taught in the *Prayer of Good Conduct*, where two verses beginning with "The hero Mañjuśrī..." are spoken. ^1-136

###### **དེ་ལྟར་བསྔོས་པའི་ཕན་ཡོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-137]]

The second part, the benefits of having dedicated in that way, has two sections: the actual benefits and their detailed explanation through examples. ^1-137

###### **དངོས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-138]]

The first is the single verse beginning with "merit and so forth." As explained, if one dedicates all merit toward the great enlightenment in that way, the cause—the dedication—is free of poison, its result is that one does not abandon or rather attains the Buddha, and one speaks correctly in accordance with the Great Vehicle teachings spoken by the Victorious One. That hero, the bodhisattva, who dedicates in that way by means of non-conceptualization, surpasses all bodhisattvas in the world who possess conceptualization. ^1-138

###### **དཔེ་ཡི་སྒོ་ནས་རྒྱས་པར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-139]]

The second part has two sections: the manner of attaining the fruition of omniscience, and the manner of attaining the purity of the path of the perfection of wisdom. ^1-139

###### **འབྲས་བུ་རྣམ་མཁྱེན་ཐོབ་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-140]]

The first part has two sections: how the other perfections are led to omniscience by the example of the eye, and how, without the eye being painted, one does not attain omniscience even if the other perfections are complete. ^1-140

###### **དམིགས་བུའི་དཔེས་ཕར་ཕྱིན་གཞན་རྣམ་མཁྱེན་དུ་འཁྲིད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-2-2-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-141]]

The first is the single verse beginning with "the blind and the guide." Just as even if millions of blind people who lack a guide to lead them on the path were to gather together, they would neither see nor know the path lying before them, and thus it is impossible for them to enter that path and proceed to another city—for there is no such possibility—in the same way, if this perfection of wisdom, which is like an eye, is absent, then the five perfections, such as generosity, which are like the blind from their own side, are not grasped by the wisdom that acts as a guide to lead them in that direction. Because they lack that companion, they do not know the noble path, and therefore they are incapable of reaching the city of perfect enlightenment. ^1-141

###### **མིག་མ་བྲིས་པས་ཕར་ཕྱིན་གཞན་ཚང་ཡང་རྣམ་མཁྱེན་མི་ཐོབ་པའི་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-2-2-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-142]]

The second is the single verse beginning with "when wisdom." When those other virtuous qualities, such as generosity, are thoroughly grasped by the wisdom that does not conceptualize the three spheres, at that time they find an eye, and having obtained the name of this perfection of wisdom, one attains the ability to proceed easily to the city of omniscience. To illustrate this: if a painting of a human figure or the like is completely drawn in every other respect, but it lacks only the eye—if it is incomplete—then just as one does not receive the reward for the painting because it is not finished due to the eye not being painted, this is the manner in which the fruition is not attained if wisdom is absent, even if other virtues are complete. Thus, one should know that the essence of the Mahāyāna path is to possess non-apprehension in dedication, with a mind of omniscience that is directed toward the mother of fruition—which is not usurped by other paths—by means of an intellect that is certain to ripen solely into the primordial wisdom of emptiness endowed with all supreme aspects, based on the wisdom that knows the suchness of all phenomena, which transforms all paths into that emptiness endowed with all supreme aspects. ^1-142

###### **ལམ་ཕར་ཕྱིན་དག་པ་ཐོབ་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-143]]

The second part consists of two points: showing how one enters the ranks of the perfection of wisdom through non-apprehension by way of the analogy of space, and the manner in which the path is not completely purified if one is separated from that. ^1-143

###### **མི་དམིགས་པས་ཤེར་ཕྱིན་གྲལ་དུ་ཚུད་པ་ནམ་མཁའི་དཔེས་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-2-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-144]]

The first point consists of one verse, "When conditioned..." and so forth. When one analyzes with the wisdom that investigates the ultimate mode of existence all that is included within conditioned and unconditioned phenomena, as well as all white and black phenomena, and having destroyed the grasping at characteristics, one does not apprehend even an atom’s worth of a domain of signs or anything truly established among them. When one sees the meaning of there being no abiding anywhere, one enters the count of what is called the "perfection of wisdom" in the world. For example, just as space, because it lacks an intrinsic nature, does not abide in dependence upon anything else whatsoever, it is similar to that. ^1-144

###### **དེ་དང་བྲལ་ན་ལམ་ཡོངས་སུ་མི་དག་པའི་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-2-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-145]]

The second point consists of one verse, "If I..." and so forth. If, even though one is seized by compassion, one wanders into a state of grasping with the thought, "I shall practice this perfection of wisdom of the Victors," without having destroyed the focus of apprehension, and one possesses a mind of intense attachment thinking, "I shall liberate the countless multitudes of sentient beings who are touched by suffering," then, because one conceptualizes them as sentient beings included within the continuum of oneself and others, such a person is called a bodhisattva who has apprehension. This sort of practice is not the practice of the supreme perfection of wisdom. ^1-145

###### **གོང་ནས་གོང་དུ་འཕེལ་བའི་རྒྱུ་མོས་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-146]]

The cause for increasing from one level to the next is interest. Regarding the third, interest, there are three parts: the benefits of faith, the faults of lacking faith, and therefore the instruction to strive with faith in the Mother. ^1-146

###### **དད་པའི་ཕན་ཡོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-147]]

The first point consists of one verse, "A bodhisattva..." and so forth. Any bodhisattva who, during the time they practiced in previous lifetimes, listened to, grasped, and contemplated the meaning of this profound perfection of wisdom, will, upon being born in this world, become skilled in the meaning of the perfection of wisdom due to the power of those previous causes. Having become free from hesitation regarding it, as soon as they hear this text, that person generates the perception of the Teacher, thinking, "I have seen the Teacher." By the cause of that, they will quickly realize the great peaceful enlightenment. ^1-147

###### **མ་དད་པའི་ཉེས་དམིགས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-148]]

The second point consists of one verse, "During the time they practiced in previous lifetimes..." and so forth. Even if someone, during the time they practiced in previous lifetimes, honored and made offerings to countless millions of buddhas and thereby accumulated merit, if they do not have faith in the perfection of wisdom—the intended meaning of the Great Vehicle of the Victors—and do not make prayers to attain it, then, upon hearing this profound perfection of wisdom in the present, that person of small intellect will abandon this supreme, profound Dharma through the negative actions of the three doors and will not show it respect. By abandoning it, they also abandon all the omniscient qualities of the buddhas of the three times, and, having become devoid of the refuge of the noble Dharma, they will go to the Avīci hell and experience immeasurable faults, such as suffering through countless aeons of the world's formation and destruction. ^1-148

###### **དེས་ན་ཡུམ་ལ་དད་པས་བརྩོན་པར་གདམས་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-1-3-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-149]]

The third point consists of one verse, "Therefore..." and so forth. Because there are great benefits if one has faith, and great faults if one does not, "Therefore, if you desire to touch the supreme wisdom of the buddhas, you should have faith in this Mother of the Victors." This is the uncommon cause, without which that [wisdom] cannot arise. At this time, when we have met with the teachings of the Tathāgata, which are like an island of precious jewels, we must ensure that there is a result. For example, it would be improper for merchants to go to an island of jewels, only to have their previous trade goods exhausted and, without having gained any new profit in jewels, return empty-handed. Similarly, even if one has the jewel of the profound perfection of wisdom in hand, one should not act in such a way that, by failing to appreciate it and casting it aside, one exhausts one's previous merit through the cause of abandoning the Dharma. ^1-149

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-150]]

Cultivating in meditative equipoise. ^1-150

###### **མཉམ་བཞག་ཏུ་བསྒོམ་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-151]]

The second, the manner of cultivating in meditative equipoise, consists of four aspects: the essence of purified primordial wisdom, its function, its sphere of activity, and [the result]. ^1-151

###### **རྣམ་པར་དག་པའི་ཡེ་ཤེས་ཀྱི་ངོ་བོ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-152]]

The essence of purified primordial wisdom: The first, the essence, consists of one verse, "The purity of form..." and so forth. Meditating on the single taste of all things as the great, stainless, luminous expanse of reality—the essence of which is primordially pure, naturally beyond sorrow, perfectly awakened, naturally luminous, and primordially unborn and unceasing in its equality—is the essence of the primordial wisdom of meditative equipoise.
What is the manner of this? As indicated by the phrase "the purity of form," all phenomena, from the objects of knowledge such as form up to the omniscience of a buddha, are primordially unestablished as any nature of dualistic phenomena—such as subject and object, cyclic existence and nirvana, self and other, or existence and non-existence. Even though they abide as the great, stainless, naturally pure equality of the single sphere of reality, which is primordially free from birth and cessation, when the adventitious delusion of not realizing this arises, then to the mind stained by the superimposition of self and phenomena, the object appears as impure, and because the mind grasps it as such, it is called an impure sentient being, possessing the stains and obscurations of the side of cyclic existence.
In order to remove those adventitious stains, the path of the three vehicles is taught. By entering that path, one sees the natural state of reality—that natural purity—ever more clearly. Through the manner of generating the realizations of the four fruits of the śrāvakas, the fruit of the pratyekabuddhas, and the nine stages of the bodhisattva’s path of seeing and path of meditation, one is said to have small, medium, or great purity in terms of abandoning the stains to be abandoned; these are called sentient beings who are purified to varying degrees of their stains.
Although there is no impurity from the beginning in the nature of objects such as form, because the adventitious stains of grasping in a distorted way are analogous to a sequence of purification—just as when an eye defect is cleared, one sees the object more clearly—the subject sees the purity of the object, and by virtue of seeing that, the subject is also called "purified." There is no other way of being purified that is separate from this. Thus, while in terms of the mode of appearance, it exists as if the subject is becoming purer and the object is also newly becoming pure, in terms of the mode of abiding, one should know that seeing the nature of objects such as form as primordially pure is itself the "fruit of primordial purity," which is the subject being freed from adventitious stains. Since these two are, from the beginning, of one meaning in the key point of abiding in the great purity where there is no duality of object and subject, one should know that these two purities are identical in essence.
Likewise, the purity of the fruit and the purity of form and so forth are the culmination of the purity of being free from adventitious stains; they become the purity of the primordial wisdom of omniscience, which has completely abandoned the two obscurations. All these purities have one key point in the meaning of the root, the natural purity, just as was explained.
Thus, these three—the purity of the culmination of omniscience, the purity of attaining the fruits of the various paths of the stages, and the purity of the phenomena of the basis, such as form, the aggregates, elements, and sense sources—are posited as distinct in terms of their mode of appearance. However, in terms of their mode of abiding, they are like the element of space, which has no distinctions; they are inseparable in the essence of the one naturally pure expanse of reality, and cannot be divided by the mind.
For the buddha’s primordial wisdom, which sees the natural state of that pure reality exactly as it is, all phenomena are seen as having the nature of being perfectly awakened or primordially beyond sorrow; there is no impurity. That primordial wisdom of realization is also the ultimate purity, having completely and permanently abandoned all obscurations, including the latent tendencies of adventitious stains. Therefore, knowing that all phenomena are of one taste, without distinction, as primordially pure and awakened—without the nature of dualistic phenomena such as object and subject, as they are seen by that wisdom—is the ultimate intent of the perfection of wisdom.
The manner of maintaining this in a state of "just resting" (cog bzhag) in the natural state is as follows:
There is nothing here to be removed,
And nothing here to be established.
Look perfectly at perfection itself;
When perfection is seen, one is completely liberated.
One should understand it in accordance with this. ^1-152

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-153]]

The function of that wisdom of meditative equipoise. ^1-153

###### **རྣམ་པར་དག་པའི་ཡེ་ཤེས་ཀྱི་བྱེད་ལས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-154]]

The second, the function, consists of two functions: not abiding in the extreme of peace and not abiding in the extreme of existence. ^1-154

###### **ཞི་མཐར་མི་གནས་པའི་བྱེད་ལས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-155]]

The first, "The hero who acts," and so forth, is one verse. By abiding in the perfection of wisdom, which is the great primordial purity where cyclic existence and peace are non-dual, the heroes—the bodhisattvas—regardless of the circumstances or how they act, have completely and perfectly transcended all the defilements of the three realms. Yet, they do not abide in the one-sided nirvana that is completely liberated from cyclic existence like the śrāvakas. Although they have eliminated the defilements that cause one to be born into cyclic existence against one's will, through their skill in means they demonstrate taking birth in cyclic existence. And although they are free from aging, sickness, and death, through the power of compassion they demonstrate passing away and so forth for the sake of others. It is a fundamental principle of reality (*dharmatā*) that when one realizes the *dharmadhātu*—which does not abide in existence or peace—there arises, for those who are attached to the extremes of existence and peace, a great compassion that is the natural fruit of realizing the profound Dharma, and one attains immeasurable skill in means without effort. ^1-155

###### **སྲིད་མཐར་མི་གནས་པའི་བྱེད་ལས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-156]]

The second, "These beings," and so forth, is one verse. These beings, who lack the wisdom that realizes the absence of intrinsic nature, are attached to the mud of the five aggregates of name and form, which is difficult to cross, and they wander in the wheel of cyclic existence, which is like the wheel of the celestial sphere that is pushed by the sun and moon, turning again and again. In this way, the twelve links of dependent origination, characterized by the three of karma, defilement, and birth, are connected in an unbroken chain, circling like a firebrand. These deluded beings are caught in the net of their own confused minds, just as wild animals are caught in an inescapable net, and knowing them to be suffering and difficult to liberate, those who possess the wisdom that realizes this reality are not bound by the net of delusion; rather, they move about in this realm of beings like birds in the sky. ^1-156

###### **མཉམ་བཞག་ཡེ་ཤེས་དེའི་སྤྱོད་ཡུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-157]]

The objective sphere of that meditative equipoise of primordial wisdom. Third, regarding the objective sphere of that meditative equipoise of primordial wisdom, there are two parts: the teaching of the objective sphere of non-practice, and the explanation of the benefits of practicing in that way by means of analogies. ^1-157

###### **མི་སྤྱོད་པའི་སྤྱོད་ཡུལ་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-2-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-158]]

The first, "Perfect practice," and so forth, is one verse. A bodhisattva who has "perfect practice" does not "practice" by apprehending form as having the nature of form, or by apprehending its characteristics, such as impermanence; likewise, they do not "practice" by apprehending consciousness, perception, feeling, or mental formation. In this way, to "practice" by not practicing anywhere is to completely abandon all subtle and coarse "attachment" to the reality of things, and it is to "practice" the wisdom of the Sugatas, who are completely liberated from all attachments. ^1-158

###### **དེ་ལྟར་སྤྱོད་པའི་ཕན་ཡོན་དཔེས་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-2-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-159]]

Second, the verse regarding the "collection" of practicing in that way is one verse. A bodhisattva who practices in that way, being wise and clear, "cuts through all attachment to arrogance and proceeds," and in some Sanskrit manuscripts it also appears as "is liberated." They proceed from one stage to the next in equality and without attachment. For example, just as the sun is free from the planet known as Rāhu and shines brilliantly, and just as setting a fire burns all grass, wood, and forests, so too does the great wisdom, like light and fire, destroy the darkness of the afflictions and the fuel of the defilements. The aspects of that meditative equipoise of primordial wisdom. ^1-159

###### **མཉམ་བཞག་ཡེ་ཤེས་དེའི་རྣམ་པར་དམིགས་མེད་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-4-2-4-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-160]]

Fourth, the demonstration of non-apprehension in the aspect of that meditative equipoise of primordial wisdom. "All phenomena," and so forth, is one verse. In this way, all phenomena included within the defiled and the purified are, by their own nature, pure in the nature of defilement, and the objects of knowledge are completely pure. When bodhisattvas abide, observing the meaning of the supreme perfection of wisdom that realizes such an intrinsic nature of things, they do not apprehend the observer—the one who practices the perfection of wisdom—nor do they apprehend any of the phenomena to be observed. Abiding in the expanse where conceptual proliferation has subsided is what it means to practice the supreme perfection of wisdom. The gateway to the extensive teaching. ^1-160

###### **རྒྱས་པར་བསྟན་པའི་སྒོ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-161]]

Regarding the third, the gateway to the extensive teaching, there are five parts: the sequence of training in application, the manner in which the fruit of that application is perfectly accomplished, the presentation of the person who applies themselves to it, the greatness of the person who engages in that way, and the greatness of the dharma that is to be learned by them. Regarding the first: ^1-161

###### **སྦྱོར་བ་ལ་བསླབ་པའི་རིམ་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-162]]

The sequence of training in application consists of five parts: the nature of the application to be cultivated, the qualities arising from cultivating the application, the faults of obstruction, the function of the application, and the explanation of the spiritual friend as the condition. ^1-162

###### **བསྒོམ་བྱ་སྦྱོར་བའི་ངོ་བོ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-163]]

The nature of the application to be cultivated: The first point consists of one verse, beginning with "Regarding the Victorious One..." It is a response to the question posed by the King of Gods, Śakra, to the Victorious One, the Lion of Men: "How should bodhisattvas, who have performed meritorious deeds for previous Victorious Ones and have generated roots of virtue from that, and who are sustained by a spiritual friend, exert themselves in practicing the perfection of wisdom?" In response, it was stated: "One should not exert oneself in the manner of apprehending or abiding in phenomena included within the aggregates and elements, even to the extent of an atom. Not apprehending, not abiding, and not exerting oneself in the aggregates and so forth—this is the authentic exertion of bodhisattvas." The qualities arising from cultivating the application: ^1-163

###### **སྦྱོར་བ་བསྒོམ་པ་ལས་བྱུང་བའི་ཡོན་ཏན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-164]]

The second point, regarding the qualities, consists of three: the quality of reverence as the cause of application, the quality of the result arising from application, and the quality of the nature of application in its current state. ^1-164

###### **སྦྱོར་བའི་རྒྱུ་གུས་པའི་ཡོན་ཏན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-165]]

The first point consists of one verse, beginning with "Whoever hears this teaching..." Regarding any person who, having heard from this text of the perfection of wisdom that all these phenomena, though lacking intrinsic nature, do not cease in their appearance—being explained as similar to illusions and magical creations—does not hesitate regarding their meaning, and having gained conviction, exerts themselves with diligence in learning them: such a sentient being should be known as one who has long since entered the Great Vehicle. It should be understood that they have previously performed meritorious deeds by way of making offerings and serving many tens of millions of Buddhas. Without relying upon such previously accumulated causes, it would not occur that one would hear the meaning of the profound perfection of wisdom and develop faith in it. ^1-165

###### **སྦྱོར་བ་ལས་བྱུང་བའི་འབྲས་བུའི་ཡོན་ཏན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-166]]

The second point consists of two: the quality of not being obstructed by hindrances, and the quality of attaining the three kāyas. ^1-166

###### **བར་ཆད་ཀྱིས་མི་ཚུགས་པའི་ཡོན་ཏན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-167]]

The first point consists of two verses, beginning with "The path of the wilderness..." Just as a person who has entered the path of the wilderness, which is devoid of people and extends for many leagues, eventually sees a delightful and perfect forest at the boundary between the wilderness and a village, where the village cowherds reside, and upon seeing it, those who have traveled the long path think, "These are signs that a village or city is near," thereby finding relief from the fears present in the wilderness, such as tigers, and from that point forward have no fear of bandits in their minds; in the same way, when one desires to attain supreme enlightenment and is searching for the means to do so, whoever obtains the fortune of hearing this, the perfection of wisdom—the mother of the Victors and the supreme path—finds relief from the suffering of cyclic existence and has no fear of falling into the extremes of existence or quiescence, for they do not fall into the stage of a Śrāvaka arhat, nor do they fall into the stage of a Pratyekabuddha. ^1-167

###### **སྐུ་གསུམ་ཐོབ་པའི་ཡོན་ཏན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-168]]

The second point consists of three: the quality of attaining the dharmakāya, the quality of attaining the saṃbhogakāya, and the quality of attaining the nirmāṇakāya. ^1-168

###### **ཆོས་སྐུ་ཐོབ་པའི་ཡོན་ཏན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-169]]

The first point consists of two verses, beginning with "A person who goes to see the water of the ocean..." When a person goes to see the water of the ocean, if they see trees, forests, and mountains in the direction ahead, they know that the ocean is still far away. However, if they do not see those signs of distance, such as mountains, and instead the ground is entirely level and sloping, and the far shore is not visible, they think, "Now the great ocean is near to me." There is no doubt about this, for the great ocean is vast and level, with no visible far shore, and it is impossible for mountains and the like to exist within its expanse; thus, one knows it even from afar. Just as in this analogy, those who have entered into supreme enlightenment and have obtained the fortune of hearing this perfection of wisdom—the mother of the Victors—should know that they are near the great ocean of the dharmakāya, the primordial wisdom that is free from all extremes. This is because, through this, the signs of conceptualization, such as forests and mountains, are gradually abandoned, and once the manifest conceptual thoughts have gradually subsided, one attains the non-conceptual primordial wisdom that is free from extremes, which is unerring. Therefore, even if a person who does not part from this perfection of wisdom has not been explicitly prophesied by the Guides, saying, "You will attain buddhahood," they will touch or attain the enlightenment of the Buddha without long delay, due to the unerring nature of cause and effect. ^1-169

###### **ལོངས་སྐུ་ཐོབ་པའི་ཡོན་ཏན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-170]]

Second, the manner of attaining the sambhogakāya. This consists of one verse, beginning with "In the pleasant season of spring..." Just as when, in the pleasant season of spring, old leaves are shed from a tree and fall to the ground, and the tree’s branches, without long delay, produce new leaves, fruits, and flowers, in the same way, whoever obtains this perfection of wisdom in their hands will, without long delay, attain the enlightenment of the Guides. From the time of obtaining the authentic instructions and generating the mind for the supreme vehicle, they will attain the sambhogakāya—the fruition of that mind-generation—which is like a wish-granting tree, possessing the cool shade of the dense leaves of compassion, the blossoming flowers of the major and minor marks, and the fruits of benefiting others. ^1-170

###### **སྤྲུལ་སྐུ་ཐོབ་པའི་ཡོན་ཏན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-2-2-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-171]]

Third, the manner of attaining the nirmāṇakāya. This consists of one verse, beginning with "For example, a woman..." Just as a pregnant woman who has reached the end of her months of gestation is distressed by the suffering of the labor pains, and this state is what is called the time for her to give birth, in the same way, any bodhisattva who obtains this perfection of wisdom of the Victors, and who generates joy of mind and the aspiration of desire for it, will quickly touch upon great enlightenment. By cultivating the seeds of the lineage through this path and completing the accumulations, just as a child is born from a pregnant woman, the nirmāṇakāya will appear in the world in actuality. ^1-171

###### **གནས་སྐབས་སྦྱོར་བའི་ངོ་བོའི་ཡོན་ཏན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-172]]

Third, regarding the qualities of the essence, there are two aspects: not conceptualizing in terms of increase, decrease, and so forth, yet not remaining fixed in a single side of nirvāṇa; and, despite being non-conceptual, the arising of benefit for beings. ^1-172

###### **འཕེལ་འགྲིབ་སོགས་སུ་མི་རྟོག་ཀྱང་མྱང་འདས་ཕྱོགས་གཅིག་ལ་མི་རེག་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-2-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-173]]

First, this consists of one verse, beginning with "The perfection of wisdom..." When a practitioner of the supreme vehicle acts in accordance with the meaning of the supreme perfection of wisdom, they do not see the "increase" of all phenomena—that is, the phenomena of the purified—which is the distinction of arising and the accumulation of a continuum; nor do they see the "decrease" of all phenomena—that is, the manner of ceasing or becoming lower than before. In the ultimate sense, all phenomena are free from increase, decrease, waxing, and waning, and abide in equality. Likewise, because they do not see—by way of superimposition—virtuous phenomena, non-virtuous phenomena, or even the nature of phenomena or the dharmadhātu, they abide in the state where all focal points are stilled and free from conceptual elaboration. Yet, they do not touch upon a one-sided nirvāṇa by way of merely abiding in the expanse of peace; that bodhisattva abides in the equality of wisdom that does not remain in the extremes of existence or peace. ^1-173

###### **རྣམ་པར་མི་རྟོག་ཀྱང་འགྲོ་དོན་འབྱུང་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-2-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-174]]

Second, this consists of one verse, beginning with "Whoever acts here..." Whoever acts in accordance with the perfection of wisdom here does not conceptualize, as they fully grasp the Buddha’s qualities, such as the powers, in their respective ways. Likewise, they do not conceptualize regarding the five powers of the path, the four feet of magical emanation, and so forth, nor regarding the result or the suchness of peaceful enlightenment. They do not conceptualize regarding the aggregates, elements, and so forth, such as form. In meaning, this shows the manner of being free from the four marks of conceptualization. To arrange this in order as stated in the *Dharani of Entering into Non-conceptualization*: it shows freedom from conceptualizing the aggregates and other samsaric phenomena as having an intrinsic nature; freedom from conceptualizing the path of the perfections as an antidote; freedom from conceptualizing suchness as a dharma-nature; and freedom from conceptualizing the Buddha’s qualities as something to be attained. Although free from all such conceptualization, by the power of that non-conceptual, spontaneously accomplished meditative concentration, and by the force of prayers or blessings, they subdue demons and perform all tasks for the benefit of others without effort. Abiding in this non-conceptual meditative concentration, by the nature of reality, they attain the power of being blessed by the Buddhas in a non-dual manner, so that their enlightened activity is never in the least bit wasted. This manner of practice is the supreme perfection of wisdom, acting within non-conceptual primordial wisdom. 
Obstacles to the path. ^1-174

###### **སྦྱོར་བའི་བར་ཆོད་དུ་གྱུར་པ་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-175]]

Third, regarding the explanation of what constitutes an obstruction to the application, there are two parts: the brief presentation and the detailed explanation. ^1-175

###### **མདོར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-176]]

First, regarding the single verse beginning with "Moon of Speech," the Buddha, the Bhagavān—who is renowned as the "Moon of Speech" because he radiates rays of light from his face, which is adorned with the full circle of the major and minor marks, thereby dispelling the darkness of ignorance and the torment of the afflictions of those to be tamed—was asked by the Elder Subhūti: "What are the obstructions to the application for bodhisattvas who delight in the qualities of the Great Vehicle?" The Teacher replied: "There are many obstructions; here, I shall state a few of them for the time being," and so forth. ^1-176

###### **རྒྱས་བཤད།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-177]]

Second, regarding this, there are three parts: the actual obstructions, their causes, and the explanation of the causes for whether or not one is harmed by those obstructions. ^1-177

###### **དངོས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-3-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-178]]

Regarding the first, there are four parts: the degeneration of the application itself, the degeneration from the Great Vehicle path, the lack of the assembly for teaching and listening, and the explanation of other faults indicated by these. ^1-178

###### **རྒྱུ་སྦྱོར་བ་ཉིད་ཉམས་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-3-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-179]]

Regarding the first, there are two: degeneration due to the mind being unserviceable, and degeneration due to having doubts about the Mother. ^1-179

###### **བློ་གྲོས་ལས་སུ་མི་རུང་བས་ཉམས་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-3-2-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-180]]

The first is a single verse beginning with "The Conqueror's wisdom." When one writes, "At the time of writing this very Perfection of Wisdom of the Conqueror," and by extension, when one is holding, reading, reciting, teaching, or listening to it, if one’s conceptualizing eloquence regarding objects of the three times, such as forms, and various aspects such as agitation, dullness, sleep, regret, attachment, aversion, and doubt do not arise, and yet, through that application, one abandons the effort quickly like a flash of lightning without having accomplished the benefit of oneself and others, one should know that this complete degeneration from the application is the work of Māra. ^1-180

###### **ཡུམ་ལ་ཐེ་ཚོམ་ཟ་བས་ཉམས་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-3-2-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-181]]

The second is a single verse beginning with "When this is taught." When this teaching is being explained, some will have doubts, thinking, "The Guide has not mentioned my name here, nor has he mentioned my lineage, the region where I reside, or my clan." Because they have not been explicitly identified in this way, they think they do not see the reason why they would certainly attain enlightenment, and by grasping at such reasons for turning away from the Mother, that doubt causes them not to listen to this profound teaching, but rather to abandon it; this is the work of Māra. ^1-181

###### **ཐེག་ཆེན་ལས་ཉམས་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-3-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-182]]

The second part has three sections: illustrating through similes the abandonment of the Great Vehicle, which is the cause of omniscience; illustrating through similes the seeking of an inferior path; and illustrating through similes the seeking of great enlightenment from an inferior vehicle. ^1-182

###### **རྣམ་མཁྱེན་གྱི་རྒྱུ་ཐེག་ཆེན་སྤང་བ་དཔེས་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-3-2-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-183]]

The first is the two lines beginning with "Not knowing in that way." Because the perfection of wisdom is the sole foundation of all phenomena, those who are obscured by not knowing it in that way abandon this perfection of wisdom, which is like the root of all the authentic teachings of the Buddha, and due to their ignorance, they seek various other teachings that are like branches and leaves; this too is the work of Māra. ^1-183

###### **དམན་ལམ་ཚོལ་བ་དཔེས་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-3-2-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-184]]

The second is the two lines beginning with "Having found the elephant." Just as someone who seeks an elephant, having actually found the elephant, leaves it behind and instead searches for the elephant's tracks, so too, when one has heard the perfection of wisdom, which directly teaches the profound and supreme Dharma, not to seek its meaning with diligence but instead to search for the meaning by way of other, indirect sutras of the perfection of wisdom—this should be understood as being illustrated by that simile; this too is the work of Māra. ^1-184

###### **ཐེག་དམན་ལས་བྱང་ཆུབ་ཆེན་པོ་ཚོལ་བ་དཔེས་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-3-2-1-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-185]]

The third is the single verse beginning with "For example, just as someone." Just as someone who has found food possessing a hundred flavors, but without tasting its flavor, having found supreme food, instead goes on to seek inferior food, so too, any beginner bodhisattva who, having found this supreme Dharma of the perfection of wisdom, does not practice its meaning in the proper way, but instead seeks the ultimate enlightenment through scriptures that teach the path or stage of an arhat—this is just like the aforementioned simile, and this too is the work of Māra. ^1-185

###### **འཆད་ཉན་གྱི་ཚོགས་པ་དང་མི་ལྡན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-3-2-1-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-186]]

The third, regarding the lack of an assembly for teaching and listening, consists of two: relying on oneself and relying on others. ^1-186

###### **རང་ལ་བརྟེན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-3-2-1-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-187]]

The first is the single verse beginning with "Desiring respect." This refers to when one desires fame and to be shown respect by others, and furthermore desires to taste the flavor of food, wealth, and the like, and with a mind accompanied by the desire to look at external objects, one becomes involved in the practice of becoming familiar with the households of the town. By way of various such distracting activities, one abandons the writing, listening, and contemplating of this profound Dharma, and instead engages in any of the various non-Dharmic activities that are the basis for unwholesome states. Abandoning the correct path and straying into a path that is not the path—this is the work of Māra. ^1-187

###### **གཞན་ལ་བརྟེན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-3-2-1-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-188]]

The second is the single verse beginning with "Those who, at that time." This refers to listeners who, having developed aspiration and faith in the Mother, have gone to listen to this supreme Dharma, but who then perceive that the speaker is possessed of a manner of attachment to bad activities, such as delighting in gain and honor. Having perceived this, they find no joy in listening to the Dharma from that speaker, and thinking, "Now I shall not request the Dharma," they become unhappy and depart. This too is the work of Māra, which acts as an obstruction to listening to the Perfection of Wisdom and the like. ^1-188

###### **དེས་མཚོན་པའི་སྐྱོན་གཞན་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-3-2-1-4-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-189]]

The fourth is the single verse beginning with "At that time." This refers to when those who are listening to this profound Dharma and so forth are not endowed with the assembly of teaching and listening, and other such conditions; this work of Māra, which obstructs the correct application, will arise. What is it? At the time of listening to the profound Dharma and so forth, those Māras, the evil ones, who are like those afflicted by pain, will agitate the minds of many monks who hold the sutras. Through various methods of obstruction, they will cause many other unstated phenomena that act as obstacles to arise, such that one does not properly hold or contemplate this profound perfection of wisdom. In short, whenever one is in a situation of holding this profound Dharma, whatever acts to its detriment should be recognized as the work of Māra, because that which obstructs is designated by the name "work of Māra." As it is said in the *Gaganagañjaparipṛcchā-sūtra*: "Son of noble family, whatever involves engaging in non-virtuous phenomena and abandoning virtuous phenomena, all of that is the work of Māra." ^1-189

###### **བདུད་ལས་འབྱུང་བའི་རྒྱུ་མཚན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-3-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-190]]

The second is the reason why the work of Māra arises. This is the single verse beginning with "Just as a precious jewel." Just as those precious jewels that are found to have immeasurable value are rare because they are not found at all times, and at the times when they are found and possessed, there is always much harm from others, those who are covetous, enemies, and so forth, likewise, these supreme Dharma teachings of the perfection of wisdom of the victorious ones are also extremely rare to arise. Because it is difficult for those with perfect merit to be worthy of them, there is always much harm. ^1-190

###### **བར་ཆད་ཀྱིས་ཚུགས་མི་ཚུགས་ཀྱི་རྒྱུ་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-3-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-191]]

The third section consists of two parts: indicating the object upon which those obstacles arise, and the manner in which the buddhas protect one from those obstacles. ^1-191

###### **བར་ཆད་དེ་གང་ལ་འབྱུང་བའི་ཡུལ་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-3-2-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-192]]

The first part consists of the three lines beginning with "Those who have newly entered the vehicle." Regarding those sentient beings of lesser intellect who have newly entered the vehicle, Māra becomes eager to create obstacles for them because he perceives an opportunity; this is because this precious Dharma, which is extremely rare and profound and which causes one to transcend the domain of Māra, has not been found by them before, meaning they have not yet been brought under his influence. ^1-192

###### **བར་ཆད་དེ་ལས་སངས་རྒྱས་ཀྱིས་བསྲུང་བའི་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-3-2-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-193]]

The second part consists of two sections: the actual subject and its illustration through analogies. ^1-193

###### **དངོས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-3-2-3-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-194]]

The first part consists of the single line beginning with "The buddhas of the ten directions." If one were to think, "If all those who initially practice the Mother are beginners, and they encounter such great obstacles from Māra, then it is difficult for a bodhisattva to complete the cultivation of the perfection of wisdom," [it is answered that] just as a person of lesser capacity who has found a precious treasure is protected by the king of the land, all the buddhas, the Blessed Ones, who dwell in the world systems of the ten directions and possess unimpeded wisdom-vision, exert themselves or engage in the effort to fully protect those who are initially practicing this Dharma and have not yet attained the realization of having transcended the domain of Māra from the abyss of obstacles. However, it should be understood that depending on the causes of that person’s previous merit and the conditions of their current faith and so forth, the blessings of the buddhas may or may not become a field of influence, and obstacles may arise in various ways, whether or not they are able to take hold. ^1-194

###### **དེ་དཔེས་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-3-2-3-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-195]]

The second part consists of the single verse beginning with "If the mother is ill." The reason why the buddhas provide protection is also, for example, just as when a mother is ill, her many children are all unhappy and attend to her, so too do the buddhas of the world systems of the ten directions hold in their hearts this supreme perfection of wisdom, which has become the mother that has given birth to each of them. The function of the application. ^1-195

###### **སྦྱོར་བའི་བྱེད་ལས་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-196]]

The fourth section, explaining the function of the application, consists of two parts: the function of generating one's own spiritual children, and the function of applying one's own spiritual children to the qualities. ^1-196

###### **རང་སྲས་བསྐྱེད་པའི་བྱ་བ་བྱེད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-197]]

The function of generating one's own spiritual children. The first part consists of the two lines beginning with "Whatever past..." As just explained, if one speaks of this as the "Mother of the Victorious Ones," one might ask in what way it becomes a mother. Just as in the world a mother is designated as such through the two aspects of giving birth to children and, having given birth, setting them to their tasks, so too here: all the protectors of the world, the buddhas and bhagavans who have passed, those who currently reside in all the fields of the ten directions, and all those who will appear in the future, arise from this very perfection of wisdom. Therefore, it is the mother of all the victorious ones of the three times. The function of applying one's own spiritual children to the qualities. ^1-197

###### **རང་སྲས་ཡོན་ཏན་ལ་སྦྱོར་བའི་བྱ་བ་བྱེད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-198]]

The second part consists of three: applying oneself to knowing the nature of the basis of cyclic existence and nirvana, applying oneself to the activity of the result—the omniscience of the buddhas—and applying oneself to the method of preventing the two extremes of the path. ^1-198

###### **གཞི་འཁོར་འདས་ཀྱི་ཚུལ་མཁྱེན་པ་ལ་སྦྱོར་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-199]]

The first, applying oneself to knowing the nature of the basis of cyclic existence and nirvana, consists of two parts: becoming skilled in the two truths of the world by relying on the perfection of wisdom, and attaining the marks through realizing suchness. ^1-199

###### **ཤེར་ཕྱིན་ལ་བརྟེན་ནས་འཇིག་རྟེན་གྱི་བདེན་གཉིས་ལ་མཁས་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-200]]

The first part consists of the two and a half verses beginning with "The world, the teacher..." The "world" refers to all those phenomena known as the aggregates, elements, and sense-fields. The perfection of wisdom reveals to its own spiritual children, the buddhas, the nature of the causes and effects and the essence of their conventional designations. This is because, by relying on this perfection of wisdom—the mother who gives birth to the victorious ones—those buddhas perceive these phenomena just as if they had placed a fresh myrobalan fruit in the palm of their hand. Furthermore, for the purpose of working for the benefit of those to be trained, it specifically highlights that the immeasurable realms of sentient beings—the internal mental activities of others, their distractions, their attachments, and their freedom from attachment, and so forth, without limit—are also revealed to its spiritual children through the power of familiarity with the perfection of wisdom; this is the teaching of the conventional. And because the suchness of the five aggregates of the world, the suchness of the arhats, the suchness of the pratyekabuddhas, and the suchness of the bodhisattvas, the children of the victorious ones, are not established as separate, they are a single suchness. Since all of these are devoid of intrinsic nature, they are not something that exists as one thing or another, and thus they are called "suchness." The perfection of wisdom, which is the suchness of the mode of existence of all such phenomena of cyclic existence and purification, is what the Tathagatas have known and realized just as it is. In this way, these two and a half verses state that, by relying on the perfection of wisdom, the buddhas have, in short, perceived the entire doctrinal system of the two truths. ^1-200

###### **དེ་བཞིན་ཉིད་རྟོགས་པས་མཚན་ཐོབ་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-201]]

Second, the single verse beginning with "The wise one, the world..." Just as in the world a mother gives a name to her child, the title "Tathāgata" (Thus-Gone) is obtained in dependence upon the Mother of the Victors. Those who are "wise" (mkhas pa) regarding "reality" (de kho na nyid)—the Buddhas—whether they are dwelling in this "world system" (jig rten gyi khams) or have "passed into sorrow" (mya ngan las 'das), remain like the sky, for this "flawless" (skyon med pa) "reality" (chos nyid) which is expressed as the "emptiness" (stong pa nyid) of all "phenomena" (chos) is immutable and cannot be disturbed by anyone. When bodhisattvas (byang chub sems dpa') have correctly realized with their intellect the mode of being of this reality, which has existed primordially and naturally, and when they have eventually perfected that realization, then, because they have "gone" (gshegs pa) or realized "reality" (de bzhin nyid) just as it is, the Buddhas have been given the title "Tathāgata." 
Connecting the result to the activity of omniscience. ^1-201

###### **འབྲས་བུ་རྣམ་མཁྱེན་གྱི་མཛད་པ་ལ་སྦྱོར་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-202]]

Second, regarding the manner of connecting the result to omniscience, there are two parts: the accomplishment of the two benefits in dependence upon the perfection of wisdom, and specifically, the manner of teaching the Dharma for the sake of others. ^1-202

###### **ཤེར་ཕྱིན་ལ་བརྟེན་ནས་དོན་གཉིས་འགྲུབ་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-203]]

First, the single verse beginning with "The perfection of wisdom..." The "perfection of wisdom" (prajñāpāramitā) is like a divine grove of joy, for when one dwells in it, boundless, undefiled joy arises. The "field of activity" (spyod yul) of the Buddhas, the Guides who possess the "ten powers" (stobs bcu) and dwell in dependence upon this, is as follows: even though they completely deliver immeasurable sentient beings from various sufferings and the three lower realms, those Buddhas never develop the perception of them as "sentient beings." This is because, through their great compassion that is free from conceptual grasping (dmigs med), they perform activities such as teaching the Dharma, which are constant, pervasive, and spontaneously accomplished. ^1-203

###### **ཁྱད་པར་དུ་གཞན་དོན་དུ་ཆོས་སྟོན་པའི་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-204]]

Regarding the second part, there are two aspects: subduing what is not the path, and demonstrating the authentic path. ^1-204

###### **ལམ་མིན་ཚར་གཅོད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-205]]

First, the single verse beginning with "For example, the lion..." Just as the lion, the king of beasts, dwells in the mountain ravines of the snow mountains, fearing no one, and strikes terror into many small animals while letting out its roar, so too the Buddha, the "lion among men" (mi yi seng ge), dwells in dependence upon the profound meaning of emptiness, the "perfection of wisdom" (prajñāpāramitā). He strikes terror into many "heretics" (mu stegs can) who hold views of a self, and he also proclaims the sound of the "holy Dharma" (dam pa'i chos) in the world. ^1-205

###### **ལམ་ཡང་དག་སྟོན་པའི་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-206]]

Second, the single verse beginning with "For example, the sky..." Just as the rays of the sun dwelling in the sky dry up the moisture on this earth and also clearly reveal all forms, so too the Buddha Bhagavān, the "king of Dharma" (chos kyi rgyal po) and "sun of Dharma" (chos kyi nyi ma), who relies upon the "perfection of wisdom" (prajñāpāramitā) which is like the expanse of the sky, dries up the rivers of craving for existence and also perfectly reveals all phenomena of the knowable. 
Applying this to the manner of preventing the two extremes of the path. ^1-206

###### **ལམ་མཐའ་གཉིས་འགོག་པའི་ཚུལ་ལ་སྦྱོར་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-207]]

Regarding the third part, there are two aspects: the essence of the path and its function. ^1-207

###### **ལམ་གྱི་ངོ་བོ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-208]]

Regarding the first, there are two parts: the meaning and the example. ^1-208

###### **དོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-3-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-209]]

First, the single verse beginning with "Not seeing forms..." Since forms lack an intrinsic nature, they are not objects of focus, or are "not seen." Likewise, one does not see sensations, there is no seeing of perceptions, and one does not see intentions. Regarding the field of experience for one who is in meditative equipoise on suchness, there is nothing at all seen that is called consciousness, mind, or mental faculty. Regarding this, other treatises explain that the application of "mind" to the past, "consciousness" to the future, and "mental faculty" to the present is a way of speaking based on those aspects, or is expressed through synonyms. However, Ye shes zhabs (Jñānapāda) explained, as is well known, that "mind" is the ālaya-vijñāna, "mental faculty" is the afflicted mind (kliṣṭa-manas), and "consciousness" refers to the six sense consciousnesses. Thus, because all phenomena, exemplified by the five aggregates, are not objects of focus, this state of not seeing them as having any nature whatsoever is what the Tathāgata taught as "seeing the true nature of phenomena." This is just like how not seeing blurriness because one has no eye defect is, in fact, seeing the nature of blurriness. ^1-209

###### **དཔེ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-3-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-210]]

The second part is the single verse beginning with "Seeing space..." What is the example for this "seeing of not seeing"? In the world, sentient beings think and express in words, "I see space." Yet, if one were to ask how they see space, they would be unable to say, for there is no form, shape, or color in space that could become an object for the eye. If you examine this for yourself, asking what the meaning of such thoughts and expressions is, you will come to understand that the convention of "seeing space" is applied merely to the fact that one does not see any obstructive phenomena. In that same way, the Tathāgata taught that "seeing the suchness of phenomena" is also, through the eye of wisdom, knowing that no phenomena are established, and thus not focusing on or seeing them as having any nature whatsoever. In the world, all instances of what is called "seeing" refer to a subject seeing an object; but this "seeing" that does not focus on any extreme and does not see any conceptualized characteristics is taught by the example of "seeing space" only as a partial analogy. In reality, the dharmadhātu, which transcends all extremes such as existence, non-existence, being, and non-being, is seen by the wisdom of individual self-awareness; it cannot be described in any way by other examples, for it is inconceivable and has truly transcended being measured or counted by examples, logical reasons, and the like. ^1-210

###### **ལམ་དེའི་བྱེད་ལས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-211]]

Second, regarding the functions of that path, there are two: the gathering of all the qualities of the path and the fruit, and the manner of distancing oneself from straying from the path. ^1-211

###### **ལམ་འབྲས་ཀྱི་ཡོན་ཏན་ཀུན་སྡུད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-3-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-212]]

Regarding the first, there are two: the performance of all activities through the analogy of a minister, and the gathering of all qualities through the analogy of a king. ^1-212

###### **བློན་པོའི་དཔེས་མཛད་པ་ཀུན་བྱེད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-3-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-213]]

First, regarding "by which, in that way," and so forth, this is one verse: a bodhisattva who does not see any aspect of conceptualization whatsoever, and who sees in that way with the eye of wisdom, is one who sees all phenomena; the attainment of great activity from that is like a king who remains in equanimity without effort, while an excellent minister performs all the king’s tasks. In the same way, all the activities of a Buddha and all the qualities of the śrāvakas are performed by relying upon the non-conceptual perfection of wisdom. If one possesses the wisdom that realizes selflessness, all paths of liberation are easily accomplished, whereas otherwise, there is no means for their accomplishment. ^1-213

###### **རྒྱལ་པོའི་དཔེས་མཛད་པ་ཀུན་བྱེད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-3-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-214]]

Second, regarding "a king in a city," and so forth, this is one verse: just as a great king does not go into cities, nor does he go into other great provinces, but while remaining in his own royal palace, he gathers in all the revenues and so forth; in the same way, the realization of the profound suchness within the continuum of a bodhisattva is unmoving and does not go anywhere else. By the power of that suchness in the continuum that has realized it, the gathering of all the qualities of the Buddha-ground is a marvelous activity. ^1-214

###### **ལམ་གོལ་བ་ལས་ཐག་བསྲིང་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-3-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-215]]

Second, there are two parts: the brief indication and the detailed explanation. ^1-215

###### **མདོར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-3-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-216]]

First, regarding "to whom the Sugata," and so forth, this is one verse: for an individual who possesses faith in the Sugata and his offspring, the bodhisattvas, and who has the stability of an irreversible faith and aspiration, and who, through reflection, seeks and diligently practices the supreme perfection of wisdom—the path to becoming a bodhisattva and a Buddha—because they possess both method and wisdom, they have completely transcended the two levels of the Śrāvaka and Pratyekabuddha, which are stray paths. Having done so, they are not overwhelmed by the obscurations of existence and peace, and will quickly attain the supreme awakening of the Victorious One. Thus, faith in the supreme vehicle is the initial gateway of entry, and because wisdom ensures that one does not decline from the fruit attained in accordance with that faith, these two—method and wisdom—are taught as primary. Although a beginner may have faith, if they do not possess the perfection of wisdom, it is possible for them to decline from the path; however, once the profound intent of the perfection of wisdom has been brought under one's realization, one attains an unassailable faith, and it is impossible to decline from the path. ^1-216

###### **རྒྱས་པར་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-3-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-217]]

Second, regarding the detailed explanation of that method, each of the four examples—sinking in water, a new vase, boarding a boat, and aging—is used to illustrate both the example and the meaning. ^1-217

###### **ཆུར་བྱིང་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-3-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-218]]

First, there are three verses beginning with "For example, in the ocean." For example, the boat of those entering the ocean breaks and falls apart; those who do not grasp onto a corpse, grass, or wood remain there, perish in the midst of the water, and do not reach the shore of the ocean, whereas those who grasp onto wood and the like rely upon it and reach the other shore of the ocean. Just like that example, whoever possesses faith and has attained a joyful sense of clarity while seeking the perfect enlightenment, if they do not follow with clarity the perfection of wisdom—the Mother of the Victors, which is the path to that perfect enlightenment—they will not touch the fruit, the enlightenment of the Sugatas. In this way, whoever is always revolving in that great ocean of cyclic existence, which is agitated by the waves of birth, aging, death, and various sufferings, if they are completely seized by the supreme perfection of wisdom that realizes the lack of intrinsic nature, they will become skilled in the non-erroneous intrinsic nature of phenomena and will demonstrate the ultimate meaning. In this way, if those qualities of means, such as the faith of one seeking perfect enlightenment, are completely seized by the perfection of wisdom that is devoid of conceptual grasping, one will quickly touch the supreme, wondrous enlightenment of the Sugatas, just as those who grasp onto wood and the like attain the other shore of the ocean. ^1-218

###### **བུམ་པ་གསར་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-3-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-219]]

The second section, "For example, some..." and so forth, consists of two verses. For example, if someone were to put water into a pot that has not been fired, because the pot lacks the quality of hardness, it is reasonable to understand that it will quickly break. If one puts water into a pot that has been fired, one has no fear that the pot will break on the road where one is traveling, and it will safely arrive back at one's home. Just like that, even if a bodhisattva has much or great faith in desiring perfect enlightenment, if they fall away from the perfection of wisdom—the path that accomplishes it—they will quickly fall into unsuitable states, such as the lesser paths, and will fall away from the path of the Great Vehicle. By means of "faith and wisdom"—that is, the perfection of wisdom accompanied by faith—if that person's continuum is completely seized, they will transcend the two stages of the hearers and solitary realizers and will attain supreme enlightenment. ^1-219

###### **གྲུ་འཛུགས་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-3-2-2-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-220]]

The third section, "For example, a well-constructed..." and so forth, consists of two verses. For example, a boat that has not been well-constructed—with its seams, joints, and leaks not properly repaired—will break or be ruined in the middle of the ocean, along with its cargo and merchants. If that boat is well-examined and has been well-constructed, it will not break or deteriorate, and it will arrive safely at the shore of the ocean, along with the merchants and their cargo. Just like that, even if a bodhisattva has cultivated their continuum with faith, if they do not rely upon the perfection of wisdom, they will quickly fall away from the teachings of supreme enlightenment. If that same faithful person possesses the supreme perfection of wisdom, they will not be tainted by the faults of the lesser paths, and without falling away from the path of the Great Vehicle, they will attain the enlightenment of the Victors. ^1-220

###### **རྒས་པའི་དཔེས་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-4-2-3-2-2-2-4-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-221]]

The fourth section, "One hundred and twenty years," and so forth, consists of two verses. For example, a person who has reached one hundred and twenty years, having reached the limit of their lifespan, is aged and suffering; even if they are helped to rise from their seat, they are unable to travel to another place by their own power, because they are old and decrepit. If that person is supported by two strong men on their left and right, they will have no fear of falling on the road and will easily arrive at the place they wish to go. Just like that, a bodhisattva who has only a small amount of the wisdom that realizes suchness, even if they have truly entered the path of supreme enlightenment, will fall away significantly before attaining the fruit. If that same person’s continuum is completely seized by means such as faith and the supreme wisdom that realizes selflessness, they will not fall away from the path and will attain the enlightenment of the Tathāgata. ^1-221

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-222]]

Explanation of the condition of the spiritual friend. ^1-222
