###### **རྐྱེན་དགེ་བའི་བཤེས་གཉེན་བསྟེན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-5-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-223]]

The fifth section, on relying upon the condition of the spiritual friend, consists of two parts: an explanation of the spiritual friend to be relied upon, and an explanation of the student who relies upon them. ^1-223

###### **བརྟེན་བྱ་བཤེས་གཉེན་བསྟེན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-5-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-224]]

The first part, on relying upon the spiritual friend, consists of two points: the manner in which one must rely upon a qualified spiritual friend, and the manner in which that spiritual friend imparts instructions. ^1-224

###### **མཚན་ཉིད་ལྡན་པའི་དགེ་བའི་བཤེས་གཉེན་ལ་བརྟེན་དགོས་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-5-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-225]]

The first part consists of two points: the actual reliance, and the reason why one must rely in that manner. ^1-225

###### **དངོས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-5-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-226]]

The first section, beginning with "bodhisattva," consists of one verse. It states: "A bodhisattva who is situated on the stage of the initial practice," meaning any beginner who, with the superior intention of focusing especially on the welfare of others and perfect enlightenment, has entered the path by seeking the supreme enlightenment of the Buddha and its fruition. Such excellent students, who possess respect for their gurus, should always rely upon learned gurus who have good views and conduct and are capable of teaching the intended meaning of the Great Vehicle. ^1-226

###### **དེ་ལྟར་བརྟེན་དགོས་པའི་རྒྱུ་མཚན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-5-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-227]]

The second section, beginning with "Why is that?" consists of one verse. If one asks why it is that beginners who have entered the Great Vehicle are instructed to rely earnestly upon a holy spiritual friend, it is because all the qualities of being learned in the path and fruition of the Great Vehicle arise from those spiritual friends, and because those spiritual friends teach this perfection of wisdom, the path of the Great Vehicle. Therefore, the Victorious One, the perfect Buddha, who possesses the supreme of all qualities, has said that the attainment of the Buddha’s qualities depends upon the spiritual friend. In this way, the path of skillful means—meditation, contemplation, and hearing—depends upon the preceding one for the subsequent one, and that hearing, in turn, depends upon relying on a spiritual friend. ^1-227

###### **བཤེས་གཉེན་དེས་གདམས་ངག་སྟོན་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-5-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-228]]

The second section, regarding the manner of presenting the quintessential instructions, consists of two parts: how the instructions are presented, and the expression of praise for having presented them in that way. ^1-228

###### **གདམས་ངག་ཇི་ལྟར་སྟོན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-5-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-229]]

The first section, beginning with "Generosity, discipline, and so on," consists of one verse. In this way, when presenting quintessential instructions to those who have newly entered the Great Vehicle, one must teach a path that does not separate method and wisdom, in accordance with the capacity of their mental continuum. The instruction to "engage in the six perfections—generosity, discipline, patience, and likewise diligence, meditative concentration, and wisdom—by way of fulfilling the three stages of preparation, main practice, and conclusion, and to dedicate all those roots of virtue to the ultimate great enlightenment with the intention of attaining omniscience" is the instruction regarding the aspect of method. As for the "enlightenment" that is sought as the ultimate attainment, one should not become attached to the entities of the aggregates, such as form, nor should one grasp at them through supreme or excessive fixation. Why is that? Because all phenomena, including enlightenment itself, are devoid of intrinsic nature; thus, the instruction to realize this is the instruction regarding the aspect of wisdom. In this way, one should teach those who have the intention and the capacity for enlightenment. ^1-229

###### **དེ་ལྟར་བསྟན་པའི་བསྔགས་པ་བརྗོད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-5-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-230]]

The second section, "Acting in that way," consists of one verse. Those who are "acting in that way"—that is, the Mahāyāna spiritual friends who teach the quintessential instructions well—have become a great ocean of excellent qualities that are difficult to fathom. These "speaking moons" who possess the rays of light of the true Dharma have become a refuge that dispels the suffering of beings by teaching the Dharma, which is the cause of benefit and happiness; they have become allies who establish them in the bliss of nirvāṇa; and they have become a place that eliminates the causes of suffering. Likewise, they have become a support for those proceeding toward enlightenment; they have become the wisdom that knows what is beneficial and what is not; and they have become an island for those submerged in the river of craving for existence. "Island" refers to a place suitable for dwelling in the midst of water; it is a name for a place situated between two waters. Because they teach the reality of the unborn, they are like captains or the like who desire the purpose of, or perform the task of, completely leading beings out of the mud of cyclic existence. By teaching the selflessness of persons, they have become illuminators; by teaching the selflessness of phenomena, they have become great lamps of the Dharma; and by way of the analytical knowledges, they are unperturbed or fearless in speaking of emptiness, the supreme Dharma. This is because they have established a deep-seated conviction in the profound Dharma and have perfected the confidence of being victorious over all directions in explaining its path and fruit. Alternatively, one may explain that "completely leading" means leading others onto the path by guiding them like a captain; that "desiring the purpose" means accomplishing the benefit of others spontaneously through compassion; that "speaking of the supreme Dharma" refers to the suchness; and that "unperturbed or fearless" means not being overwhelmed or shaken by the polemics of others, such as māras and non-Buddhists. 
[The section on the disciple who is the recipient follows.] ^1-230

###### **རྟེན་པ་པོ་སློབ་མ་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-5-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-231]]

Second, regarding the explanation of the disciples who listen to the profound perfection of wisdom, there are two parts: the indication of disciples who possess conviction in the profound, and the indication that such conviction is difficult to attain. ^1-231

###### **ཟབ་མོ་ལ་ཡིད་ཆེས་པ་དང་ལྡན་པའི་སློབ་མ་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-5-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-232]]

Regarding the first, there are two parts: the manner of having a profound intellect, and the indication of the qualities of that profundity. ^1-232

###### **བློ་ཟབ་པའི་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-5-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-233]]

First, regarding "those possessing great renown," and so forth, this is one verse. Those who have entered the Great Vehicle, the path that is supremely profound and vast, and who possess the great renown of the name "bodhisattva" throughout the infinite buddha-fields, are those who don the armor of what is difficult to perform. This is because they arouse the mind, saying, "I shall lead all sentient beings, who are conventionally as infinite as space, to the realm of nirvāṇa without remainder." Furthermore, they are free from the focus on cyclic existence and the path of purification; thus, they do not don the armor of focusing on the reality of the aggregates, elements, and sense-fields. They are free from the perception of being strongly attached to the three vehicles, and because they abide in the perfection of wisdom, which does not apprehend or grasp any phenomenon whatsoever, they do not turn back from attaining the fruit of complete enlightenment, which possesses the three great purposes through conviction in the profound meaning, nor do they waver from the manner of the Great Vehicle path. Because no adverse condition—neither Māra nor any opponent—can cause their mental continuum to become afflicted, they are of the nature of being unshakeable. As the extensive sūtra states: "Blessed One, those bodhisattvas who don the armor to lead all sentient beings to nirvāṇa, yet do not apprehend sentient beings, are performers of what is difficult," and "Subhūti, that armor is not connected to form," and so forth. ^1-233

###### **ཟབ་པ་དེའི་ཡོན་ཏན་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-5-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-234]]

The second section, "These are like this," and so forth, consists of one verse. In this way, those bodhisattvas possess such profound and unfathomable Dharma, and by the power of that, they are free from the conceptual elaborations of all objects of focus. They are free from hesitation, doubt, and indecision regarding the profound meaning, and they possess the meaning of conviction. Regarding this, "indecision" and so forth are merely synonyms for doubt, indicating that there is no doubt whatsoever, whether subtle or coarse, that would manifest. Alternatively, one should view this as a distinction of reversal, such that there is no dualistic mind regarding the path and its fruit, in the manner of having doubt about the present, indecision about the past, and hesitation about the future. Through that conviction which is free from doubt, having heard the profound perfection of wisdom, one’s mind does not turn back from its meaning. Because one has brought the profound Dharma under the control of one’s own wisdom, one does not rely on others; this means it is not the case that one lacks certainty, such that one would enter wherever another leads. Because one has become like that, one should know that one will not turn back from the path and fruit of the supreme vehicle. ^1-234

###### **དེ་ལྟ་བུའི་ཡིད་ཆེས་པ་དཀའ་བར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-5-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-235]]

The second section, on the manner in which that profound [Dharma] is difficult to realize, consists of two parts: the teaching that the Dharma to be practiced is profound, and the reason why it is profound in that way. ^1-235

###### **སྤྱད་བྱའི་ཆོས་ཟབ་པར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-5-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-236]]

The first section, beginning with "The Guides," consists of one verse. This great mother, the Dharma of the perfect Buddhas, the Guides, is supremely profound. Because all phenomena of the basis are unborn and, as mere suchness, cannot be apprehended, they have primordially transcended the sphere of the mind that grasps and is grasped; thus, they are difficult to see. For this reason, there is no one who realizes them during the stage of the path, nor is there any attainment of them at the time of the result. For if there were something that existed as an entity to be realized or attained, it would not be appropriate to call that a true realization or attainment. As other sutras also state, if even the Buddha himself did not find enlightenment, what need is there to speak of others? This is because the entity of enlightenment is primordially empty. It is just as has been repeatedly stated in this manner. Because this profound suchness of phenomena—in which there is no seeing, realizing, or attaining—is profound and difficult to fathom, the perfect Buddha, who is endowed with compassion and works for the benefit of all sentient beings, having attained supreme, great enlightenment, felt little inclination to teach the Dharma, thinking, "Who among the assembly of sentient beings is capable of knowing such a profound Dharma as this, which I have realized in my heart?" As it is said in the *Lalitavistara*: "I have found a Dharma that is profound, peaceful, free from proliferation, luminous, and uncompounded, like nectar. Since no one to whom I might teach it would understand, I shall remain silent and dwell in the forest." And as it is said in the *Mūlamadhyamakakārikā*: "Therefore, having realized that the depth of this Dharma is difficult for the weak to fathom, the Sage's mind turned away from teaching the Dharma." ^1-236

###### **དེ་ལྟར་ཟབ་པའི་རྒྱུ་མཚན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-1-5-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-237]]

The second point, beginning with "Sentient beings," consists of one verse. If one asks for the reason why this profound Dharma is difficult for the majority to understand, it is because sentient beings, from beginningless time, have been habituated to attachment to the reality of the mind. Due to this, they delight in the ways of grasping at a self and the like within the bases of craving, and they desire the objects that are grasped. They dwell in the mind that grasps these, and because they fall into the sphere of activity of the conceptual proliferation of that which is grasped and the grasper, they are unskilled in the meaning of suchness—which is distinguished by the absence of grasping and the grasped—and have become foolish and ignorant, not knowing how to apply their minds to its way, just as if they were dwelling in the midst of darkness. The meaning of the Mother, which is the Dharma to be taught and attained, is that there is no object to be established by apprehension, and there is no grasping at anything whatsoever. Since those supramundane phenomena are thus discordant with the entire world and are supreme, disputes arise with the world, because worldly people grasp at phenomena as existing, while the other side teaches that they do not exist. Therefore, because worldly people do not see the way of the Dharma that teaches the non-existence of phenomena, they dispute; whereas the Tathāgata, having seen the nature of phenomena just as it is, even though there is no intrinsic nature, acts in accordance with the world by using conventional terminology for those phenomena that appear like illusions to the worldly, just as they appear in the world. For this reason, it is stated in another sūtra: "The worldly dispute with me, but I do not dispute with the world; whatever the worldly believe to exist, I also believe to exist," and so forth. ^1-237

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-238]]

The manner in which the result is perfectly accomplished through that application. ^1-238

###### **སྦྱོར་བ་དེའི་འབྲས་བུ་ཡང་དག་པར་འགྲུབ་པའི་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-239]]

Second, regarding the manner in which the result is perfectly accomplished through that application, there are two parts: identifying the result to be attained, and the manner in which it is not attained if this path is absent. ^1-239

###### **ཐོབ་བྱའི་འབྲས་བུ་ངོས་བཟུང་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-240]]

Regarding the first, there are two parts: the example and the meaning. ^1-240

###### **དཔེ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-241]]

The first part consists of one verse, beginning with "The space element..." It is as follows: the space element is limitless in the eastern direction, the southern direction, and likewise in the western direction and the northern direction—that is, it is as limitless as the four directions extend. Similarly, it exists in the upper, lower, and four intermediate directions; in short, it exists throughout the limitless expanse of the ten directions, and there is nowhere that is not pervaded by space. Because it lacks an essence that has become a concrete entity in itself, that space of the different directions is not divided into separate parts, and in its own nature, there is no distinction whatsoever such as shape or the like. ^1-241

###### **དོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-242]]

Second, the verse beginning with "The suchness of the past..." consists of one stanza. Just as in the example of space there is no distinction, in the same way, whatever is the suchness of phenomena of the past, whatever is the suchness of the future, whatever is the suchness of the present, whatever is the suchness of persons such as the arhats, whatever is the suchness of all phenomena that are the basis of knowledge, whatever is the suchness of the result, the Victorious One, and whatever is the suchness of the path’s phenomena, such as the realization of emptiness—in this way, there is no suchness among all of these that is established as a separate distinction; rather, they are of one taste as the essence of the dharmadhātu. Although all things divided by dualistic phenomena—such as cyclic existence and nirvāṇa, or past and future—appear as distinct and are designated as such merely in terms of conventional appearance, in the ultimate sense of the actual state, all phenomena are unborn and unceasing. Because they have become equal in the nature of the dharmadhātu by their very essence, there is no way to differentiate them by way of the three times, self and other, or good and bad. Everything abides in the nature of the single, unique sphere of the dharmadhātu. Having realized this through wisdom endowed with a single moment of consciousness, one attains the perfect, complete enlightenment in the essence of the dharmakāya, which possesses the supreme of all aspects of self-arisen wisdom. This great enlightenment, which is devoid of increase or decrease and is unchanging and untransforming, is the ultimate result. ^1-242

###### **ལམ་འདི་མེད་ན་དེ་མི་ཐོབ་པའི་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-243]]

The second part has two sections: a brief presentation and a detailed explanation. ^1-243

###### **མདོར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-244]]

The first section, "The Sugata's enlightenment," and so forth, consists of one verse. This ultimate fruit to be attained—the enlightenment of the Sugata, which is like the sky, free from the distinct phenomena of the knowable and the knower—is the nature of the non-dual, naturally occurring primordial wisdom or the dharmakāya. Any bodhisattva who wishes to attain this must, through the means of faith and so forth that seek the perfect enlightenment, engage in or strive for the supreme perfection of wisdom that realizes the suchness of all phenomena. Without this perfection of wisdom, the mother of the victors, which acts as the guide, that great enlightenment to be attained will never be achieved; for the cause of that ultimate fruit is definitively this very non-conceptual primordial wisdom, which is the supreme of all paths and bears the name "perfection of wisdom." ^1-244

###### **རྒྱས་པར་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-245]]

The second part has two sections: the manner in which the ultimate fruit is not accomplished if one is separated from this path, and the manner of engaging in this path once that is understood. ^1-245

###### **ལམ་འདི་དང་བྲལ་ན་མཐར་འབྲས་མི་འགྲུབ་པའི་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-246]]

The first part has two sections: the example and the meaning. ^1-246

###### **དཔེ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-2-2-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-247]]

The first section, "A bird with a large body," and so forth, consists of one verse. To provide an example of what has been posited, consider a bird whose large body measures one hundred and fifty yojanas. If its wings were exhausted and it lacked the strength to fly, and that bird were to leap from the realm of the Trayastriṃśa gods toward this Jambudvīpa, then in that situation, the bird would inevitably suffer harm, such as injuring its body, and there is no other possibility. ^1-247

###### **དོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-2-2-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-248]]

The second section, "The Victors," and so forth, consists of one verse. One might ask: what is the meaning that corresponds to the manner of the example just explained? Even if these five perfections, such as generosity, which constitute the path of the Victors, are practiced diligently for many billions of eons, and even if one constantly relies in this world upon vast and limitless aspirations to engage again and again in the vast path and fruit, that alone is not sufficient to complete the causes for attaining great enlightenment. The unerring cause of great enlightenment is the union of the two—method and wisdom—like the pair of wings that allow a bird to fly through the sky: the immeasurable skillful means of the Great Vehicle, such as dedicating the roots of virtue toward omniscience, and the wisdom that realizes the equality of all phenomena. If one lacks such skillful means and is separated from the wisdom that realizes suchness, one falls into the fruit of the Śrāvaka, just as in the example where, if a bird with a large body lacks wings, it will fall downward when it leaps. If one possesses the perfection of wisdom that is the ultimate nature of reality, then method and wisdom are inseparable in the essence of that primordial wisdom, because it is the subject that realizes the two truths as non-dual. ^1-248

###### **དེ་ལྟར་ཤེས་ནས་ལམ་འདིར་འཇུག་པའི་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-249]]

The second section, "The Buddha Vehicle," and so forth, consists of one verse. One might ask: since this perfection of wisdom—the supreme Buddha Vehicle—is the sole path that traverses the causes of ultimate great enlightenment, and since one who wishes to attain the state of great enlightenment must rely upon it, what kind of conduct should one possess when engaging in it? The Great Vehicle has as its root compassion that focuses on the welfare of others, it is accompanied by skillful means, and its fruit is the accomplishment of the welfare of sentient beings for as long as space remains. Therefore, the manner of engaging in it and the manner of the person who has entered it is to treat all limitless sentient beings with an impartial mind, without discrimination, abiding in the perception of them as fathers, mothers, brothers, sisters, and so forth. With a mind of love that desires their long-term benefit and immediate happiness, one should strive for the welfare of those sentient beings. One should be free from the stains of anger and malice, and without deceit or hypocrisy, one should maintain a straightforward mental continuum, speak gentle words—such as teaching the Dharma in accordance with the truth—and perform actions of body that are loving. For one who has generated a mind that sincerely benefits sentient beings, it is not appropriate to engage in conduct that is inconsistent with this, such as deceit, hypocrisy, or malice. ^1-249

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-250]]

Regarding that, the explanation of the individual who engages in the practice. ^1-250

###### **སྦྱོར་བ་པོ་གང་ཟག་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-251]]

Regarding the third point of the root text, the explanation of the individual who engages in the practice, there are two parts: a brief presentation by way of a question, and a detailed explanation by way of a response.
Brief presentation by way of a question: ^1-251

###### **ཞུས་པའི་སྒོ་ནས་མདོར་བསྟན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-252]]

The first is a single verse beginning with "Protector of the world." The Elder Subhūti requested the Buddha, the Blessed One, the Protector of the three worlds, as follows: "Please reveal to me what the signs and tokens are of the absence of defilements in bodhisattvas who possess immeasurable oceans of qualities. How, and by what signs, can one know that those bodhisattvas who have great power to accomplish the two goals will not turn back from the path of great enlightenment—that is, how are they to be known as irreversible bodhisattvas? Please, Victorious One, prophesy for me even a portion or a fraction of the immeasurable qualities of those great beings, the bodhisattvas."
Detailed explanation by way of a response: ^1-252

###### **ལན་གྱིས་རྒྱས་པར་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-253]]

Regarding the second, there are three parts: identifying the irreversible [bodhisattva] as indicated by signs, the distinct characteristics of the conduct of that irreversible [bodhisattva], and the explanation of the distinction between those who turn back and those who do not. ^1-253

###### **རྟགས་ཀྱིས་མཚོན་པའི་ཕྱིར་མི་ལྡོག་པ་ངོས་བཟུང་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-254]]

Identifying the irreversible [bodhisattva] as indicated by signs. The first is taught through six verses beginning with "different" and "perception." Thus, the signs of irreversibility, as requested by Subhūti and explained by the Sugata, are as follows: A bodhisattva who has realized the profound nature of phenomena sees all phenomena as suchness, without division, and is therefore free from the perception of difference regarding all phenomena of cyclic existence and purification. They speak with words praised by the wise, possessing the logic that accords with the nature of reality when teaching the profound Dharma, and they cannot be swayed to another view. Having attained irreversible confidence in the Teacher of this path, they do not rely upon other sectarian ascetics or brahmins with the perception that they are teachers of the path, nor do they place deep trust in the various paths taught by them. Being skilled in and confident in the ways of the Dharma, such as the infallibility of cause and effect, they have abandoned manifest non-virtue and have thus attained the state of having completely abandoned the three lower realms and all the unfortunate states represented by them. Those wise ones naturally dwell on the path of the ten virtuous actions and are zealously devoted to establishing others therein. Without a mind that looks toward material gain, such as offerings and honors, they teach the path of the perfections and other such phenomena in accordance with how they were taught by the Buddhas and bodhisattvas, following them accordingly. Through confidence in the meaning of the profound Dharma, they are single-pointedly joyful in the holy Dharma, and, not engaging much in other activities, they make the Dharma their sole priority. Having cultivated a mind that benefits others, they are always endowed with the three actions of body, speech, and mind of love, such as speaking gentle words. Through the habit of being conscientious regarding virtuous phenomena, they are endowed with mindfulness and clear comprehension while walking, lying down, or sitting—that is, in whichever of the four postures they are engaged, they have abandoned heedlessness. What is this like? It is like this: even when walking on the path, they look only as far as a yoke’s length, and they are free from mental confusion, such as the mistaken paths or physical injuries that arise from constant heedlessness. By the power of a wise and conscientious mind, they dwell in the blameless Dharma, and in order to abandon the disbelief of other beings, they [maintain] all external and internal conduct. ^1-254

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-255]]

By acting purely and wearing pure garments, one maintains pure conduct that accords with the minds of those to be tamed; one’s three doors of body, speech, and mind are free from faults and as clear as a stainless crystal; and by seeing the inherent flaws of conditioned things, one does not become overly attached to them, thus taking on the qualities of training, and in this way, one is not one who desires gain. By suppressing the opposing factors of the perfections, such as stinginess, one’s own continuum is adorned with supreme qualities, and by working extensively for the sake of others, one naturally becomes a leader among men. One is always desiring the profound Dharma, meaning that because the Dharma and one’s own continuum are blended, one proceeds at all times possessing the reality of the Dharma; thus, having brought the profound reality of the Dharma under one’s control, one’s mind naturally becomes inseparable from it, and whatever one says or does is in accordance with it and does not transcend it—just as those who are attached proceed in all their conduct in accordance with their attachment. Because one has become a person of faith who is patient with the profound Dharma, even when the emanations of the sinful Māra show other paths or manifest hells and the like to cause fear of cyclic existence, and through various means attempt to turn one away from the path of the Great Vehicle by showing a mere semblance of a path, one recognizes them as Māra and is not harmed by his various activities; therefore, one has passed beyond the domain of Māra and does not rely on others, meaning one possesses the strength of one’s own mind, not dependent on others. One does not abide in the four meditative absorptions, even when entering into them or practicing them, because one does not taste the flavor of those absorptions. Because one knows things to be like illusions, one is not one who desires fame and renown for oneself. Because one’s own continuum is tamed by the holy Dharma, one is free from anger that disturbs the mind or from being afflicted by it. ^1-255

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-256]]

Not attached to their own desires and skilled in means, even if they become householders for the sake of others, they always give without attachment to any phenomena; and, having realized selflessness and being governed by a mind of compassion for others, they abandon all wrong livelihood and ends of action that arise from grasping strongly at their own benefit. How is this so? For the sake of their own livelihood, they do not seek wealth through means that have light or minor ripening, such as taking life or flattery; they do not perform the mantras of violent action that inflict harm upon others for their own sake; they do not perform mantras to subjugate women or to bind them to their own will due to attachment; and they do not use their speech to prophesy that a being in the womb or one about to be born in the desire realm will become a male or a female for the sake of their own fame. By these examples, they possess the signs of having purified wrong livelihood and the ends of wrong action. Furthermore, they strive for the supreme perfection of wisdom by being completely secluded from the various distractions of the activities of the three doors—namely, the busyness of action, speech, and conceptual proliferation. Because their mental continuum has become refined in this way, wherever they abide, they are free from fighting, quarreling, and the like, and their mind of love is also completely stable, not to be snatched away by external conditions. ^1-256

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-257]]

With a mind that takes singular delight in the omniscient Buddha, one engages in seeing him and making offerings, while desiring and aspiring to attain his rank oneself. With a mind that constantly aspires to the Buddha’s teaching, the sublime Dharma, and is devoted to it, one possesses the practice of the ten ways of acting in accordance with the Dharma. Through the power of aspiration or wisdom, one gains mastery over birth, thereby completely abandoning the lands of border-dwellers, barbarians, and the fringes of civilization where the Dharma does not flourish, and by the nature of reality, one attains a dwelling in a land endowed with the Dharma. Having become certain, without doubt or hesitation, regarding the qualities of one’s own stage and so forth, one remains as steadfast as Mount Meru, never to be swayed by other paths. For the sake of the sublime Dharma, one becomes free of difficulty in even relinquishing one’s own life, and one is highly diligent in the yoga of upholding the Dharma and contemplating its meaning. These are to be known as the signs of non-retrogression for those individuals who have entered the path of application, where the certainty of experiencing the profound Dharma has reached the vital point, and who have seen the truth. If one possesses the yoga of the path of method and wisdom, and possesses such self-evident signs of being certain of and familiar with it, then by those signs one can be certain of non-retrogression, just as smoke indicates fire. ^1-257

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-258]]

The specific characteristics of the conduct of that irreversible one. ^1-258

###### **ཕྱིར་མི་ལྡོག་པ་དེའི་སྤྱོད་པའི་ཁྱད་པར་ཕྱིར་ལྡོག་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-259]]

Regarding the second, there are three types of conduct: the profound conduct that has the ultimate as its object, the vast conduct that has the conventional as its object, and the conduct of skillful means that is the union of the two. ^1-259

###### **དོན་དམ་པའི་ཡུལ་ཅན་ཟབ་མོའི་སྤྱོད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-260]]

The profound conduct that has the ultimate as its object. The first of these has two parts: identifying the profound meaning to be practiced, and the manner of practicing it. ^1-260

###### **སྤྱད་བྱ་ཟབ་མོའི་དོན་ངོས་བཟུང་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-261]]

Identifying the profound meaning to be practiced. The first of these has two parts: explaining the profound nature itself, and the manner of accumulating merit by practicing it. ^1-261

###### **ཟབ་མོ་ཉིད་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-262]]

The first is the single verse beginning with "Form, feeling, and so on." Form, feeling, perception, volition, and consciousness—these phenomena are profound. How are they profound? Because they are empty, being devoid of intrinsic nature; they are without the marks of being "formable" and so on; and because the aspiration of grasping at them is completely pacified, they cannot be apprehended by the intellect. For example, just as one cannot find the bottom of the ocean when searching for it with an arrow, saying "it is like this," when one examines and investigates with the wisdom that analyzes that very nature, one cannot find the bottom of the aggregates. ^1-262

###### **དེ་ལ་སྤྱད་པས་བསོད་ནམས་མངོན་པར་འདུ་བྱ་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-263]]

The second has two parts: the actual explanation and the refutation of objections. ^1-263

###### **དངོས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-264]]

The first is the commentary on the Verse Summary, which has two parts: a brief thematic summary and a detailed explanation. ^1-264

###### **མདོར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-265]]

The first is the single verse beginning with "Bodhisattva, and so on." Which bodhisattva? One who has realized the nature or abiding mode of phenomena—the object of realization—as being profound in its very essence; and who, by abiding in that which is free from attachment through non-conceptualization regarding the supreme, ultimate object, has realized this profound teaching: that there is no such thing as an established self-nature of the aggregates, elements, and sense-fields. What merit could be more supreme than the merit of one who has realized this? That is indeed the supreme merit perfectly established. ^1-265

###### **རྒྱས་པར་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-266]]

The second part has four points: the benefits of keeping that profound teaching in mind, the benefits of upholding it, the benefits of meditating upon it during and after the session, and the benefits of viewing even such merit as being like an illusion. ^1-266

###### **ཟབ་མོ་དེ་ཡིད་ལ་བྱས་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-267]]

The first is the single verse beginning with "For example, desire, and so on." Regarding this, the extensive sutras state: "Whoever contemplates, weighs, and definitively realizes this profound abiding place associated with the perfection of wisdom, and practices yoga in it for even a single day, just as it has been taught in the perfection of wisdom—how much merit does that person generate in that single day?" It is taught that, just as one who engages in desire and so on—up to the point of abandoning the faults that cause one to turn one's back on saṃsāra and turn away from perfect awakening for as many eons as those thoughts—so too here: For example, if a person who engages in the phenomena of desire makes an appointment with a woman, and if they do not meet, then for that entire day, various discursive thoughts of the mind will arise in that person; due to the power of attachment, they will think of that very object again and again. The merit accumulated over as many eons as there are such momentary discursive thoughts of the mind will be attained by a bodhisattva who practices yoga in this teaching for a single day, for even each single moment of the mind will gather an immeasurable accumulation of merit. ^1-267

###### **ཟབ་མོ་དེ་འཆང་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-268]]

The second is the single verse beginning with "A bodhisattva, whoever..." If a bodhisattva were to practice giving for many thousands of millions of eons to arhats, pratyekabuddhas, and so on, or practice giving for the sake of the fruit of the śrāvaka or pratyekabuddha paths, and also maintain pure morality, yet another person were to express even for a single day the excellence of the teaching associated with the supreme perfection of wisdom, that giving and morality would not even approach a fraction of it. ^1-268

###### **མཉམ་རྗེས་སུ་བསྒོམས་པའི་ཕན་ཡོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-269]]

The third is the single verse beginning with "A bodhisattva, whoever..." A bodhisattva, having meditated in meditative equipoise on the supreme perfection of wisdom, arises from that and, in the subsequent attainment, expresses the teaching that is free from attachment, which is in accordance with their meditation and free from the faults of conceptualization regarding signs, or as it is also said, expresses the teaching that is not tainted by conceptualization seeking gain, honor, and the like. If one also dedicates that teaching to the cause of supreme enlightenment for the sake of the benefit of beings, there is no virtue in the three worlds—under the earth, on the earth, or above the earth—that is equal to it. ^1-269

###### **དེ་ལྟ་བུའི་བསོད་ནམས་ཀྱང་སྒྱུ་མ་ལྟར་བལྟས་པའི་ཕན་ཡོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-2-4-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-270]]

The fourth is the single verse beginning with "That merit also..." In this way, although there are immeasurable, countless, and incalculable merits conventionally in thinking about and explaining the perfection of wisdom and so on, in the ultimate sense, merit also lacks inherent nature. Because the merit that arises from explaining and so on is not established by its own characteristic, it is "accumulation" (gsog); because it is empty of the aspects of suffering and so on, it is "emptiness" (stong pa nyid); because it is devoid of signs, it is "hollow" (gsob); because it is not established by its own essence but is dependently originated, it is "unreliable" (ya ma brla); and because it is separated from what is to be grasped by conceptualization, it is "essenceless" (snying po med pa). Having thoroughly understood it in this way, if one practices the conduct of the perfection of wisdom of the Sugatas, at the time of practicing such conduct, one fully attains immeasurable merit. Here, "accumulation" because it lacks own-characteristic, and the three beginning with "emptiness" are emptiness. Because of the three beginning with "emptiness of emptiness," it is "accumulation." Because of the five beginning with "emptiness of the conditioned," it is "hollow." It is also explained that because of "emptiness of intrinsic nature" and so on, it is "essenceless." In meaning, it is taught through a series of deceptive terms: "accumulation" because, from the perspective of the mode of things lacking inherent nature, it is gathered in many ways but is like something flimsy, lacking stability; "hollow" because it is like a fabricated reflection, such as a lion; and "unreliable" because it is unable to stand on its own, depends on others, is crooked, and cannot be relied upon. ^1-270

###### **ཀླན་ཀ་སྤང་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-271]]

Second, the refutation of objections consists of two parts: refuting the objection that, in the ultimate sense, it is illogical to attain buddhahood because there is no increase in the fruit of merit and no abandonment or attainment, and refuting the objection that it is also illogical to attain buddhahood in the conventional sense. ^1-271

###### **དོན་དམ་པར་བསོད་ནམས་ཀྱི་འབྲས་བུ་འཕེལ་བ་དང་སྤང་ཐོབ་མེད་པས་སངས་རྒྱ་བ་མི་འཐད་པའི་ཀླན་ཀ** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-272]]

Regarding the first, there are two aspects: the absence of exhaustion and increase in the ultimate sense, and the way in which buddhahood is nonetheless tenable in the conventional sense. ^1-272

###### **དོན་དམ་པར་ཟད་འཕེལ་མེད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-273]]

First, the verse beginning "What the Buddha has explained" consists of one stanza. All the various teachings that the Buddha, the Bhagavan, has explained—those he has summarized, those he has explained in great detail, and those he has taught extensively along with the logical reasoning that establishes them—are, in reality, beyond apprehension, for both the expressions and the expressed are inexpressible. However, knowing that they are merely names used in conventional discourse, even though he taught countless gateways to the Dharma over many billions of eons, expressing them in the manner of refutation or proof, there is no exhaustion in the words of the expressions. Yet, in the very nature of the dharmadhātu itself, there is no exhaustion of faults and not the slightest increase of qualities; it is the state of equality, which is inexpressible and remains forever free from decrease or increase. Therefore, in the ultimate sense, all phenomena are primordially nirvana, in the state of equality without exhaustion or increase, and there is not the slightest thing that needs to be attained as enlightenment. ^1-273

###### **མེད་ཀྱང་ཀུན་རྫོབ་ཏུ་སངས་རྒྱས་འཐད་པའི་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-274]]

Second, the verse beginning "The path of the Victors" consists of one stanza. Even those phenomena that are called the "perfection of wisdom of the Victors" are proclaimed to be merely designated by names, without their own essence being established. However, conventionally, a bodhisattva who possesses the union of method and wisdom—knowing that the path and the fruit are devoid of intrinsic nature and thus having no mind that clings to this or that—dedicates all roots of virtue to unsurpassed, perfect enlightenment. Such a bodhisattva does not fall away from the true path due to the infallible nature of dependent origination, and it is tenable in conventional discourse that they will attain the supreme enlightenment of a Buddha. This is because, from the realization of the state of reality—the primordially pure nature of all phenomena as nirvana—the attainment of enlightenment, characterized by the removal of adventitious stains, is infallible from the perspective of how things appear. ^1-274

###### **ཀུན་རྫོབ་ཏུ་ཡང་སངས་རྒྱས་ཐོབ་པ་མི་འཐད་པའི་ཀླན་ཀ་སྤང་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-275]]

Second, regarding the refutation of objections conventionally, there are two parts: the validity of abandoning what is to be abandoned, and the manner in which the attainment of qualities is valid. ^1-275

###### **སྤང་བྱ་སྤོང་བ་འཐད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-276]]

Regarding the first, there are two parts: the example and the meaning. ^1-276

###### **དཔེ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-277]]

First, the verse beginning "The essence of butter" consists of one stanza. If one asks: "In terms of conventional discourse, the mind that brings about the attainment of enlightenment is momentary and cannot be accumulated; therefore, how can that mind attain an enlightenment that is free from obscurations? For the previous mind alone does not abandon obscurations, nor does the subsequent mind alone, which does not rely upon the previous one, abandon them, and since those two are not accumulated together, how can this be?"—to this, one should first take an example well-known in the world. Just as when the essence of butter—that is, the clarified butter—and the burning flame of a lamp come into contact, it is not the case that the first instant of the flame alone burns that essence completely, nor does it fail to burn it without that first instant; and it is not the case that the final contact between the butter and the flame alone burns that essence of butter, nor does it fail to burn it without that final flame. Nevertheless, when the continuum of instants from the first instant of the flame to the final one is complete, the entire essence of the butter has been burned by the fire and is entirely consumed. One should understand it by taking that as an example. ^1-277

###### **དོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-278]]

The second, the verse beginning "The first mind also," consists of one stanza. Just as in the example just explained, it is not the case that the first mind, the mind that has just generated the aspiration, by itself alone touches the supreme enlightenment that is free from all that is to be abandoned; nor is it possible to touch that enlightenment without it. Furthermore, it is not the case that the final mind of the continuum, which is at the threshold of attaining enlightenment, by itself alone attains the great enlightenment that is free from all stains; nor is it possible to attain that enlightenment without it. Nevertheless, the continuum of mind, in which each subsequent moment becomes more excellent than the one before it, ultimately attains the great enlightenment that has abandoned all that is to be abandoned; this is the nature of reality, dependent origination, which cannot be denied. ^1-278

###### **ཡོན་ཏན་ཐོབ་པ་འཐད་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-279]]

The second, concerning the manner of attaining qualities, consists of two parts: the validity of attaining qualities and the validity of the perfection of qualities. ^1-279

###### **ཡོན་ཏན་ཐོབ་པ་འཐད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-280]]

Regarding the first, there are two parts: explaining how there is no irrationality, and explaining how it is definitely valid by way of dependent origination. ^1-280

###### **མི་འཐད་པ་མེད་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-2-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-281]]

The first, the verse beginning "From a seed," consists of one stanza. To those who argue that if the initial mind that brings about the attainment of enlightenment were to remain until the result is attained, it would be permanent, and if it did not remain but ceased, then since enlightenment would not arise from each of those momentary minds individually, the emergence of the qualities of enlightenment cultivated over countless eons would be conventionally irrational, the response is as follows: Although all conditioned things are momentary, when the momentary causes have preceded, their result cannot be delayed; this is the nature of reality, dependent origination. Just as in the world, from a seed, a sprout, and from that, a stalk, flowers, and fruits emerge in sequence, and although at the time of the fruit their respective causes have ceased, it is not the case that the seed has ceased and the fruit, the tree, has become non-existent; rather, even though the seed has ceased, the fruit, the sprout and so forth, appear in sequence. Likewise, in this context of examining the result of the path, the mind that first generates the aspiration, or the "first mind," is the cause of enlightenment. Even though that mind ceases, it is not the case that its subsequent result, enlightenment, is non-existent; rather, in the manner of unfailing cause and effect, the result, enlightenment, is unfailingly attained from the causes of the earlier and later minds during the stages of the path. ^1-281

###### **རྟེན་འབྲེལ་གྱི་སྒོ་ནས་ངེས་པར་འཐད་པ་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-2-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-282]]

The second point concerns the single verse beginning with "Having found the seed," and so forth. The mode of dependent origination is as follows: when one has found—that is, possesses—a seed that is endowed with the collection of conditions capable of producing its own result, then the result, such as various grains or rice, will arise. The result of that seed does not exist in the seed from the very beginning, yet it is not the case that it is nonexistent in the sense of being incapable of arising; for if it existed from before, a cause would not be needed, and if it were nonexistent in the sense of being incapable of arising, the term "cause" would be meaningless. Thus, when one analyzes its nature, even though it is devoid of the nature of entities such as existence or nonexistence, its appearance in the manner of an illusion or the like, manifesting in an unerring way, is the mode of the phenomenon of dependent origination. In that same way, this enlightenment of the Victors also arises by the power of dependent origination; therefore, one should understand that because it is devoid of the nature of an entity established as true when analyzed, it arises like an illusion. ^1-282

###### **ཡོན་ཏན་རྫོགས་པ་འཐད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-283]]

The second point concerns the justification of the perfection of qualities, which is a single verse beginning with "Like a drop of water." If all phenomena are momentary and cannot be accumulated, then even if one practices for countless eons, it is impossible for them to gather together and increase; so how, then, is the result of enlightenment, which is the perfection of all qualities, attained? Although all phenomena are indeed momentary, because each subsequent mind depends upon the preceding mind, the later ones become endowed with special qualities, and thus, in the end, there is a perfection of qualities. Just as a vessel is filled by collecting many drops of water starting from a single one, where, by gradually collecting small amounts from the first drop to the last, the vessel is eventually completely filled, in that same way, when one considers the completion of the number of bodhicittas generated in sequence across the ten stages and five paths, the attainment of buddhahood is similar to that example. Even the initial generation of bodhicitta during the stage of engagement is a cause of supreme enlightenment; although that alone does not perfect all qualities, by gradually perfecting white qualities from one level to the next, one attains buddhahood, just as a vessel is not filled by a single drop of water but is filled by many. One should take the example of how a vessel is filled by many drops, not by one, as an analogy for how one attains buddhahood by completing many moments of generating bodhicitta. And just as the nature of water is also momentary, yet the vessel is filled by the mutual gathering of the continuum of drops, one should understand that the continuum of momentary minds, where each subsequent mind becomes more excellent than the previous one, functions in that same way. ^1-283

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-284]]

As for the manner of engaging in that conduct: ^1-284

###### **ཟབ་མོ་དེ་ལ་ཇི་ལྟར་སྤྱོད་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-285]]

Second, regarding the manner of engaging in that profundity, there are two parts: the actual practice and the explanation of its qualities. ^1-285

###### **དངོས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-286]]

The first is a single verse on emptiness, signlessness, and so forth. In this way, because all phenomena are in their own essence emptiness, the meaning is that all views that take them as objects of focus are thoroughly pacified; because the basis or cause to be focused upon in that way is signless, the meaning is that all conceptualization and discursive thought are thoroughly abandoned; and because, therefore, there is no attachment to that or aspiration for a result, the meaning is that one is free from all aspirations of the three realms. Thus, one engages in the profound dharma of realizing the three gateways to liberation, which is the meaning of the equality of existence and peace. By the power of this, one does not touch upon a nirvana that is one-sided peace, nor does one engage in the signs of grasping at entities in existence. This is like a skilled navigator who, while traveling between the far shore and the near shore of the ocean, does not dwell on the two extremes of far and near, nor does he dwell within the ocean itself. Furthermore, in this context of the perfection of wisdom, the various synonyms taught—such as the three gateways to liberation, non-arising, suchness, and reality—all agree in a single meaning. Since that which is emptiness is also signless and aspirationless, one should understand that although these various synonyms have different negations, they are one in the meaning of the dharma-sphere to be realized, just as honey has a single taste. ^1-286

###### **དེ་ཡི་ཡོན་ཏན་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-287]]

Second, the verse beginning "Thus practicing" is a single stanza. Bodhisattvas who practice the meaning of the equality of existence and peace in this way have no such arrogance as thinking, "I will be prophesied by the Victorious One who possesses the ten powers," or "I will attain the enlightenment that is to be obtained." Because they have realized that in this meaning of the way things are, there is nothing to be eliminated, nothing to be established, nothing to be obtained, and nothing to be abandoned, they have penetrated deeply into the nature of enlightenment as being like the sky, without any fear that the mind cannot bear. Practicing in this way is to practice the profound perfection of wisdom of the Sugatas. ^1-287

###### **ཀུན་རྫོབ་པའི་ཡུལ་ཅན་རྒྱ་ཆེ་བའི་སྤྱོད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-288]]

The practice of the vast domain of conventional reality. Second, the practice of purifying the field, which is the vast domain of conventional reality, is a single stanza beginning with "In the wilderness of the world." This refers to seeing the ten directions of the world-realm as appearing impure: the faults of the container, such as wilderness paths that are dangerous, rough places, and areas filled with vicious wild beasts, being devoid of water, villages, and prosperity; and the faults of the contents, seeing sentient beings as afflicted by famine, being poor in the wealth of Dharma and material goods, and being sick with the various illnesses of physical and mental suffering and internal afflictions. Seeing all of this with a mind that knows it to be illusory, they are without fear. Then, having donned the great armor of purifying the field in this way, they engage in it, thinking: "Alas, these sentient beings have little merit. Since they are tormented by various afflictions and sufferings, I shall purify the field for the sake of all of them, so that when I eventually become a Buddha, in that Buddha-field there will be no such faults at all, and I shall establish perfect prosperity." For that purpose, they constantly strive until the very end of future eons in the accumulations of the perfections. Furthermore, through the gateway of thoroughly knowing that these lack intrinsic nature, they do not produce even a particle of a mind that is weary of that path. The practice of the union of skillful means. ^1-288

###### **ཟུང་དུ་འཇུག་པ་ཐབས་མཁས་ཀྱི་སྤྱོད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-289]]

Third, regarding the teaching on the conduct of skillful means in union, there are three parts: a brief presentation, an extensive explanation, and a summary of the meaning. ^1-289

###### **མདོར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-290]]

First, regarding the bodhisattva and so forth, there is one verse: Bodhisattvas always practice and abide in the perfection of wisdom, the mother of the victors. How is this so? By the power of the wisdom that is the subject, they know that all these phenomena, such as the aggregates, are from the very beginning empty and unborn; thus, they enter into meditative equipoise on the meaning of suchness in a non-dual manner. Yet, just as in the state of non-equipoise, they engage in great compassion for the immeasurable and diverse dispositions of sentient beings. They do not abandon the welfare of sentient beings by abiding solely in the peaceful expanse through meditative equipoise, because they have attained the profound and supreme yoga of the union of wisdom and compassion. Even in that interval of engaging in the diverse welfare of sentient beings, they do not fall away from the perfection of wisdom—the non-abiding abiding of the Buddha’s qualities—because they have attained the wisdom of skillful means in which wisdom and compassion are non-dual. Here, the expression "non-equipoise" refers to engaging in compassion for the deluded dispositions of sentient beings who are not in meditative equipoise on the profound meaning of suchness; it also refers to the bodhisattva themselves engaging in compassion for the dispositions of sentient beings when they are not in meditative equipoise; and it also explains that in those intervals, such as when abiding in the midst or between the union of wisdom and compassion, they do not fall away from the Buddha’s qualities, such as the powers, because they possess such skillful means and thus do not manifest mere peace alone. In any case, this indicates the essence of the skillful means that is the union of wisdom and compassion. ^1-290

###### **རྒྱས་པར་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-291]]

The second point is taught by way of eight examples: the example of an illusion, not abandoning sentient beings; the example of a vessel, fulfilling the force of aspirations; the example of a flying bird, not falling despite having no support; the example of shooting an arrow, remaining without being completed; the example of magical display, not becoming weary in working for the sake of others while remaining; the example of holding a parasol, not falling into extremes; the example of a captain, accomplishing the benefit and happiness of oneself and others; and the example of a merchant, being skilled in the path—these are the eight. ^1-291

###### **སྒྱུ་མའི་དཔེས་སེམས་ཅན་མི་འདོར་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-3-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-292]]

The first point consists of three verses beginning with "For example, a person..." If one asks what kind of skillful means it is that unites wisdom and compassion, it is explained sequentially through examples. First, having briefly stated, "For example, a person skilled in the means of subduing enemies, possessing all qualities," if one asks what qualities they possess, the way they are explained in detail is as follows: they have become endowed with the strength of body and mind, and have applied themselves to tasks that others cannot overcome. If one asks what these are, it is that they have become knowledgeable in the arts or rituals, such as leaping with the body; they have gone beyond the perfection of archery, such as shooting arrows, and many other fields of craft; and furthermore, they know how to accomplish the methods of displaying various illusions, and are those who greatly desire the purpose of walking the path—or, as the commentary says, those who possess compassion and apply themselves to the benefit of other beings. Such a person, having gathered together their own father, mother, and wife, has traveled on a path through a wilderness filled with many who act as enemies. ^1-292

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-293]]

When that person arrives at the gathering of enemies, they manifest many fearless, heroic beings who are capable of attacking the adversary, thereby ensuring that no harm from the enemies or others occurs; having done so, they travel safely to the place they desire and return safely to their own home. Just as in this example, when a bodhisattva is in the stage of practicing the path and engaging in the benefit of beings, they are skilled in means, having generated great compassion for the entire range of sentient beings. They transcend the four māras—such as the māra of the god-child, which acts as an obstacle to the Mahāyāna path—as well as the two stages of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. They abide in the supreme samādhi endowed with the three gateways to liberation, and they do not touch upon enlightenment by way of actualizing the reality limit, which is merely the element of peace.
If one wonders whether it is possible for great bodhisattvas of the pure grounds—who have matured in non-conceptual wisdom and are in meditative equipoise, having blended into the reality limit as a single taste, since the reality limit is by nature devoid of all conceptual proliferation—to pass into parinirvāṇa in the interim, within that very element of peace, it is not possible. Because they are completely seized by skill in means, they do not actualize the reality limit until the qualities of a buddha are perfected. Furthermore, the teachings in other sūtras regarding the Buddha’s exhortation to arise from peace, and so forth, which explain the method of preventing the extreme of peace, do not imply that there is a basis for falling into the extreme of peace within the nature of the reality limit, the dharmadhātu. If such a basis existed, it would not be the perfection of wisdom, as it would not abide by its own nature in the state of peace; consequently, it would not be suitable as the knowledge of the basis that is close to the resultant Mother, and so forth. Thus, the vital point of the Mahāyāna would be lost, and the perfection of wisdom would become a path common to the three vehicles, rather than the uncommon path of the Mahāyāna.
If one asks what, then, is the case: the reality limit, the dharmadhātu, is the ultimate meaning of the abiding nature, the equality in which the two truths are inseparable by their own nature; thus, it does not abide in either existence or peace. Since actualizing it as it is constitutes buddhahood itself, it is impossible to actualize it on the path of learning, for the causes for such actualization are incomplete. If one wonders why, then, the text speaks of skill in means that prevents one from actualizing it in the interim, as if there were a need for such concern: during the path of learning, there are varying degrees of wisdom and means. From the perspective of the aspect of the reality limit that is devoid of conceptual objects, if one were to cling to it as "just that," it would become the one-sided extreme of peace. Therefore, in order to teach the skill in means that eliminates such grasping at extremes, and so that they may know that the ultimate emptiness—the dharmadhātu endowed with all supreme aspects—is a profound teaching that even those on the pure grounds must enter again, the text speaks of the power of internal skill in means on the eighth ground. Regarding those who abide there, it is also a law of reality that the Buddhas, as external conditions, exhort them, saying, "Son of noble family, you have not yet attained my power of dharma and so forth; therefore, apply yourself! Generate compassion for the immeasurable range of sentient beings!"
Although that bodhisattva on the eighth ground has attained the yoga of non-effort while abiding in the emptiness endowed with all supreme aspects, because their samādhi—which abides in the aspect of wisdom, the element of peace—is predominant, they have not yet attained the equality of the Buddhas, whose domain of engagement is the ultimate, where the dharmadhātu, the nature of all-pure emptiness endowed with all supreme aspects, is integrated into a single unity without any inequality between means and wisdom. Thus, the meaning is that they must enter the path again, from higher to higher. It is not the case that there could ever be a pure-ground bodhisattva who passes into parinirvāṇa in the interim. Even from the first ground, because they see the reality of the two truths as inseparable, they are certain to be of the buddha-lineage; however, because they have not attained the non-conceptual wisdom of the pure grounds, they are like the seven impure grounds, where the aspect of abiding in means is predominant.
If one asks, "Do the sūtras not say that if the Buddhas did not exhort them, they would inevitably pass into parinirvāṇa?" that is indeed so, but that is stated as an example of "clinging to the ultimate" in order to make it known that the samādhi of the eighth ground is supreme in its extreme peacefulness. It is not at all possible that the Buddhas would not exhort them; rather, it is a law of reality that, by the power of their internal wisdom of skill in means, the Buddhas provide that external condition of exhortation to accomplish the true gateway. Moreover, this is true even from the time one is born into the Mahāyāna lineage and attains the first ground of non-retrogression, so there is no need to speak of this case. Therefore, if one passes into parinirvāṇa in the interim without having attained the ultimate result—the great parinirvāṇa of non-abiding, which is buddhahood itself—it is nothing other than the parinirvāṇa of the one-sided extreme of peace. The skill in means that prevents this is none other than the perfection of wisdom, in which emptiness and compassion are inseparable, having as its object the two truths as inseparable. Understanding that this teaches a specific context of the practice of pure-ground bodhisattvas regarding how to engage in such skill in means is of vital importance. This is a brief explanation of the significant points of doubt. ^1-293

###### **སྣོད་ཀྱི་དཔེས་སྨོན་ལམ་གྱི་ཤུགས་རྫོགས་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-3-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-294]]

Second, regarding the two verses beginning with "The wind relies upon the sky": when the world forms and abides, initially the mandala of wind relies upon the sky, and the mass of water relies upon that wind, and this great earth relies upon that water, and the beings that dwell upon the earth, such as those of the four continents, rely upon that earth. The cause or basis for sentient beings to experience the collective karma they have accumulated is just like this. Even though the elements thus come to rely upon one another, contemplate the meaning of the fact that, ultimately, the sky does not rely upon anything else; by doing so, you will come to understand the manner in which it relies upon nothing at all. ^1-294

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-295]]

In that same manner, a bodhisattva who abides in sky-like emptiness also manifests various and manifold deeds of benefit and happiness throughout the realms of sentient beings, relying upon the immeasurable prayers they have made for the sake of those beings, through a great compassion that lacks the grasping of sentient beings as mere designations or as having an ultimate nature. Furthermore, they do not abide solely in the path of emptiness while remaining untouched by nirvana, which is the singular result. ^1-295

###### **བྱ་འཕུར་བའི་དཔེས་རྟེན་མེད་ཀྱང་མི་ལྟུང་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-3-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-296]]

Third, regarding the two verses beginning with "At the time when": when one is training on the path of the Great Vehicle, at the time when a bodhisattva is themselves skilled in the ultimate meaning and has become one who clarifies by dispelling the darkness of others' ignorance, they engage in this supreme meditative stabilization that has emptiness as its object and in which conceptual proliferation is pacified. At that stage of training on the path, they do not cultivate even the slightest sign to be apprehended; rather, they abide in the meaning of having become signless. They engage in the pacification of the conceptualizations of cyclic existence and the complete pacification of the conceptualizations of nirvana. Just as there is no other place for a bird flying in the sky to rely upon—for although the sky does not rely upon anything, it does not fall to the ground—in that same way, a bodhisattva who engages through the doors of the three liberations does not touch the extreme of nirvana by means of skillful means, nor do they engage in the signs of existence by means of wisdom; thus, they do not abide in any phenomenon, nor do they fall into any extreme. ^1-296

###### **མདའ་འཕང་པའི་དཔེས་མ་རྫོགས་པར་སྡོད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-3-2-4-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-297]]

Fourth, regarding the two verses beginning with "For example, an archer": for example, a person who has well-mastered the training of archery shoots an arrow equipped with a wooden board into the sky, and then, by shooting other arrows in succession that follow the first, they are joined together in a continuous chain; because they are held together in a connected way, the previous arrow does not find an opportunity to fall to the ground but is instead pushed upward. If those people were to desire to cease that effort and not shoot, those arrows would fall to the ground. In that same way, one who engages in the supreme perfection of wisdom does not, during the stage of the path, realize the supreme emptiness—the stainless expanse—in the intermediate state, as long as the roots of virtue that complete the perfection of ripening and purifying have not been perfected. This is because they engage through skillful means—such as non-conceptual wisdom, compassion, and dedication—and through miraculous powers, such as manifesting the body as one wishes, and they continue to accumulate merit until the task of fulfilling their aspirations is completely finished. ^1-297

###### **རྫུ་འཕྲུལ་གྱི་དཔེས་སྡོད་ཀྱང་གཞན་དོན་དུ་མི་སྐྱོ་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-3-2-5-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-298]]

Fifth, regarding the two verses beginning with "For example, a monk": for example, a monk who possesses the supreme power of miraculous display remains in the sky and, all at once, manifests various miraculous displays, showing himself walking, standing, lying down, and sitting in various ways; yet, through those various activities, his mind does not become weary, and there is no fatigue in his body. In that same way, a wise bodhisattva who abides in the meaning of emptiness, which is like the sky, and who has reached the perfection of wisdom and miraculous powers through skill in means, does not abide by way of grasping at any phenomenon whatsoever; yet, they manifest infinite, various activities of benefit and happiness for sentient beings that are appropriate to each of them, and even for immeasurable tens of millions of eons, they do not become weary in mind, nor is there any fatigue in their body. ^1-298

###### **གདུགས་འཛིན་པའི་དཔེས་མཐར་མི་ལྟུང་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-3-2-6-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-299]]

Sixth, regarding the two verses beginning with "For example, some people": for example, if some person sits at the edge of a great abyss and, holding two parasols in both hands, leaps into the sky, and if, having cast their body into the great abyss, they hold the parasols, then by the power of being lifted by the wind, they will not immediately fall to the bottom of that abyss, but for as long as the distance lasts, they will move slowly. In that same way, a bodhisattva who is wise regarding suchness and abides in compassion, having fully grasped the two parasols of skill and wisdom, remains [in the world]. In this way, by grasping the parasol of wisdom, they realize that phenomena are empty, signless, and wishless; and by grasping the parasol of skill, they do not touch the nirvana that is merely a one-sided peace, and they come to see all phenomena as they are. ^1-299

###### **དེད་དཔོན་གྱི་དཔེས་རང་གཞན་གྱི་ཕན་བདེ་བསྒྲུབ་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-3-2-7-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-300]]

Seventh, regarding the two verses beginning with "For example, precious things": for example, if a person of noble lineage, in order to provide for their relatives and others, goes to an island of precious things and, having found many precious things, returns to their own home, it is not the case that the merchant alone lives happily on what they have found while leaving their relatives and kin in unhappiness by giving them nothing at all; this should be understood as the analogy. Why? Just as in that analogy, when a bodhisattva goes to the island of the precious things of the meditative absorption of emptiness and attains immeasurable qualities—such as the joy of meditative stabilization, the five faculties, and the five powers—it is not the case that they alone, as one who has touched nirvana, take delight in that way while failing to benefit other sentient beings with those qualities, thereby leaving them in suffering; rather, it is for the sake of sentient beings that they accomplish those qualities. ^1-300

###### **ཚོང་པའི་དཔེས་ལམ་ལ་མཁས་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-3-2-8-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-301]]

Eighth, regarding the two verses beginning with "For example, seeking": for example, merchants who seek precious things, in order to become familiar with the path, travel from their own homes through the great cities, the towns—that is, the medium-sized cities—and the small villages that lie between them and the Island of Jewels. Yet, realizing that it is pointless to remain there, they do not dwell in those intermediate cities and the like. Furthermore, knowing that staying on the Island of Jewels does not benefit their own homes, they do not dwell on the Island of Jewels either. While traveling on the path, they become familiar with the path, and those who are wise in traversing it, without dwelling in their own homes, proceed along the path with diligence. ^1-301

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-302]]

In the same way, a bodhisattva who seeks the benefit and happiness of sentient beings, while training in the path of method and wisdom, becomes clear-minded regarding that method. In this manner, having become skilled in all the modes of the path of knowledge—that is, the wisdom and the resultant liberation—of the Śrāvakas and Pratyekabuddhas, who are like the intermediate cities and the like, they do not dwell in those inferior paths and results. Furthermore, they do not dwell in the Buddha’s wisdom, which is like the Island of Jewels, by way of being attached to it. Although they do not dwell in the unconditioned expanse of nirvāṇa, which is like their own home, by way of merely tasting its flavor, they should understand that, having become familiar with the modes of the three paths—those of the Śrāvakas, Pratyekabuddhas, and bodhisattvas—as if traversing the path for the time being, it is precisely by doing so that they perform the immeasurable benefit of sentient beings who belong to those respective lineages. ^1-302

###### **དོན་བསྡུ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-3-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-303]]

Third, regarding the summary of the meaning, there are two parts: the meaning and... ^1-303

###### **དོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-3-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-304]]

the two examples. First, the single verse beginning with "At the time when one goes..." [means]: At the time when a bodhisattva, having engaged in the path by way of being seized by great love for all sentient beings, practices the supreme samādhi that has the nature of emptiness, signlessness, and wishlessness, is it the case that they will attain only the unconditioned expanse of nirvāṇa, or is it that they can be designated as being one who belongs to the conditioned cycle of existence? Neither is the case. ^1-304

###### **དཔེ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-2-3-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-305]]

Second, the single verse beginning with "For example, a magical creation..." [means]: Although in this way one cannot be designated as belonging to either cyclic existence or peace, that bodhisattva will not in any way become someone who is unknown or unseen by anyone. For example, the body of a magically created person is not something that does not appear to anyone; it can be designated by a name, and in the world there are those who speak of its manner of being. One should understand it in that way. Just as, even though it is designated by a name, that magical creation does not become a human or any other type of sentient being, in the same way, a bodhisattva who practices by way of the three liberations, even though they do not abide in cyclic existence or peace, appears in the world and can also be designated by a name. 
Showing the difference between turning back and not turning back. ^1-305

###### **ཕྱིར་མི་ལྡོག་པ་དེའི་སྤྱོད་པའི་ཁྱད་པར་ཕྱིར་ལྡོག་པ་དང་མི་ལྡོག་པའི་ཁྱད་པར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-306]]

Third, regarding the difference between turning back and not turning back, there are two parts: a brief thematic summary and a detailed explanation.
Brief thematic summary: ^1-306

###### **མདོར་བསྟན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-307]]

First, the single verse beginning with "If the conduct..." [means]: If someone asks about the kind of conduct a bodhisattva must engage in to abide as a child of the Victorious One, and about the nature of the faculties—such as faith and wisdom—by which that person aspires to and contemplates the profound path, that bodhisattva does not fully reveal the profound dharma of emptiness and signlessness to be realized. Because they are unable to explain the signs, aspects, and so forth of the irreversible stage to one who is not yet capable of trusting in the nature of those with ripened faculties, one should understand that this is not a case of being prophesied as irreversible. For example, it is illustrated by the question: "How does a bodhisattva who wishes to attain awakening familiarize themselves with emptiness without making it manifest?" Because those who have not personally gained experience cannot explain the conduct, faculties, and signs of irreversibility when asked about them. Even so, the sutra states, "There are many who practice for awakening, but few who provide the answer." This is because only one who has personally realized the dharma of the irreversible path can provide an answer with a manner of absolute certainty when asked, and only then can one trust that "this person is irreversible because they have gained experience in such profound dharma"; otherwise, one cannot be certain that they are irreversible. ^1-307

###### **རྒྱས་པར་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-308]]

The detailed explanation: the second part consists of two sections, the signs of non-retrogression and the distinctions of being deceived by Māra. ^1-308

###### **ཕྱིར་མི་ལྡོག་པའི་རྟགས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-309]]

The signs of non-retrogression: The first section consists of three verses beginning with "the stage of an arhat." Because one has become certain of the Great Vehicle path, which is the union of method and wisdom, one does not even desire—let alone directly perceive—the stage of a śrāvaka arhat or the wisdom of a pratyekabuddha, which is called the "buddhahood of conditions," even in dreams.
