Furthermore, seeing in dreams or similar states that the buddhas are surrounded by an ocean of followers and that they teach the Dharma to beings is to be known as a prophecy that one will not retrogress from the unsurpassed enlightenment to a lesser path. Moreover, a bodhisattva endowed with a compassionate mind who sees in a dream the realms of the three lower states of existence will, in that very instant, make aspirations to sever the continuity of those lower states, and through that, they see those sufferings as pacified. Again, whether asleep or awake, upon seeing a city burning with fire, if that bodhisattva says, "If I have been prophesied as non-retrogressing, then by this truth and this word of truth, may this fire be pacified," the power of that truth—the blessing of truth—will cause the mass of fire to be pacified; this is to be known as a prophecy of non-retrogression. ^1-309

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-310]]

In the human world, through the expression of words of truth by one endowed with a compassionate heart—who wishes to benefit and protect from suffering those afflicted by various spirits, demons, and many illnesses—those afflictions are pacified by the blessing of truth. However, one should know that it is a sign of having attained the prophecy of non-retrogression that one does not become arrogant due to that power, thinking, "I am powerful because I have such ability," and that one does not give rise to the pride of thinking oneself superior. Thus, the signs are taught as the internal warmth of being certain of the path, the external condition of seeing buddhas in dreams and so forth, and the ability to manifest the power of internal qualities without any arrogance. 
The distinctions of being deceived by Māra. ^1-310

###### **བདུད་ཀྱིས་བསླུས་པའི་ཁྱད་པར།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-311]]

Regarding the second, there are two parts: the deeds of Māra, and the instruction to strive in the methods for abandoning them. ^1-311

###### **བདུད་ལས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-312]]

The deeds of Māra: regarding the first, there are three: the Māra of arrogance regarding qualities, the Māra arising from the basis of names, and the Māra of arrogance regarding seclusion. ^1-312

###### **ཡོན་ཏན་གྱིས་རློམ་པའི་བདུད།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-313]]

The first, "If that person," and so forth, is a single verse. "If that person" refers to a case where, due to the reason that various blessings of truth—such as the pacification of demons through the power of oneself or others—have been accomplished or attained, an arrogant thought arises, "I am one who has attained a prophecy because I possess such qualities." Or, if one gives rise to arrogance, thinking, "Other bodhisattvas will come to prophesy and speak of my fame," then one who dwells in such arrogance regarding the qualities of praise and fame should be known as someone who is immature, undeveloped, and susceptible to being deceived by Māra, because they possess that arrogance. In this case, the Māra that arises from the basis of blessings enters. ^1-313

###### **མིང་གི་གཞི་ལས་འོང་བའི་བདུད།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-314]]

The second, "the basis of names," and so forth, consists of two verses. The Māra of arrogance regarding the basis of names also arises; how is this? In this way, the sinful Māra, assuming any appearance such as that of a Buddha, approaches that person and speaks thus: "This is your name, and the names of your parents, and the names of your ancestors back through seven generations of your lineage." He speaks all those names, and when he has aligned his speech with what you have previously thought in your own mind, he says, "This is the name by which you will become a Buddha." He clearly states what that person is currently like, such as their dwelling in the qualities of ascetic practices like begging for alms and eating only one meal, their possession of pure ethical discipline, and their possession of yogic attainments such as reflection and meditation. He says, "In the past, your manner of qualities, such as ascetic practices, was also just like this one at present." When a bodhisattva hears those words of Māra and, relying on that basis or cause—the mere name of their own qualities and so forth—becomes arrogant, it should be known that such a bodhisattva has been completely incited by Māra and is of little intelligence. ^1-314

###### **དབེན་པས་རློམ་པའི་བདུད།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-1-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-315]]

The third section has two parts: the brief presentation and the detailed explanation. ^1-315

###### **མདོར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-1-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-316]]

The first section consists of one verse, beginning with "In complete isolation." Although a bodhisattva may indeed rely upon and dwell in any of the various places that are truly isolated—such as a village or great city where one does not encounter others, the hollows of mountains, wilderness areas far from villages, uninhabited secluded spots, or the midst of a forest—if they think, "The Tathāgata has highly praised dwelling in isolation, and I am one who dwells in isolation," and, becoming conceited through that basis of isolation, they praise themselves and disparage other bodhisattvas who do not dwell in isolation, then that bodhisattva should be known as one whose mental continuum has been entirely corrupted or agitated by Māra, and they should be recognized as having a narrow mind. ^1-316

###### **རྒྱས་པར་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-1-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-317]]

The second section has two parts: teaching the internal seclusion, and explaining the faults of not understanding that seclusion. ^1-317

###### **ནང་གི་དབེན་པ་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-1-3-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-318]]

The first section consists of one verse, beginning with "Always in a village." This means that one dwells in a village such as Videha, in a region such as Campā, or in a city such as Pāṭaliputra. Apart from ripening sentient beings there and striving for the path of enlightenment, that bodhisattva does not in the least desire the fruit of a Śrāvaka or a Pratyekabuddha, and such a state is never produced. This is what is taught as the true seclusion of the bodhisattvas, the sons of the Sugata; for merely being physically secluded does not make the mind secluded from defilements. When the internal mind is secluded from self-interested mental engagement and from mental engagement with the inferior path, then wherever one dwells, one is dwelling in the supreme seclusion. This is like the account in the *Ārya-ratnakāra-sūtra* where, in the past, the noble Mañjuśrī vowed to observe the summer retreat in the Jetavana, but instead of remaining there in the assembly of the Saṅgha, he ripened sentient beings while in the retinue of King Prasenajit’s queens. ^1-318

###### **དབེན་པ་དེ་མ་ཤེས་པའི་སྐྱོན་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-1-3-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-319]]

The second section has two parts: teaching the faults of being devoid of internal seclusion, and teaching the faults of disparaging those who possess internal seclusion. ^1-319

###### **ནང་གི་དབེན་པ་དང་བྲལ་བའི་སྐྱོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-1-3-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-320]]

The first section consists of one verse, beginning with "Whoever." This means that even if one dwells for many eons in a mountain ravine that is secluded from the coming and going of people—a place within five hundred leagues that is devoid of humans, filled with poisonous snakes, and impossible to inhabit—a bodhisattva who does not understand this true internal seclusion will, having acquired excessive pride, dwell in a state of being mixed with inferior mental engagement. The reason for this is that "seclusion" means the state of being unmixed; and if one asks, "With what must one not be mixed?" it is that the continuum of a bodhisattva must not be mixed with attachment to the self or with mental engagement in the inferior path. Therefore, not understanding such seclusion, merely being physically secluded while becoming arrogant and negligent in the work of benefiting sentient beings—that is not dwelling in seclusion, because the mind has become mixed with the discordant factors of the defilements. This is the meaning. ^1-320

###### **ནང་གི་དབེན་པ་ཅན་ལ་བརྙས་པའི་སྐྱོན་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-1-3-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-321]]

The second section has two parts: the actual subject, and teaching that because of that, it is difficult to gauge others. ^1-321

###### **དངོས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-1-3-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-322]]

The first section consists of one verse, beginning with "Bodhisattva." This refers to a bodhisattva who is endowed with diligence for the sake of sentient beings, and who, having attained the meditative concentrations of the four dhyānas, the five powers, the five faculties, and the meditative absorption of the three gateways to liberation, wanders through towns and cities, demonstrating various methods for the sake of ripening sentient beings. Regarding such a one, those who do not understand his profound intention and conduct think, "This one is not practicing in solitude or seclusion," and disparage him. The Victorious One has stated that such a person who disparages him is within the sphere of Māra, having come to dwell in a domain where Māra finds an opportunity. This is as stated in texts such as the *Sūtra Teaching the Absence of All Phenomena*, which contains the history of the monk who is a preacher of the Dharma, who possesses pure conduct, and who possesses the wisdom of conduct. ^1-322

###### **དེས་ན་གཞན་གྱི་ཚོད་དཔོག་དཀའ་བར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-1-3-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-323]]

The second section consists of one verse, beginning with "Whoever, in a town or..." This refers to a bodhisattva who, whether dwelling in a town or in a wilderness, has abandoned the mind of the two vehicles—the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas—and has become certain of the path to supreme enlightenment. This way of being is the true seclusion for bodhisattvas who have entered into the work of benefiting beings. Not understanding this, other bodhisattvas may judge such a bodhisattva, thinking, "This one does not dwell in seclusion," or similar assessments; such judging causes them to lose the qualities of attainment and non-attainment. Therefore, the sūtras state that, except for those like myself, one person should not judge another, and that a bodhisattva should examine his own continuum rather than seeking the faults of others. 
Striving for the means to abandon that. ^1-323

###### **བདུད་ཀྱི་ལས་སྤོང་བའི་ཐབས་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-324]]

The second section, explaining the means to abandon the activities of Māra, has two parts: a brief indication and a detailed explanation. ^1-324

###### **མདོར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-325]]

Brief indication: The first part consists of one verse, beginning with "Therefore..." Since many activities of Māra arise when one is seeking the path, one should rely on external and internal support in order to turn them back. What is this like? The one who relies is someone who possesses an intense aspiration to seek supreme enlightenment and who is skilled in the means to accomplish it. How should one rely? One should definitely subdue the pride in one's own continuum and, with great respect for a spiritual friend, rely on them without negligence, just as a group of patients would rely on a good doctor to cure their own illnesses. Even when listening to the Dharma, one should possess the four perceptions of a patient—regarding oneself as the patient, the Dharma speaker as the doctor, the Dharma as the medicine, and the earnest practice as the curing of the illness. Who should one rely on? A spiritual friend. In this context, "virtue" is the Mahāyāna Dharma, and one who teaches it well is a spiritual friend, for they are able to protect one from falling from the path by showing the correct path, identifying the obstacles of Māra's activities, and teaching the means to transcend them. Therefore, one should rely on such a person. ^1-325

###### **རྒྱས་པར་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-326]]

Detailed explanation: The second part consists of two sections: the explanation of the two types of external and internal support, and the specific explanation of internal support. ^1-326

###### **ཕྱི་ནང་གི་ཡོངས་འཛིན་གཉིས་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-327]]

Explanation of the two types of external and internal support: First, regarding the single verse beginning with "Buddha" and "enlightenment," if one asks who the spiritual friend to be relied upon is, it is as follows: one should rely as a spiritual friend upon the Buddhas, the Blessed Ones, the bodhisattvas who have entered into the supreme enlightenment, and the path of the perfection of wisdom. Furthermore, because those Buddhas and bodhisattvas instruct others in this Great Vehicle, they are called the "external support," and because this path of the perfection of wisdom taught by them is the ground or basis for experience and practice, it is called the "internal support." In this way, because these external and internal supports prevent one from falling away from the path of the supreme vehicle into other paths, by means of these two factors of relying on those two supports, one will swiftly realize the enlightenment of the Buddhas, for they constitute a perfect and complete cause that lacks nothing.
Specific explanation of internal support: ^1-327

###### **ནང་གི་ཡོངས་འཛིན་བྱེ་བྲག་ཏུ་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-328]]

The second part, the specific explanation of internal support, consists of two sections: the manner in which the perfection of wisdom is a spiritual friend, and the manner in which one should rely upon that spiritual friend. ^1-328

###### **ཤེར་ཕྱིན་བཤེས་གཉེན་ཡིན་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-329]]

The manner in which the perfection of wisdom is a spiritual friend: First, regarding the single verse beginning with "The Victors of the past" and so forth, one might wonder: although it is widely renowned that the Buddhas and bodhisattvas are spiritual friends, in what way does the perfection of wisdom become a spiritual friend? The path of all the Victors of the three times—those perfect Buddhas who have already passed away in the past, those who have not yet come and are future Buddhas, and those who, having attained Buddhahood, now reside in the ten directions—is this very perfection of wisdom, and there is no other path than this. It is impossible in every respect to attain Buddhahood without relying upon the perfection of wisdom, for it is certain that one cannot attain Buddhahood through any other path that lacks the wisdom that realizes the equality of existence and peace. The Victors have explained that this perfection of wisdom is the clarifier, the lamp, the light, and the supreme teacher for those who have entered into the path of supreme enlightenment. This is because, in sequence, it teaches the selflessness of persons, teaches the selflessness of phenomena, teaches all worldly and supramundane phenomena, and teaches the path of enlightenment of omniscience, which is the ultimate fruit.
The manner in which one should rely upon that spiritual friend: ^1-329

###### **བཤེས་གཉེན་དེ་ཇི་ལྟར་བསྟེན་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-330]]

Regarding the second, there are two parts: the nature of the perfection of wisdom to be relied upon, and the instruction that one must rely upon it. ^1-330

###### **བསྟེན་བྱ་ཤེར་ཕྱིན་གྱི་ངོ་བོ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-331]]

The nature of the perfection of wisdom to be relied upon. The first part has two sections: the actual nature, and dispelling the objection that it is untenable for everything to become purified in that way. ^1-331

###### **དངོས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-332]]

The first part, "How is it that wisdom, etc.," is a single verse. In the *Ārya-aṣṭasāhasrikā*, in response to a request for the defining characteristic of the perfection of wisdom, it is stated that "the perfection of wisdom has the defining characteristic of emptiness." By not teaching a conventional defining characteristic, it teaches the ultimate defining characteristic. Conventional modes of existence, such as the defining characteristic of form—which is "suitability as form"—are merely imputed by the mind and do not exist ultimately; they cannot withstand analysis. The true mode of existence of all phenomena is emptiness, and because this is the flawless way things are, it is taught in that manner. Here, too, it is the same: "The defining characteristic of the perfection of wisdom is emptiness." It is not only that the defining characteristic of that reality is emptiness, but one should also know that the defining characteristics of all these phenomena, such as form, are likewise empty, just like that perfection of wisdom. If one thoroughly knows that all phenomena designated as things are empty in their essence and devoid of the signs of apprehension, then that way of practicing is practicing the perfection of wisdom of the Sugatas. ^1-332

###### **ཀུན་བྱང་དུ་འགྱུར་བ་མི་འཐད་པའི་རྩོད་སྤང་།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-333]]

The second part consists of three sections: the actual meaning, its analogy, and the conclusion of what has been taught in that way. ^1-333

###### **དོན་དངོས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-334]]

The first part, "Sentient beings, thoroughly," is a single verse. In accordance with the meaning of the response given in the sutra to the question, "If all phenomena are isolated and empty, how can sentient beings be subject to affliction and purification?", it is explained that affliction and purification are tenable: Sentient beings, due to the power of having become possessed of improper thorough conceptualization, generate desire for the food of desirable qualities that increases that conceptualization. Because of the power of that desire, those who possess a mind attached to cyclic existence are like pigs moving in filthy mud; because they are always turning in cyclic existence, the aspect of affliction exists in terms of how things appear. Although it exists due to the power of being confused about how things appear, it has never existed that way in terms of the mode of existence; the two phenomena of "self" and "what belongs to self," as grasped by that thorough conceptualization, are not truly established and are empty, because they have been unborn from the beginning. Although there is no object, childish beings who are not wise grasp at what is non-existent as being existent; for example, it is like thinking that a knot has been tied in the sky. Therefore, while there is no affliction in the emptiness of the defining characteristic of all phenomena, there is affliction for those who are confused and do not grasp things according to their defining characteristic. ^1-334

###### **དེ་ཡི་དཔེ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-335]]

Second, there is the verse beginning with "For example, the apprehension of doubt." If one wonders how the conceptualization of grasping at an object arises when it does not exist in terms of its own reality: for example, when a person, through the perception of doubt, is stirred by poison—thinking, "The poison has entered my body"—a conceptualization of poison arises vividly. Even though, in reality, the poison has not entered their body, due to the power of that conceptualization, they feel as if they are suffering from poison and, out of fear, may even faint. This is like a timid person who trembles merely because someone threatens them in the manner of a spirit or says, "An enemy is killing you." Just as fainting and the like occur because one’s own conceptualization grasps at the poison even though it has not entered the body, in the same way, because foolish, unlearned ordinary beings are habituated to grasping at a self that does not exist and to claiming as "mine" those phenomena that belong to their own continuum, there arises a conceptualization that is not correct, which perceives an object called "self." Due to the power of that, there arises the illusory appearance of experiencing a cycle of birth and death in existence. ^1-335

###### **དེ་ལྟར་བསྟན་པའི་མཇུག་བསྡུ་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-1-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-336]]

Third, there is the verse beginning with "Just as one grasps." It teaches that just as one grasps at the existence of a self and what belongs to a self, even though they do not exist in terms of their actual state, one becomes thoroughly afflicted due to the power of one's own conceptualization. Conversely, it states that if one does not perceive a self or what belongs to a self in terms of the actual state of reality, that is complete purification. Although it is the case that thorough affliction and complete purification occur in this way conventionally, in this ultimate state of reality, there is no person who becomes afflicted, nor is there any thorough affliction that becomes complete purification; the bodhisattva realizes the perfection of wisdom, which is the primordial equality of all things. (Instruction on the necessity of relying upon that perfection of wisdom.) ^1-336

###### **ཤེར་ཕྱིན་དེ་བསྟེན་དགོས་པར་གདམས་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-337]]

Second, regarding the instruction on the necessity of relying upon that perfection of wisdom, there are three parts: the instruction to apply oneself to that Dharma by way of its necessity, the way in which one should apply oneself by way of examples, and the demonstration of the benefits of having applied oneself in that way. ^1-337

###### **དགོས་པའི་སྒོ་ནས་ཆོས་དེ་ལ་བརྩོན་པར་གདམས་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-338]]

Regarding the first, there are three: the accomplishment of great merit through minor deeds, becoming a field of merit for all beings, and becoming a guide who shows the path to those to be tamed. ^1-338

###### **བསོད་ནམས་ཆེན་པོ་བྱ་བ་ཅུང་ཟད་ཀྱིས་འགྲུབ་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-339]]

First, regarding the two verses beginning with "In the Jambudvīpa," to provide an example of the "ultimate examination," if all the sentient beings existing in the Jambudvīpa were to obtain human bodies without exception, and if all of them were to generate the mind set upon supreme, unsurpassable enlightenment, and for many billions of thousands of years were to perform vast generosity toward the Buddhas and all sentient beings, and were to dedicate all those roots of virtue as a cause for supreme enlightenment for the sake of all beings, then although there would be immeasurable benefits in that, if one were to apply oneself earnestly to the supreme perfection of wisdom—never mind for a long time, but even for just a single day—by acting in accordance with that perfection of wisdom, such as by contemplating it oneself and teaching it to others, then the merit of that cannot even be compared to a fraction of the merit of the aforementioned heap of generosity. Therefore, one must apply oneself to this perfection of wisdom at all times without negligence. ^1-339

###### **འགྲོ་བ་ཀུན་གྱི་སྦྱིན་གནས་སུ་འགྱུར་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-340]]

Second, regarding the single verse beginning with "Perfection of wisdom," those yogins who practice the supreme perfection of wisdom, even while generating great compassion for all realms of beings, do not fall into the perception of sentient beings. At the time when they possess such non-conceptual compassion, those wise ones become the supreme field of merit for all beings, and their activities, such as begging for alms in the various regions, are always meaningful and fully utilized, because there is great, profound benefit in what is offered to them. ^1-340

###### **གདུལ་བྱ་ལ་ལམ་སྟོན་པ་པོར་འགྱུར་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-1-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-341]]

Third, regarding the single verse beginning with "Which bodhisattva," any bodhisattva who wishes to liberate from bondage and suffering the sentient beings of the five destinies—namely gods, humans, and the three lower realms—who have been connected as fathers, mothers, children, and so forth for a long time in this place of samsara, and who wishes to show to the realm of sentient beings the shore beyond samsara, which is nirvana itself, by relying upon the vast path—that is, the path from which the three awakenings arise and which opens the opportunity for immeasurable sentient beings—should exert themselves day and night in the perfection of wisdom. By exerting themselves in that, they become a teacher who speaks of the immeasurable qualities of a buddha. ^1-341

###### **དཔེའི་སྒོ་ནས་ཇི་ལྟར་བརྩོན་པར་བྱ་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-342]]

Second, regarding how to exert oneself, there are two verses beginning with "Just as a person." For example, if a person were to find a supreme jewel that they had never found before—that is, had never previously encountered—at some other time, they would be extremely joyful. If, immediately after finding it, they were to become heedless and lose that jewel, then because they had lost something so difficult to find, they would constantly suffer while longing for that jewel in their mind. In that same way, for those who have entered the path of supreme awakening, one must not abandon the practice of yoga in this perfection of wisdom, which is like the splendor of a jewel that is difficult to find. For example, if, after losing it, one were to find that jewel again, take it up with joy, and exert oneself greatly to wrap it in one's clothing or the like, hiding it so it would not be wasted, and then—fearing that it might be lost or stolen by another—quickly go to one's own home, then all the grief of being separated from the jewel and the suffering of poverty would be pacified. ^1-342

###### **དེ་ལྟར་བརྩོན་པར་བྱས་པའི་ཕན་ཡོན་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-343]]

Third, regarding the benefits of exerting oneself in that way, there are five: outshining other practices, becoming a refuge for gods and humans, defeating the māras, gathering all the perfections, and the benefit of others rejoicing in one's merit. ^1-343

###### **བསྒྲུབ་པ་གཞན་ཟིལ་གྱིས་གནོན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-344]]

First, regarding the two verses beginning with "The sun, clouds, and so forth," for example, when the sun, free from clouds, rises and destroys the dense darkness—the web of light rays—as well as all other darkness, it outshines the four types of light: the firefly, which has only a faint light on one part of its body; the light of living beings and spirits, which illuminates only its immediate surroundings by the power of its own body's light, torches, and so forth; the slight illumination of the collection of stars; and the general illumination of the moonlight. Similarly, the bodhisattva who practices the supreme perfection of wisdom, like the sun free from the clouds of obscuration, is skilled in practicing emptiness and signlessness. By radiating the supreme web of the light rays of the Dharma, they destroy the dense darkness of viewing self and phenomena without remainder. They outshine the many beings who are like fireflies, the śrāvakas who are like other living beings, the pratyekabuddhas who are like stars, and other bodhisattvas who are like the moon, all of whom lack the skillful means of this perfection of wisdom. Thus, through the gateway of wisdom, they are superior to all other practices that do not possess this. ^1-344

###### **ལྷ་མིའི་རེ་གནས་སུ་གྱུར་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-345]]

Second, regarding the two verses beginning with "For example, a king's son," for example, a king's son who gives away wealth to protect his subjects and who, being skilled in the treatises on statecraft, desires the purpose of fully protecting the realm—that is, he is one who understands purpose and possesses a nature endowed with purpose, not one who is purposeless—becomes the foremost of all the heirs of the Victorious One. All the subjects rely upon him and hold him in high regard, and because they hope for his benefit, he becomes worthy of being approached. If this prince, even at a time when he has not yet become king, makes many sentient beings happy, then it goes without saying that once he has been empowered on the throne, has had the crown bound upon him, and has attained the means of power over the kingdom, he will act for the benefit of the subjects and make them happy as a king established in the sovereignty of the realm. Just like that example, a bodhisattva who is skilled in practicing the perfection of wisdom and is endowed with purpose also bestows the nectar of the Dharma upon sentient beings and makes gods and humans happy, so that the gods and others come into his presence, make him their refuge, and honor him. If this bodhisattva, even now in the stage of training on the path, strives to benefit many sentient beings, then it goes without saying that once he has become a Dharma king, has become established in the wisdom of a buddha, and acts for the benefit of sentient beings, he will make them happy. ^1-345

###### **བདུད་ཕམ་པར་བྱེད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-346]]

Regarding the third point, there are two aspects: how one strikes terror into the Māras, and how one is not harmed by the Māras. ^1-346

###### **བདུད་རྣམས་སྐྲག་པར་བྱེད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-347]]

First, regarding the verse beginning with "At that time," there is one stanza. When a bodhisattva who is endowed with the essential points of the perfection of wisdom practices in that way, at that time, the sinful Māras of the trichiliocosm think as follows: "This bodhisattva will certainly transcend my domain, and once he attains buddhahood, my domain will become empty." Because of this, they become filled with mental anguish and distress. Thinking, "It is difficult for me to suppress him with my power," they become overwhelmed by grief. Due to this, their bodies suffer, their minds become uneasy, and, being suppressed by the qualities of the bodhisattva, they become discouraged, lacking any pride in their own power. Because they have become like this, those Māras, unable to bear it, think, "How can I cause this bodhisattva to turn back and retreat from the path of omniscience?" and, thinking that they might find an opportunity to disturb even a single moment of his omniscient mind, they manifest various demonic illusions, such as burning the directions with fire and causing meteors to clash, in order to display great terror to that bodhisattva. ^1-347

###### **བདུད་ཀྱིས་དེ་ལ་མི་ཚུགས་པའི་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-348]]

Regarding the second point, there are two aspects: the manner in which they find no opportunity, and, incidentally, the explanation of the causes for finding an opportunity for the opposing side. ^1-348

###### **གླགས་མི་རྙེད་པའི་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-3-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-349]]

First, regarding the verse beginning "When the wise," there is one stanza. When the display of the māra’s magical illusions occurs in that manner, those wise bodhisattvas possess an extremely intense, unwavering intention toward the path of the Great Vehicle, and through the gateway of application, they constantly, day and night, look toward the meaning of the supreme perfection of wisdom. At the time when they act in that way, the body and mind of those bodhisattvas move without fear of the abyss of existence and peace, like a bird moving through the sky, due to the power of abiding in the non-abiding perfection of wisdom. Therefore, how could the black forces find an opportunity against them? It is just like how dogs and the like cannot reach a bird moving through the sky. ^1-349

###### **ཞར་ལ་ལྡོག་ཕྱོགས་གླགས་རྙེད་པའི་རྒྱུ་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-3-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-350]]

Regarding the second point, there are two parts: the explanation that the primary cause for finding an opportunity is conflict, and the explanation of the methods for avoiding that fault. ^1-350

###### **གླགས་རྙེད་པའི་རྒྱུ་གཙོ་བོ་རྩོད་པ་ཡིན་པ་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-3-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-351]]

Regarding the first point, there are two parts: the general nature of conflict, and the specific disadvantages of engaging in conflict with a bodhisattva who has attained a prophecy, as opposed to one who has not. ^1-351

###### **རྩོད་པ་སྤྱི།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-3-2-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-352]]

First, regarding the two verses beginning "When the bodhisattva," as explained above, although Māra cannot find an opportunity against a bodhisattva who possesses the armor of skillful means and wisdom, he does find an opportunity against those of lesser intellect who do not possess that method. Regarding the final point of the seven faults of finding an opportunity mentioned in the *Aṣṭasāhasrikā* and the twelve mentioned in the *Śatasāhasrikā*, what is taught here is: at the time when any bodhisattvas engage in physical fighting and verbal quarreling with one another, and become possessed of minds of anger, being in discord with one another, at that time, that sinful Māra rejoices, and the more the conflict increases, the more he becomes supremely satisfied. The reason for this is that because of the fault of that conflict, which is contrary to the path of skillful means and wisdom, those two bodhisattvas become far removed from the wisdom of the Victors, and he thinks, "I have found an opportunity." In that way, both of them become far removed from the wisdom of the Buddha, and their mental continuums become unrefined, resembling flesh-eating spirits, hungry ghosts, and the like. Furthermore, both the one who is angry and the one who retaliates against that anger are causing their own vows—to attain complete awakening out of compassion for the sake of others and to practice the path of the perfections—to degenerate through conflict, because conflict born of anger is in every way contrary to compassion, the mind of awakening, and the wisdom that perceives selflessness. How could there be attainment of awakening for one who is hateful and devoid of the antidote, patience? It is like a sprout from a burnt seed. At the time when they have thus fallen into conflict, the Māras, together with those who belong to their faction, rejoice, because things turn out according to their intentions. Therefore, the way in which a moment of anger is a greater fault than the fault of attachment that has arisen over eons for a bodhisattva is explained extensively in the sūtras because it is in every way contrary to compassion; in particular, anger toward a bodhisattva as an object is a greater fault. As stated in the *Ratnakūṭa* discourse called "The Lion’s Roar of Maitreya": "One should distance oneself by a hundred leagues from a place involving conflict born of hatred; one should not give rise to a mind of hatred. Compared to abusing, beating, and attacking with weapons all the sentient beings in a billion-fold world system, it is a far greater fault to give rise to a single thought of malice or hatred toward another bodhisattva; by this, a bodhisattva is wounded and impaired. Just as iron cannot be cut by anything other than iron, the roots of virtue of a bodhisattva are exhausted by hatred toward another bodhisattva, and cannot be exhausted by anything else. Therefore, one should treat one another with respect and reverence, and one should generate the perception of a teacher even toward a bodhisattva who has just generated the mind for the first time." ^1-352

###### **བྱེ་བྲག་ཏུ་ལུང་བསྟན་མ་ཐོབ་པས་ཐོབ་པའི་བྱང་སེམས་ལ་རྩོད་པའི་ཉེས་དམིགས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-3-2-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-353]]

The second section consists of one verse, beginning with "not having received a prophecy." Specifically, if a bodhisattva who has not received a prophecy of non-retrogression becomes angry at a bodhisattva who has received the sign of a prophecy and engages in physical or verbal dispute, and if they do not abandon that dispute by letting go of that mind through a manner of remorse and making confession, then for as many moments as that mind—which is like a blight, possessing the fault of being an unwholesome continuum where the seeds of virtue do not increase—is generated, for that many eons they must don the armor of entering the path anew. It is said that because they are born in hell for as many eons as that faulty, disputatious mind was generated, and because they have exhausted the roots of virtue accumulated over those same eons through anger and increased their negativity to that extent, they do not regain the path until they have donned the armor of training in all aspects of the path, such as generosity, from the very beginning for the number of eons corresponding to those moments; thus, they distance themselves from unsurpassed enlightenment for that long. ^1-353

###### **ཉེས་པ་དེ་ལས་སྡང་བའི་ཐབས་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-3-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-354]]

The second section, explaining the method for purifying those faults, consists of two parts: confession through the complete four powers in terms of conventional reality, and confession through viewing them as non-arising in terms of ultimate reality. ^1-354

###### **ཀུན་རྫོབ་ཏུ་སྟོབས་བཞི་ཚང་བའི་སྒོ་ནས་བཤགས་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-3-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-355]]

First, regarding the verse beginning "The Buddhas," there is one stanza: "The Buddhas have attained awakening through the perfection of wisdom; my own angry mind, which is the opposite of this, is not good, for it is of a nature that burns away the roots of virtue accumulated over many eons and is greatly contrary to the path of awakening." By thinking in this way, one generates the mindfulness of knowing a fault as a fault, and feeling supreme remorse for the misdeed that has been committed is the power of repudiation. For the sake of confessing that entity of misdeed individually, the power of support is to mentally visualize the Buddhas and bodhisattvas of the ten directions and rely upon them. The power of the antidote is to engage in any of the sequences of Dharma practice, such as reading profound sūtras, reciting dhāraṇīs, or resting in equipoise on the profound meaning. The power of restoration is to refrain from committing such faults again in the future, and by thinking, "Such unwholesome behavior is not fitting for me, a bodhisattva," to not take delight in the nature of conflict and the like through a state of mind that has turned away from them; this is training in the Buddha’s Dharma, not training in the ways of Māra. ^1-355

###### **དོན་དམ་པར་མི་དམིགས་པར་ལྟ་བས་བཤགས་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-3-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-356]]

Regarding the second, there are two parts: the actual subject and the benefits of knowing it in that way. ^1-356

###### **དངོས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-3-2-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-357]]

First, regarding the verse beginning with "When training in whatever," there is one stanza. When training in the path, one does not acknowledge any activity whatsoever called "training" through a mode of grasping at it. Furthermore, one does not perceive the "trainer" as a self, nor does one perceive the "phenomena to be trained in," which are the perfections. One does not conceptualize these two—the antidote "training" and its contrary "not training"—but rather knows all phenomena to be of the nature of being unperceivable. Whoever trains in this way is training unerringly in this cause of omniscience, the perfection of wisdom, which is the Buddha's teaching. ^1-357

###### **དེ་ལྟར་ཤེས་པའི་ཕན་ཡོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-3-2-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-358]]

Regarding the second, there are two stanzas beginning with "Bodhisattva." Any bodhisattva who knows this "training of not training" will never, at any time, decline from the training in wisdom and meditative stabilization, nor will they become one who violates discipline. This is because they have abandoned the view that possesses the grasping of the causes of faults, just as the fruit of a poisonous plant does not grow when the seed of the poison is absent. Such a bodhisattva trains in this profound path of wisdom in order to attain or accomplish the Buddha’s qualities, such as the powers. It also appears in the commentary that this is for the sake of abiding in the phenomena that please or delight the Buddhas. That bodhisattva increases the training in mind and discipline, and is skilled in the training of wisdom; the reason for this is that they abide in the path of non-grasping, for they think that if one possesses grasping, one will not become one who has the higher training, due to being obscured by the view of a self and so forth. Thus, a bodhisattva who is skilled in the two truths should train in this way in the wisdom of those who are the light-makers of the teachings. When training in this way, they do not produce even a single moment of a non-virtuous mind that possesses conceptualizations of afflictions, which are contrary to the path of omniscience. Because their own continuum is pervaded by the light of profound wisdom, there is no opportunity for the darkness of evil conceptualizations to abide, just as darkness can never abide in the space in front of one that is being completely overcome by the rays of the sun moving through the sky. ^1-358

###### **ཕར་ཕྱིན་ཀུན་སྡུད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-4-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-359]]

The fourth part has two sections: how one comes to train in all the perfections, and how one comes to train in all the vehicles. ^1-359

###### **ཕར་ཕྱིན་ཀུན་ལ་བསླབ་པར་འགྱུར་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-4-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-360]]

The first part has two sections: the analogy that serves as the root, and the analogy that serves as the essence. ^1-360

###### **རྩ་བར་གྱུར་པའི་དཔེ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-4-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-361]]

The first part consists of one verse on the perfection of wisdom and so forth. In this way, for those who have trained in the perfection of wisdom, all the perfections, such as generosity, are gathered into this perfection of wisdom, which is their own basis of training; there is nothing at all that is not included within its scope. Why is that? Because the path of the perfection of wisdom is truly expert in the mode of the two truths. By means of this wisdom, one eliminates the discordant factors of the perfections, such as stinginess, and by means of skill in means, one does not abandon the result, great enlightenment. Furthermore, because there is no conceptualization of the three spheres, all paths become the essence of the single perfection of wisdom, which possesses all supreme aspects, and all of them are made of one taste within the result, the omniscience that is the wisdom of emptiness possessing all supreme aspects. Therefore, if this perfection of wisdom exists, then the training in all the perfections is present without being incomplete, for all of them arise in dependence upon this root. For example, the sixty-two views spoken of in the Brahmajāla Sūtra—the eighteen views based on the past, such as the four eternalist views, and the forty-four views based on the future, such as those asserting the existence or non-existence of consciousness and the annihilationist views—all of these are based on grasping a self as the basis of characteristics and then investigating the various attributes of that self. Thus, the view of the self or the view of the transitory collection is what contains those sixty-two views; they arise in dependence upon that basis and are merely the differentiation of its characteristics, and if that were absent, all the others would not exist either. Having understood it in that way, it is taught that, just like that analogy, all these perfections are gathered into this perfection of wisdom. ^1-361

###### **སྙིང་པོར་གྱུར་པའི་དཔེ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-4-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-362]]

Second, the verse beginning with "For example, the life-faculty..." constitutes a single stanza. Just as, even if one possesses many faculties such as the eye, if the life-faculty that resides in this world ceases, then all other faculties belonging to this life, however many there may be, will also cease; in that same way, it is taught that for a supreme wise one, a bodhisattva, who practices the perfection of wisdom, all other perfections—the trainings of the bodhisattvas—are included within this perfection of wisdom. If this is absent, none of those others arise, whereas if this is present, all of them become perfections that transcend the world and lead to the attainment of buddhahood. If one practices generosity and so forth without being connected to the realization of selflessness, one cannot even be liberated from cyclic existence, so how could there be any basis for them to become the perfections of the path to great enlightenment? ^1-362

###### **ཐེག་པ་ཀུན་ལ་བསླབ་པར་འགྱུར་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-4-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-363]]

Second, the section on training in all vehicles is a single stanza beginning with "The bodhisattva who is skilled..." A bodhisattva who is skilled in the perfection of wisdom becomes skilled in the three vehicles. Just as the analogy of a single load containing a full measure of grain illustrates, if one knows this perfection of wisdom—the supreme path that perfectly completes the aspects of method and wisdom—then the lesser method and wisdom of the śrāvaka and pratyekabuddha paths are known as a corollary. Therefore, it is taught that the path of the three vehicles is extensively revealed through this perfection of wisdom. It is also taught that those who wish to train in the stage of the śrāvaka must train in this; just as a great king’s treasury contains various kinds of wealth, so that even the items needed by those of lesser status can be obtained there, the path and its fruits for those who have entered the path of self-interest alone can also be thoroughly understood as a corollary to this. Although this is the case, those bodhisattvas train without confusion in all the qualities that are to be trained in by the śrāvakas, and likewise in all the qualities of training of the pratyekabuddhas, and they guide those of those respective lineages through that very approach. Yet, those bodhisattvas do not themselves abide in those lesser paths, nor do they even give rise to a desire for them; rather, they think, "This approach is something I must train in, because the realization of the selflessness of persons and so forth is also accepted here in the same way, and I must know the aspects of the lesser path—which are characterized by limited method and wisdom—in order to transcend them and to be fit to teach them to others of those lineages." For that reason, they train in all vehicles. ^1-363

###### **དེའི་དགེ་བ་ལ་གཞན་གྱིས་ཡི་རང་བའི་ཕན་ཡོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-5-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-364]]

The fifth section has two parts: the actual subject and the reason for it. ^1-364

###### **དངོས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-5-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-365]]

The first section consists of one verse, beginning with "non-returning" and "supreme enlightenment." In this way, exerting oneself in the perfection of wisdom is a merit of immeasurable quality; let alone the merit to be accumulated by the one who exerts themselves, even for others who rejoice in that exertion, a great merit is obtained. Why is this? If a person, a bodhisattva, has entered into the practice of the perfection of wisdom through intense intention and application toward the supreme enlightenment of non-returning, and another person, with sincere intention, rejoices in that person’s generation of bodhicitta, then the merit of that rejoicing cannot be measured, even if one were to weigh all the Mount Merus existing in this great trichiliocosm on a scale. ^1-365

###### **དེའི་རྒྱུ་མཚན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-3-2-3-2-2-2-2-2-2-2-3-5-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-366]]

The second section consists of one verse, beginning with "seeking virtue." Why is it that the merit is so great? By rejoicing in the virtue of that bodhisattva alone, one indirectly rejoices in the entire mass of merit of all those in the world who seek their own virtue and all those sentient beings who desire to benefit others. Why is this? Because the roots of virtue of a bodhisattva serve the purpose of all sentient beings. Therefore, those irreversible bodhisattvas, having attained the qualities of the Victorious One, the Guide, through that path, and in order to permanently exhaust the many types of suffering, bestow the gift of Dharma throughout the entire world. By doing so, they fulfill the intentions of those who seek virtue and those who desire to benefit others. This is similar to how, if one carefully nurtures a great medicinal tree, although one is directly cultivating the tree, it indirectly serves the purpose of nurturing many living beings. ^1-366

###### **དེ་ལྟར་འཇུག་པའི་གང་ཟག་གི་ཆེ་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-4-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-367]]

The greatness of the individual who engages in this way. Regarding the fourth enumeration, the greatness of the individual, there are three parts: the attainment of non-conceptual conduct, the analogy showing that being non-conceptual and performing activities are not contradictory, and the qualities of having attained such realization. ^1-367

###### **མི་རྟོག་པའི་སྤྱོད་པ་ཐོབ་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-4-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-368]]

Regarding the first, there are two parts: the meaning and the analogy. ^1-368

###### **དོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-4-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-369]]

The first part consists of one verse beginning with "The bodhisattva..." A bodhisattva who abides in non-conceptualization and, having fully understood all phenomena included within the entirety of the purified and the defiled as empty, signless, and without elaboration or aspiration, does not seek enlightenment through a wisdom that conceptualizes in the manner of the defiled and the purified, the grasped and the grasper, or the near and the far, which is to say, a wisdom that grasps at the two extremes. That yogin, who is free from the conceptualization of grasping at duality, is one who strives for the supreme perfection of wisdom. ^1-369

###### **དཔེ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-4-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-370]]

Regarding the second, there is one verse beginning with "The space element..." The manner of that state, which is free from all conceptualization such as grasping at enlightenment as near or far, is, for example, that the space element does not possess both its own intrinsic nature and that which is contradictory to space, which cannot abide together. This is because space has been devoid of an intrinsic nature from the very beginning, and therefore it is not attained by any phenomenon whatsoever. That which has no attained self-nature is neither near to any phenomenon nor contradictory to any, and thus there is no distance. Just as in that analogy, all phenomena are of the nature of enlightenment, and enlightenment, in the manner of space, is not something that can be newly attained by anyone in the ultimate sense. A bodhisattva who is skilled in practicing the profound perfection of wisdom, which abides in the equality that has existed from the beginning without being near to or far from anyone, is also like space and possesses the greatness of practicing the complete pacification of all conceptualization. ^1-370

###### **མི་རྟོག་ཀྱང་བྱ་བ་བྱེད་པ་མི་འགལ་བའི་དཔེ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-4-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-371]]

Regarding the second, there are three points: not conceptualizing the object as a sentient being through the analogy of a magical illusion; not conceptualizing the agent through the analogy of an emanation; and not conceptualizing the result through the analogy of a carpenter’s craft. ^1-371

###### **སྒྱུ་མའི་སྐྱེས་བུའི་དཔེས་ཡུལ་སེམས་ཅན་དུ་མི༷་རྟོག་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-4-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-372]]

Regarding the first, there are two verses beginning with "In the midst of the crowd..." If one asks what the nature of the bodhisattva’s benefit for sentient beings is, once they have attained such a non-conceptual sphere of activity, it is explained by way of an analogy. For example, in the midst of a crowd gathered for a spectacle, a magical illusion—that is, a person appearing through the power of magical mantras and the like—does not think, "By this appearance of a deity or similar form, I shall delight this assembly of many people." Yet, that magical person does perform the task of delighting the eyes of the spectators; for instance, they display various miraculous movements and the like. Even though the crowd sees this, that magical person has no body composed of parts, nor do they have a mind. Thus, for that which is empty of a basis of designation, there is no established name either. Similarly, bodhisattvas who are non-conceptual and who practice the profound perfection of wisdom, which is the meaning of non-apprehension, never think, "Having realized the great enlightenment, I shall liberate all beings." Nevertheless, they appear in the world in various births and possess many activities for training those to be trained. Just as the magical illusion appears even though it does not exist, they act in a manner that is non-conceptual regarding this and that. ^1-372

###### **སྤྲུལ་པའི་དཔེས་བྱེད་པ་པོར་མི་རྟོག་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-4-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-373]]

Second, the verse "For example, the Buddha..." constitutes one stanza. Just as the Tathāgata performs the deeds of Śāntamati, the Buddhas perform all the deeds of a Buddha, such as teaching the Dharma, through emanations (nirmāṇa) that they manifest as Buddhas. Although they perform these deeds, the emanation itself does not give rise to even the slightest pride, thinking, "I am performing the deeds of a Buddha," because it is merely an appearance of an emanation. Similarly, a bodhisattva who is a learned one (paṇḍita), having familiarized themselves with the practice of the perfection of wisdom (prajñāpāramitā) and attained conviction in the realization that all phenomena are by nature empty and appear like an illusion, and who has attained the sphere of activity of the non-conceptual, does not give rise to conceptualization even while demonstrating all activities, such as teaching the Dharma, just like an emanation or an illusion. ^1-373

###### **ཤིང་མཁན་གྱི་བཟོའི་དཔེས་འབྲས་བུ་ལ་མི་རྟོག་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-4-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-374]]

Third, the verse "For example, a woodworker..." constitutes one stanza. When a woodworker skilled in their craft creates a figure resembling a man or a woman, that created man or woman performs all activities, such as walking, yet does not conceptualize the result of their actions—thinking, "I have done this"—because they lack a mind. Similarly, a bodhisattva who is skilled in practicing the non-conceptual perfection of wisdom (prajñāpāramitā) also does not conceptualize anything, yet while abiding in non-conceptual primordial wisdom (nirvikalpa-jñāna), they perform all the activities of perfecting, ripening, and purifying. This is because all phenomena are primordially empty by their own nature and appear like an illusion; when the clarity of the mind that has familiarized itself with this reaches its limit, all phenomena appear only as having that nature, and thus, like an illusory person, non-conceptualization arises. That they demonstrate various spontaneously accomplished deeds through the power of that non-conceptual primordial wisdom should be understood as being like a wish-fulfilling jewel. One should know that this non-conceptual primordial wisdom is present as a corresponding likeness during the stage of the path of training, and is fully perfected at the stage of a Buddha. ^1-374

###### **དེ་ལྟར་རྟོགས་པ་ཐོབ་པའི་ཡོན་ཏན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-4-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-375]]

Third, there are two parts: the qualities of being worshipped by the gods and the qualities of vanquishing the māras. ^1-375

###### **ལྷ་རྣམས་ཀྱིས་འདུད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-4-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-376]]

First, there is one verse beginning with "skilled in conduct." Those who are skilled in practicing non-conceptual conduct are worshipped by many hosts of worldly gods, such as Brahmā and Indra, who join their palms and bow their bodies with great reverence. Furthermore, the buddhas—the gods of gods—who reside in the buddhafields of the ten directions also proclaim the vast, continuous garlands of praise for the qualities of that bodhisattva who has attained the domain of non-conceptual conduct. This is just as, at present, the buddhas proclaim the praises of Mañjuśrī and Samantabhadra. ^1-376

###### **བདུད་ཕམ་པར་བྱེད་པའི་ཡོན་ཏན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-4-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-377]]

Second, there are two parts: the actual [qualities] and the reason for them. ^1-377

###### **དངོས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-4-3-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-378]]

First, there is one verse beginning with "equal to the Ganges." If one were to take all the sentient beings in as many buddhafields as there are grains of sand in the great river Ganges and, for the sake of illustration, transform them all into māras, and if from each individual pore of each of those māras one were to emanate as many māras again, and if they were to seek an opportunity to harm that bodhisattva who has attained non-conceptual wisdom, even all of them together would be unable to cause even the slightest obstruction to that skilled one, not even enough to move a single pore. ^1-378

###### **དེའི་རྒྱུ་མཚན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-4-3-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-379]]

Second, there is one verse beginning with "by the four causes." If one asks why it is that [the bodhisattva] cannot be obstructed in that way, it is because, by means of the four causes, a bodhisattva who is skilled in means and possesses the power of realizing the nature of phenomena is difficult for the four māras, such as the devaputramāra, to overcome, and they are unable to divert him from the path. What are these four causes? They are: wisdom whose intellect rests upon the meaning of emptiness; compassion that never abandons sentient beings; a firm vow to act without turning back, just as one previously made a promise and spoke the words of a solemn pledge, such as "I shall accomplish unsurpassed enlightenment for the sake of all sentient beings"; and the fact that, having fully accumulated the two accumulations, the buddhas are pleased and, through the nature of phenomena, the blessings of the sugatas have entered their continuum. Thus, by way of these four causes, he is able to defeat all māras. ^1-379

###### **དེས་བསླབ་བྱ་ཆོས་ཀྱི་ཆེ་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-5-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-380]]

The fifth section of the root outline, concerning the greatness of the dharma to be practiced, consists of three points: the greatness of entering the single path of the fruit, the greatness of being the supreme among those who abide on the path, and the greatness of being the supreme among all objects of knowledge and training. ^1-380

###### **འབྲས་བུ་བགྲོད་གཅིག་ལམ་དུ་ཚུད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-5-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-381]]

First, there is one verse beginning with "the mother of the tathāgatas." When this perfection of wisdom, which is the mother of all tathāgatas, is being explained, any bodhisattva who has heartfelt devotion to this method and who, with sincere intention, exerts himself in practicing or accomplishing it, is a person of composure whose three doors are peaceful and disciplined; such a bodhisattva should be known as one who has entered into the way of omniscience. This is because it is the sole path, non-dual, that leads to the fruit of omniscient wisdom. ^1-381

###### **ལམ་གནས་པ་རྣམས་ཀྱི་མཆོག་ཏུ་གྱུར་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-5-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-382]]

The second point, concerning being the supreme among those who abide on the path, consists of two parts: identifying the perfection of wisdom as the abiding state of the bodhisattva, and demonstrating that this very state is the supreme among all other abiding states. ^1-382

###### **བྱང་སེམས་ཀྱི་གནས་པ་ཤེར་ཕྱིན་ངོས་བཟུང་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-5-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-383]]

First, there are two verses beginning with "the sphere of reality, suchness." If one asks what the abiding state of a bodhisattva is, it is abiding in the perfection of wisdom. If one asks what that is like, it is that since not even the slightest phenomenon can be apprehended apart from the sphere of reality, all phenomena have become merely the nature of the sphere of reality. Having known this, the term "the mind abiding in the sphere of reality" also means not abiding in anything at all; for the sphere of reality is designated as the suchness that is free from all objects of apprehension, and since there is no basis to be apprehended therein, one does not abide in the sphere of reality, the suchness of entities, or suchness itself by way of apprehension. Because it does not abide in anything, it is termed "abiding without abiding." Thus, that wisdom which realizes the equality of being devoid of all objects of apprehension abides without abiding in any phenomenon, just as the clouds in the sky do not rely upon anything. ^1-383

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-384]]

By the power of that profound "abiding without abiding," even though one does not abide in anything, one gains mastery over the ocean-like liberation of perfecting, ripening, and purifying. Just as a mantra-holder who has no house or the like built for their own dwelling travels through the sky, when they wish to abide, they use the power of the mantra to arrive at that expanse of space in an instant; even though it is not a place where things grow from the earth, they bless it and cause it to appear as a beautiful pleasure grove, complete with delightful flowers, fruit-bearing trees, and the like, just as they desire. In that way, through the blessing of the profound perfection of wisdom—which is an abiding without abiding and has become a supreme mantra—the bodhisattva who is wise and clarifies the mental continuums of others engages in the manner of perfecting, ripening, and purifying just as they wish. Yet, even while engaging in such inconceivable perfecting, ripening, and purifying, they do not apprehend the phenomena of the path that bring about the realization of one's own benefit, nor do they apprehend the qualities of a Buddha that are to be attained. ^1-384

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-385]]

One does not apprehend the act of explaining for the benefit of others, nor does one apprehend the one who desires the teaching—that is, the very object of the teaching—because one has realized the nature of all things as being without apprehension. This way of abiding is the state of bodhisattvas who, merely conventionally, wish to pacify the suffering of other sentient beings and who delight in the path of the Great Vehicle and the qualities of its fruition. ^1-385

###### **དེ་ཉིད་གནས་པ་གཞན་ཀུན་གྱི་མཆོག་ཏུ་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-5-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-386]]

Second, the three verses on the liberation of the arhats and so forth: apart from the abiding of the Tathāgata himself, who dwells in the supreme meditative absorption of liberation that has vanquished the two obscurations, this abiding of the bodhisattva is the supreme among all the various states of abiding of others—including śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas—which possess the bliss of stainless, perfectly pacified meditative absorption where all defilements are stilled; and because there is nothing higher than this, it is the unsurpassable abiding. Even though one dwells in such a state of meditative absorption where all elaborations are stilled, one does not become one-sidedly pacified. For example, just as birds, though they dwell in the unsupported sky, do not fall to the ground, and fish, though they dwell in water, are not disturbed by their ears or nose and so forth, and thus do not die; in that same way, through the power of the meditative concentration of the bodhisattva who dwells without abiding, one reaches—that is, realizes—the realm of nirvāṇa, the peace beyond the world. Yet, because one dwells in emptiness without support, one does not fall into the extremes of existence or peace, like the bird; and because one is not attached even to emptiness, one is not disturbed by the water of one's own dwelling, and thus one does not become one-sidedly pacified in nirvāṇa. ^1-386

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-387]]

Therefore, those who wish to attain the supreme qualities of all beings, to touch the most marvelous and supreme primordial wisdom of the Buddha—which is the supreme among all phenomena—and to bestow the most excellent, supreme, and holy gift of Dharma, are told: "Rely upon this supreme abode of the Victors and their spiritual heirs, who work for the benefit of all sentient beings." Regarding this, it is also seen that some apply these terms as follows: the attainment of the supreme qualities of all beings refers to the state of the dharmakāya, which transcends all worlds; the possession of the most marvelous primordial wisdom of the Buddha refers to the sambhogakāya; and the gift of Dharma bestowed upon the world refers to the nirmāṇakāya. Furthermore, some apply the "excellent gift of Dharma" to the path corresponding to merit, the "supreme gift" to the path corresponding to liberation, and the "holy gift" to the Dharma of the supreme vehicle. However, it is also acceptable to treat these as a list of the greatness of these qualities without applying them individually. 
*Showing the greatness of being the supreme among all objects of knowledge and training.* ^1-387

###### **ཤེས་བྱ་བསླབ་པ་ཐམས་ཅད་ཀྱི་མཆོག་ཡིན་པའི་ཆེ་བ་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-5-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-388]]

Third, regarding the teaching that it is the supreme among all trainings, there are two parts: the teaching of its supremacy in terms of its essence, and the teaching of its supremacy in terms of producing inexhaustible results. ^1-388

###### **ངོ་བོའི་སྒོ་ནས་མཆོག་ཏུ་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-5-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-389]]

Teaching its supremacy in terms of its essence: The first part consists of one verse, beginning with "This training..." This training in the perfection of wisdom is the supreme among all the countless trainings that the perfect Buddha, the Guide, has taught through various gateways of the Dharma. Because there is no training whatsoever that surpasses it, it is unsurpassable. Therefore, any wise person who wishes to go to the far shore—to reach the culmination of all trainings—should train in this perfection of wisdom, the training of the Buddha. ^1-389

###### **འབྲས་བུ་མི་ཟད་པར་འབྱིན་པའི་སྒོ་ནས་མཆོག་ཏུ་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-5-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-390]]

Teaching its supremacy in terms of producing inexhaustible results: The second part consists of three sections: a brief presentation, an extensive explanation, and a summary of the meaning. The brief presentation: ^1-390

###### **མདོར་བསྟན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-5-3-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-391]]

The first part consists of one verse, beginning with "This is the Dharma treasure..." This perfection of wisdom is the source of all inexhaustible Dharmas taught by the Buddhas of the three times; thus, it is the supreme, great Dharma treasure. Because it gives rise to the precious qualities of the result, such as the powers, and also gives rise to all the various treasures of qualities such as the path’s perfections, the dāraṇīs, and the factors of enlightenment, it is the supreme and wondrous treasury of the vast Dharma. It is the lineage from which all Buddhas arise, for they are born from it. It is the treasury where all the physical and mental well-being and happiness of sentient beings reside, for from the realization of this profound perfection of wisdom, all profound and vast Dharmas arise, and all the excellent paths up to the correct view of worldly beings arise. It should be understood that from the basis—the perfection of wisdom—and from the path—the practice of the perfection of wisdom—and from the result—the attainment of the perfection of wisdom—all worldly and supramundane excellence arises; for if it is absent, all these cease, and if it is present, they follow in its wake. Although all the protectors of the world in the ten directions—the Buddhas of the past, future, and present—are born from the practice of this perfection of wisdom, its essence, the dharmadhātu, never knows exhaustion and remains free from increase or decrease. Furthermore, if the basis, the perfection of wisdom, is absent, there is no realization or practice of it in accordance with the truth, and if the path is absent, there is no result. However, one should understand the way in which the wisdom that realizes and teaches the basis—the dharmadhātu, which is the non-dual reality or the nature of phenomena—is also the cause. Extensive explanation: ^1-391

###### **རྒྱས་པར་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-5-3-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-392]]

The second part consists of two sections: an explanation by way of external dependent origination, and an explanation by way of internal dependent origination. ^1-392

###### **ཕྱི་ཡི་རྟེན་འབྲེལ་གྱི་སྒོ་ནས་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-5-3-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-393]]

The first part consists of two sections: the example of external dependent origination, and the meaning, which is the explanation of the perfection of wisdom. ^1-393

###### **དཔེ་ཕྱི་ཡི་རྟེན་འབྲེལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-5-3-2-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-394]]

The first is the single verse on trees, fruits, and so forth. In this way, although the essence of the dharmadhātu is unchanging, the play of dependent origination of conditioned phenomena is unceasing; thus, external phenomena also arise dependently and in connection. As for trees, fruits, flowers, and forests, however many there may be, they all arise perfectly from the earth and emerge upon the surface of the earth. Yet, even though trees and the like are produced again and again, the earth does not become exhausted or depleted as it was before, nor does it increase; because it brings forth fruits, it changes from its initial state, yet it does not diminish. It does not conceptually construct, "I am producing these fruits," and even though it produces fruits again and again, it has no weariness. Just as, due to the causal power of the karma of sentient beings, plants and trees and so forth arise from the great earth as long as the world endures, this is the manner of dependent origination, which is devoid of any other agent. By way of this example, one should understand that all external phenomena also arise from their own respective causes and function without conceptual construction. ^1-394

###### **དོན་ཤེར་ཕྱིན་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-5-3-2-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-395]]

The second is the single verse on the "Sons of the Buddha" and so forth. The Sons of the Buddha, the bodhisattvas, as well as the śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas, gods, and all other beings such as humans—all the phenomena of their happiness and well-being arise from the supreme perfection of wisdom. For from the perfection of wisdom, the Buddha arises, and from the Buddha’s teaching of the Dharma, all the qualities of higher status and definite goodness arise. Without that teaching, it is certain that even the mundane correct view, which understands the cause and effect of virtue and non-virtue, would not arise, because it cannot be known by one’s own power alone. If there is no mundane correct view, then all mundane happiness cannot be accomplished and thus will not arise; and it goes without saying that the path and fruit of the supramundane correct view would not arise. Alternatively, the perfection of wisdom is the meaning of supreme wisdom; it is the mind that engages with the nature of the two truths without error, just as they are. Therefore, since all minds that are unerring regarding the mode of the two truths are included within it and belong to its parts, all wisdom that accomplishes higher status and definite goodness also relies upon it, either directly or indirectly. Thus, although all happiness of cyclic existence and nirvana arises from the perfection of wisdom, that perfection of wisdom itself never experiences depletion or increase; it remains just so. ^1-395

###### **ནང་གི་རྟེན་འབྲེལ་གྱི་སྒོ་ནས་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-5-3-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-396]]

The second part consists of two points: the dependent origination of cyclic existence in the forward order, and the dependent origination of purification in the reverse order. ^1-396

###### **དཔེ་ལུགས་འབྱུང་འཁོར་བའི་རྟེན་འབྲེལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-5-3-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-397]]

The first part consists of one verse beginning with "The lowest of sentient beings..." In this world, in relation to body and resources, the Sugata has taught that all the various classes of sentient beings—the lowest, being the three miserable realms; the intermediate, being humans; and the supreme, being the gods—arise from the root of ignorance. Just as it is said, "All phenomena arise from causes; the Tathāgata has taught those causes," any conceptual thought that grasps at an "I" or a "self," which does not see the true meaning and grasps things in a distorted way, is called ignorance. Because the twelve links of dependent origination, beginning with formative actions, arise from that ignorance, this wheel of suffering—which is of the nature of revolving again and again as contaminated karma, existence, and other conditions of affliction gather together—arises continuously. That wheel of ignorance within the continuum of sentient beings does not come to an end through the arising of results, nor does it increase; it remains just so. Therefore, because it appears while not being established by nature, the fact that there is no manifest increase or decrease in existence, which is like an illusory appearance, is taught from the perspective of conventional truth, taking as the basis the general world systems pervaded by the dharmadhātu. ^1-397

###### **དོན་ལུགས་ལྡོག་རྣམ་བྱང་གི་རྟེན་འབྲེལ་གྱི་ཚུལ་གྱིས་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-5-3-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-398]]

The second part consists of one verse beginning with "The wisdom, the method, the gateway..." All the various classes of the non-learner’s wisdom of a buddha, the path of meditation that accords with its method, the path of seeing that serves as its very gateway, the path of preparation that is the means for its arising, and the path of accumulation that is its root—all of these arise from the supreme perfection of wisdom, for they all emerge from the realization of selflessness. Through the gathering of conditions such as the accumulation of merit by that spiritual friend, the three types of wisdom, there arises a wheel of activity—or a wheel of wisdom—that transcends the world, which abandons the objects to be abandoned on one’s own path and generates the qualities of realization. Its root, the perfection of wisdom, never suffers exhaustion through cessation, nor does it increase beyond its previous state; it remains just so. In this regard, the *Ārya-Gaganagañja-paripṛcchā Sūtra* states:
"If one fully understands all phenomena
That arise in dependence upon causes,
Their treasury is inexhaustible;
The treasury of the Dharma is inconceivable.
The Protector of the World has taught
These four things as inexhaustible:
Sentient beings, space, the mind of enlightenment,
And likewise the qualities of a buddha.
If these were substantial entities,
They would become completely exhausted;
But because they are devoid of such, they are inexhaustible—
Therefore, they are taught as inexhaustible." ^1-398

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-399]]

To summarize the meaning: ^1-399

###### **དོན་བསྡུ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-1-3-5-3-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-400]]

Third, summarizing the meaning: "A bodhisattva, etc." is a single verse. To know that all phenomena of cyclic existence and nirvana are merely appearances of dependent origination is to grasp the profound essence of the Buddha’s teaching. It is taught that by realizing this, one attains the vision of the Dharma and sees the Buddha’s Dharmakāya. Therefore, one should understand the conventional truth, which is the division of the two truths, as the unerring mode of dependent origination. If a bodhisattva thoroughly investigates this phenomenon of dependent origination through reasoning, they find that while it is merely something that cannot withstand analysis as a dependent origination, in the ultimate sense, it is primordially unborn by its own nature and unceasing, as it is extinguished; it is equal in the nature of the Dharmadhātu. If one understands and familiarizes oneself with this profound meaning of the perfection of wisdom, in which the two truths are inseparable, its function is like the sun, which, being free of clouds and radiating light, dispels all darkness. By completely destroying the dense, adventitious obscuration of ignorance, one attains the self-arisen primordial wisdom that is non-dual with the Dharmadhātu, and thus becomes a Buddha. So it is said.
Explaining the other five companions. ^1-400

###### **ཕར་ཕྱིན་གཞན་ལྔ་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-401]]

Second, explaining the other five perfections: regarding the five—meditative concentration, diligence, patience, ethical discipline, and generosity— ^1-401

###### **བསམ་གཏན་གྱི་ཕར་ཕྱིན་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-402]]

Explaining the perfection of meditative concentration. Regarding the first, explaining the perfection of meditative concentration, there are four points: training in meditative concentration as the basis for the supreme result; training without attachment to one's own benefit; engaging in the benefit of others through the gateway of non-attachment; and concluding by way of the superior and inferior distinctions of intention. Training in meditative concentration as the basis for the supreme result: the first involves training in the four meditative concentrations and training in the four formless meditative absorptions. ^1-402

###### **བསམ་གཏན་བཞི་ལ་བསླབ་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-403]]

First, the single verse beginning with "possessing great power."
