A bodhisattva who possesses the "great power" to accomplish qualities abides in and becomes familiar with the four meditative concentrations. At that time, they do not abide by grasping at characteristics, nor do they abide by tasting the flavor of those concentrations. Nevertheless, these four—the actual meditative concentrations, each with their respective limbs such as examination and analysis—become the basis for attaining the abandonment and realization of the supreme, perfect enlightenment, because the path of seeing and so forth rely upon the mind of meditative concentration. ^1-403

###### **གཟུགས་མེད་ཀྱི་སྙོམས་འཇུག་བཞི་ལ་བསླབ་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-404]]

Second, the single verse beginning with "attaining the supreme perfection of wisdom." A bodhisattva who has "attained the supreme perfection of wisdom" and abides in meditative concentration also experiences the four "supreme formless meditative absorptions," yet they do not become attached to them. Rather, they train in these formless "meditative concentrations" because they serve as favorable conditions for the absence of faults and suffering, as well as for clairvoyance and the like, thereby acting as conditions that assist in "attaining the supreme, perfect enlightenment." They do not train in them as a śrāvaka would, in order to exhaust the "taints" in their own continuum and manifest the result of a one-sided peace. Training without attachment to one's own benefit. ^1-404

###### **བསླབ་ཀྱང་རང་དོན་དུ་ཆགས་པ་མེད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-405]]

The second part has two sections: a brief presentation and a detailed explanation. ^1-405

###### **མདོར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-406]]

First, this is the single verse beginning with "qualities and so forth." This method is the "wondrous and marvelous" nature of bodhisattvas who "gather all qualities and so forth." What is this? It is that by "abiding in meditative concentration," they "realize" through "wisdom" that it is "without marks." If bodhisattvas who "abide in that meditative concentration" happen to "exhaust their karma" and "change their life," they "take birth" in the "desire realm" as they "wish," according to their own "intentions," for the sake of "ripening sentient beings" and so forth. ^1-406

###### **རྒྱས་པར་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-407]]

The second part has two sections: the example and the meaning. ^1-407

###### **དཔེ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-1-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-408]]

First, this is the single verse beginning with "For example, in Jambudvīpa..." If one asks why, despite having attained the meditative concentration of the causal equipoise, one does not take birth as a god in the resultant meditative concentration, but instead takes birth in the desire realm, it is because one is not attached to meditative concentration and it is for the sake of perfecting one's own qualities. For example, if a person from Jambudvīpa who had not previously gone to the divine realms—such as the "wondrous and marvelous" divine cities—were to go there later, and upon seeing those delightful places, such as the palaces and pleasure groves "fully possessed" by the gods, were to "not become attached" to them and instead "return" to this Jambudvīpa, then just as that person, having seen the splendor of the divine realm but not being attached to it, does not remain there, so too does one "not become attached" to the realms of this Jambudvīpa and so forth. ^1-408

###### **དོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-1-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-409]]

Second, this is the single verse beginning with "In that way, the bodhisattva..." Just as in that example, the bodhisattva who "holds" the supreme qualities, such as wisdom and compassion, and who "strives" in the yoga of meditative absorption, "remains" in the meditative absorptions of the four concentrations and so forth, yet does not "become attached" to them; instead, he "remains" in the desire realm, having no "attachment" to either the previous meditative concentrations or the phenomena of the desire realm. Even though he is born in the desire realm, he is not "stained" by the desire of the desire realm and so forth, just as a "lotus" is not "stained" by "water," because he does not "remain" in the phenomena of the childish. Engaging in the benefit of others through the gateway of being without attachment. ^1-409

###### **ཆགས་པ་མེད་པའི་སྒོ་ནས་གཞན་དོན་དུ་འཇུག་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-1-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-410]]

Third, this has two parts: the brief presentation and the detailed explanation. ^1-410

###### **མདོར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-1-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-411]]

First, this is the single verse beginning with "The great being..." The purpose for which that bodhisattva, even though having attained the concentrations and form realms, does not take birth there but instead takes birth again in the desire realm, is solely to "ripen" sentient beings through means such as teaching the Dharma, to "purify" the buddha-fields, and to "accomplish" the perfection of wisdom and the other perfections; it is not for any other cause. If one were to think, "Is it not sufficient to accomplish these in the formless realm and so forth?"—it is because in the formless realm there are no objects of generosity or opportunities to teach the Dharma, and thus the great "perfection" of generosity and the other causes for generating the "qualities" of "bodhi" would be diminished; therefore, that bodhisattva does not "seek" birth in the "formless" realm. ^1-411

###### **རྒྱས་པར་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-1-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-412]]

Second, this has two parts: the example and the meaning. ^1-412

###### **དཔེ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-1-3-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-413]]

First, this is the single verse beginning with "For example, a person..." It should be understood by applying the example: just as a person who has found a "treasure" of "precious" things does not "generate" a "mind" of "attachment" or "desire" for that treasure and leaves without "taking" it, but then at another time "takes" those "great" treasures in their entirety for the sake of others, and having taken them, goes to their own "home" and, without "becoming attached" to them, gives them to their "friends and relatives." ^1-413

###### **དོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-1-3-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-414]]

Second, this is the single verse beginning with "Just as that..." It should be understood by applying the example: just as those skillful bodhisattvas who provide sentient beings with immediate "joy" and future "happiness" attain the four "calmings" of the faults of the "meditative stabilization" (dhyāna) that are contrary to it, and, without "becoming attached" to the "happiness" of that meditative stabilization, abandon the meditative stabilization they have attained that produces results, those who possess "compassion" for "beings" enter back into the "desire realm." This is because, since there is much suffering in this desire realm, they cultivate the aspects of the path for purifying the field; because there is a domain for practicing the perfections, they quickly perfect the accumulations; and because they are able to mature sentient beings by teaching the Dharma, they wish to attain enlightenment quickly, and thus, by taking birth in this desire realm, they quickly perfect the accumulations. 
Concluding by way of the superior and inferior through the distinction of intention. ^1-414

###### **བསམ་པའི་དབྱེ་བས་མཆོག་དམན་དུ་གྱུར་པའི་ཚུལ་གྱིས་མཇུག་བསྡུ་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-1-4-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-415]]

Regarding the fourth, there are two aspects: the inferior to be abandoned and the superior to be adopted. ^1-415

###### **སྤང་བྱ་དམན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-1-4-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-416]]

First, this is the single verse beginning with "If a bodhisattva..." If a bodhisattva, even while abiding in the meditative stabilizations of liberation and the four meditative stabilizations (dhyāna), generates the intention of desiring the vehicle of the hearer arhats or solitary realizers, then, in relation to the path of the Great Vehicle, such a state is considered not to be "equipoised," and is "agitated" and "distracted" in mind. For what reason? Because by attending to that inferior path, the mind is captivated and distracted from the purpose of the Great Vehicle, and thus one is completely "deprived" of the great purpose, the "qualities" of the "buddha" fruit, just as a merchant with a broken ship does not reach the island of jewels. ^1-416

###### **བླང་བྱ་མཆོག་གི་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-1-4-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-417]]

Second, this is the single verse beginning with "Furthermore, form..." There is also a state that is different from that manner, which is as follows: even if one is delighted by and attached to the five qualities of desire—namely, form, sound, and likewise smell, taste, and touch—and is actively striving to accomplish them, if one is free from the desire for the vehicle of the arhats or solitary realizers and generates the mind of unsurpassed enlightenment or delights in the path of the bodhisattva, that hero should be known as being "always equipoised." Although one may be distracted by the five qualities of desire, because one has not generated a mind that is an obstacle to omniscience, one is single-pointed in mind upon the great purpose, and thus it is said to be equipoise. In this way, all meditative concentration of a bodhisattva is a factor in attaining the fruition of complete enlightenment, a means seized by compassion for the sake of others, and, being seized by the wisdom that realizes the lack of intrinsic nature, it proceeds in a manner of non-attachment. These three distinctions in the manner of explaining the meditative concentration of the Great Vehicle are likewise the case for all the subsequent perfections. ^1-417

###### **བརྩོན་འགྲུས་ཀྱི་ཕར་ཕྱིན་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-418]]

The explanation of the perfection of diligence. Second, regarding diligence, there are three: the instruction to exert diligence for the sake of others, exerting diligence without discouragement, and exerting diligence in the manner of the purity of the three spheres. ^1-418

###### **གཞན་དོན་དུ་བརྩོན་པར་གདམས་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-419]]

The instruction to exert diligence for the sake of others. The first of these has two parts: a brief thematic presentation and a detailed explanation. ^1-419

###### **མདོར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-420]]

The first, "sentient beings, persons, and so forth," is a single verse. A bodhisattva is one who has donned the armor of accomplishing benefit and happiness for the sake of all those others—however many there may be—who bear the names of "sentient beings" or "persons," and who is furthermore completely pure of the stains of self-interest. Such a one engages in the perfection of wisdom by means of reverently exerting themselves and constantly applying themselves. For example, just as female servants who fetch water have no independence and are under the power of their masters, in the same way, heroic bodhisattvas are under the power of all sentient beings, or have become subject to others. ^1-420

###### **རྒྱས་པར་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-421]]

The second part consists of two aspects: not abandoning diligence for their sake even when harmed, and the manner of constantly striving for the benefit of others. ^1-421

###### **གནོད་པ་བྱས་ཀྱང་དེའི་དོན་དུ་བརྩོན་པ་མི་འདོར་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-2-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-422]]

The first of these has two parts: the example and the meaning. ^1-422

###### **དཔེ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-2-1-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-423]]

The first, "if one is scolded," and so forth, is a single verse. In this way, even if one is "scolded" by means of verbal abuse, or even if one is constantly "struck" by the body, a female servant does not speak words that contradict the commands of her master. Not only does she not feel anger in return, no matter how much anger he displays toward her, but she thinks, "If I am at fault, he might even kill me, for I am subject to him." Thinking thus, with a mind of extreme fear and trepidation, she obeys his commands, and she is completely overcome by the power of that master. ^1-423

###### **དོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-2-1-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-424]]

The second, "just as enlightenment," and so forth, is a single verse. Just as in the example just explained, bodhisattvas who have entered the path of supreme enlightenment for the sake of unsurpassed enlightenment should dwell as if they had become servants to all sentient beings. The reason for this is that because I attain enlightenment and perfect the qualities by completely embracing these sentient beings with compassion, I should regard them with deference and act in accordance with their intentions, just as a female servant does. If one does not act in this way and becomes angry at sentient beings, it is like fire arising from grass and wood, which then burns that very grass and wood; for by the fire of one's own anger, one destroys oneself, and just as if one were killed, the life of the path to enlightenment is cut off. ^1-424

###### **རྟག་ཏུ་གཞན་དོན་ལ་བརྩོན་པའི་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-2-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-425]]

The second, "my happiness," and so forth, is a single verse. Since cherishing oneself leads to all the sufferings of cyclic existence, while cherishing others leads to the attainment of all the qualities of a buddha, having understood this, one should abandon the mind that desires one's own happiness and, with a mind that does not hope for any return or maturation, strive day and night to accomplish the needs of other sentient beings. Just as a mother serves her only child with a mind that is never weary in any way, you should dwell in the service of the welfare of sentient beings with a sincere heart, without ever becoming weary. Unwavering effort. ^1-425

###### **མ་ཞུམ་པར་བརྩོན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-426]]

The second, unwavering effort, has two parts: a brief presentation and a detailed explanation. ^1-426

###### **མདོར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-427]]

The first, "bodhisattva," and so forth, is a single verse. Any bodhisattva who, by the power of great compassion, wishes to remain in this cyclic existence for a long, immeasurable time without abandoning it, and who, while dwelling there, is a yogi striving to perform the welfare of sentient beings and to purify the buddha-fields—if, in such a manner, not even an atom of a mind of weariness arises, then that person possesses the perfection of wisdom and will be without negligence in accomplishing the welfare of migrating beings. ^1-427

###### **རྒྱས་པར་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-2-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-428]]

The second has two parts: the factors to be abandoned and the antidotes. ^1-428

###### **སྤང་བྱ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-2-2-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-429]]

The first, "bodhisattva," and so forth, is a single verse. Any bodhisattva who is unskilled in means, thinking, "If I exert myself for a count of immeasurable eons and so forth, up to this much, I will attain unsurpassed enlightenment," and who, having thought this, perceives that duration as long, and who practices the path to enlightenment through the perception of suffering in difficult practices such as giving away one's head and limbs—at the time of practicing such unsurpassed, authentic Dharma, they will, by the power of that very perception, experience suffering for a long time. By that method, they fall away from the perfection of perseverance and become lazy. This is because the essence of perseverance is enthusiasm for virtue; if one possesses that enthusiasm, one is able to engage in that task without being discouraged. If, however, that enthusiasm is diminished by the perception of length and so forth, one becomes listless. For example, in the world, when one has intense enthusiasm, one travels a hundred yojanas happily, but when one lacks enthusiasm, one finds even traveling a single *gyang-drak* to be daunting. Therefore, the generation of a mind that is without limits or boundaries—thinking, "I shall perform the perfecting, ripening, and purifying of the immeasurable realms of sentient beings, equal to the limits of the ends of the immeasurable," by means of that skillful method in which the wisdom realizing non-nature and great compassion are joined—is the armor-like perseverance of a bodhisattva. By entering through such armor, as one becomes familiar with the path, that perseverance increases, and becoming immeasurable and boundless is to be understood in the same way as the liberation of Mañjuśrī and others; one should enter into it in that way. ^1-429

###### **གཉེན་པོ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-2-2-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-430]]

Regarding the second, there are two parts: the perspective of time and the perspective of the nature of things. ^1-430

###### **དུས་ཀྱི་དབང་དུ་བྱས་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-2-2-1-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-431]]

Regarding the first, there is one verse beginning with "From the moment the mind is first generated for supreme enlightenment." The method for initiating the diligence that abandons the perception of time being long and the nature of things being difficult is as follows: from the moment the mind is first generated for supreme enlightenment until the final attainment of unsurpassable enlightenment, one should contemplate and become joyful in the manner of thinking that this interval is as easy to pass through as the duration of a single day and night. One should know that this is the manner in which a wise person, through the clear light of wisdom, initiates diligence. Even though one has experienced unbearable suffering in this beginningless cycle of existence without achieving any benefit, if one does not feel weary of that, then why should one feel weary of this path, which is capable of being accomplished and which improves ever more as one practices it to achieve the great purpose, the final result? If one makes oneself joyful with the thought, "If I practice this path, it will be accomplished," then, by the power of that, one will suppress the perception of it being long and difficult. By viewing the long duration as short, one will increase one's diligence through a state of happiness, free from suffering, just as one who sees the profit of a precious jewel does not think of the long and difficult path as difficult, but rather perceives it as happy. Therefore, one should know that it is not dependent on the nature of the task itself, but rather that it becomes difficult or not difficult due to the power of the mind, just as Bodhisattvas find joy and the perception of happiness even when giving away their bodies or entering the Unrelenting Hell for the sake of others. ^1-431

###### **དངོས་པོའི་དབང་དུ་བྱས་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-2-2-1-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-432]]

The second point, "If someone," and so forth, consists of two verses. If someone were to say, "Destroy this king of mountains, Mount Meru, and after destroying it, you will attain supreme enlightenment," why would that be? If one generates a discouraged mind, thinking, "Mount Meru is so vast and firm, it is difficult to destroy," and calculates, "How many eons would it take to destroy it?"—that kind of mind, accompanied by discouragement, means that the bodhisattva has become lazy, because their enthusiasm has diminished. In that way, if that bodhisattva thinks, "If supreme enlightenment can be attained by just this much effort, then since it is possible to destroy this mountain eventually, what is difficult about it? It is not difficult," and having thought thus, generates a mind of intense enthusiasm, as if they could destroy that Mount Meru into dust in a single moment without measuring the duration of time—at that time, that bodhisattva, who is skilled in the spheres of what is meaningful and what is not, has truly initiated diligence. Possessing such diligence, which does not become discouraged by what is difficult to do, through the gateway of enthusiasm for omniscience, they will attain supreme enlightenment without long delay, because they possess such diligence, which is the cause for attaining it. ^1-432

###### **འཁོར་གསུམ་དག་པའི་ཚུལ་གྱིས་བརྩོན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-433]]

Striving by way of the purity of the three spheres. Regarding the third, there are two: what is to be turned away from, and what is to be engaged in. ^1-433

###### **ལྡོག་བྱ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-2-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-434]]

The first is the single verse beginning with "bringing beings to maturity." In this way, when one initiates diligence, one who has a focal point becomes, by that very power, lazy with an impurity; for what reason? It is because, while in the state of holding to signs—thinking, "I shall bring beings to maturity and perform the benefit of others"—if one exerts oneself through body, speech, and mind while remaining in the perception of a self, then, in comparison to the diligence of non-perception, one has become lazy. The reason for this is that, because of that view which has a focal point, one's exertion does not become authentic, and by falling into its opposite, one becomes far from omniscience, just as the sky and the earth are far apart. ^1-434

###### **འཇུག་བྱ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-2-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-435]]

The second is the single verse beginning with "at the time of body and..." When one initiates diligence, at the time when the initiator is without the perception of their own body and mind, or the perception of the body and mind of others, and having completely turned away from the perception of the three spheres of exertion, one engages in the manner of phenomena that are without the extremes or the duality of self and other. The Victors, who perform benefit, have taught that this is the perfection of wisdom of those bodhisattvas who desire the supreme state of great enlightenment, where stains are pacified and which never declines.
Explanation of the perfection of patience. ^1-435

###### **བཟོད་པའི་ཕར་ཕྱིན་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-436]]

The third is patience, which consists of three types: the patience of certain conviction in the Dharma, the patience of not being provoked by harm, and the patience of voluntarily accepting suffering. ^1-436

###### **ཆོས་ལ་ངེས་སེམས་ཀྱི་བཟོད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-437]]

Regarding the patience of certain conviction in the Dharma, the first has two parts: the actual nature and its benefits. ^1-437

###### **དངོས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-3-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-438]]

The first is the single verse beginning with "if from another..." If one happens to hear harsh words of abuse, such as someone else disparaging oneself, a bodhisattva who is skilled in means will rejoice, thinking, "This is a cause for my own happiness, because patience arises precisely through finding an object for patience." Furthermore, if one examines this in terms of ultimate reality—thinking, "Who is the speaker of these harsh words, who is the one hearing them, what is the cause such as anger that leads to speaking ill, and for what purpose, such as hurting another's feelings, are they spoken?"—one realizes that these dependently arisen appearances are like illusions, lacking any inherent nature. Having realized this, one who possesses the supreme perfection of patience is wise, for they have understood the meaning that one should not be angry at mere phenomena that have arisen from conditions. Foolish people who do not understand this in that way become angry in return, thereby harming both themselves and others. ^1-438

###### **དེའི་ཕན་ཡོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-3-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-439]]

The second is the two verses beginning with "a bodhisattva..." If a bodhisattva possesses the virtuous qualities of patience, then even if one were to fill this trichiliocosm—the great thousand-fold world system—with various precious substances and offer them as a mass of generosity to the Buddhas who know the world, as well as to the arhats and pratyekabuddhas, one would not be able to compare even a fraction of the merit of that person who possesses the nature of patience. ^1-439

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-440]]

The fruition of maturation for one who abides in patience is that the body becomes completely pure like gold, possessing the thirty-two excellent marks and attaining boundless power and strength. The fruition of human effort for the person of patience, who has a mind convinced of the Dharma, is that they will proclaim the supreme Dharma of emptiness to sentient beings. The dominant fruition is that in this world, beings will rejoice in and rely upon that person who possesses patience. The fruition of separation is that one becomes a wise person who is free from ignorance. ^1-440

###### **གནོད་པས་མི་མཇེད་པའི་བཟོད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-441]]

Patience that is not provoked by harm. The second, the patience of being unperturbed by harm, consists of two parts: how one should practice patience, and the fruition of that patience. ^1-441

###### **ཇི་ལྟར་བཟོད་པར་བྱ་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-3-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-442]]

The first is the single verse beginning with "If a sentient being..." If one were to take sandalwood paste or powder and, with a mind of great devotion, smear and apply it to the body of a bodhisattva, and another person, with a mind of harm, were to scatter hot embers upon his head, the bodhisattva should generate a mind that is equal and impartial toward both, free from attachment and aversion. ^1-442

###### **དེ་ལྟར་བཟོད་པའི་འབྲས་བུ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-3-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-443]]

The second is the single verse beginning with "The bodhisattva..." A bodhisattva who is skilled in the manner of the meaning, having practiced patience in that way, generates a mind endowed with patience and dedicates it completely to supreme enlightenment. That heroic bodhisattva, because of his patience for the sake of benefiting all worlds, will surpass the śrāvaka arhats, pratyekabuddhas, and as many of the various dispositions of other sentient beings as there are. In other sūtras, it is taught, "Śāriputra, the patience of a śrāvaka is minor because it only abandons one's own afflictions, whereas the patience of a bodhisattva is immeasurable because it is for the sake of all sentient beings," and so forth, illustrating this with analogies of the difference between Jambu river gold and brass, Mount Meru and a mustard seed, the ocean and a drop of water from the tip of a hair. ^1-443

###### **སྡུག་བསྔལ་དང་ལེན་གྱི་བཟོད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-3-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-444]]

Patience of accepting suffering. The third is the explanation of the patience of accepting suffering. This consists of two verses beginning with "The one who practices patience thinks thus..." The one who practices patience should also generate a mind thinking thus: In the realms of hell beings, animals, and the Lord of Death—that is, the realm of hungry ghosts—there are many sufferings that are difficult to endure, such as heat, cold, hunger, and thirst. Because of attachment to the qualities of desire, they experience those harms without any independence, and since they do not accomplish any purpose for themselves or others, they experience suffering in vain. Why, then, should I not practice patience now for the sake of supreme enlightenment, which accomplishes the purpose of self and others? Thinking thus, the bodhisattva who accepts and endures all the sufferings that exist in the world—such as being whipped with iron, struck with sticks, pierced with weapons, killed by having their life cut off, bound with iron chains and the like, beaten with stones and hammers, having their head severed, or having their ears, limbs, and nose cut off—that bodhisattva will abide in the perfection of patience. Explanation of the perfection of discipline. ^1-444

###### **ཚུལ་ཁྲིམས་ཀྱི་ཕར་ཕྱིན་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-4-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-445]]

The fourth is the explanation of the benefits of possessing supreme ethical discipline, and the distinction between the superior and inferior aspects of that ethical discipline. ^1-445

###### **མཆོག་གི་ཚུལ་ཁྲིམས་དང་ལྡན་པའི་ཕན་ཡོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-4-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-446]]

The benefits of possessing supreme ethical discipline. First, there is one verse beginning with "By ethical discipline..." By ethical discipline, those who desire peace, which is nirvana, become supreme, because that ethical discipline is like the feet for walking toward liberation. Regarding the distinction of its nature, the phrase "the ethical discipline of a bodhisattva dwells in the sphere of activity of the ten powers" means that the ten powers are the Buddha, and this is the ethical discipline of training in his footsteps; because it is not associated with the inferior path or similar mental states, and is not stained by the defilements of all that is to be abandoned on the Mahāyāna path, it is "undiminished ethical discipline," and by this, the ethical discipline of restraining from misconduct is indicated. The "activity of restraint," or ethical discipline, is that which follows all virtuous qualities; because protecting against the discordant side and engaging in the antidote itself becomes the nature of ethical discipline, this is called the ethical discipline of gathering virtuous qualities. Protecting ethical discipline in such a way for the sake of benefiting all sentient beings, and also dedicating that to great enlightenment, is the ethical discipline of benefiting sentient beings. Having thus briefly indicated the nature of ethical discipline, ^1-446

###### **ཚུལ་ཁྲིམས་དེའི་མཆོག་དམན་གྱི་དབྱེ་བ་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-4-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-447]]

Explaining the distinction between the superior and inferior aspects of that ethical discipline. Second, regarding the explanation of the distinction between its superior and inferior aspects, there are three divisions: by way of complete dedication, by way of motivation, and by way of the presence or absence of grasping. ^1-447

###### **ཡོངས་བསྔོའི་སྒོ་ནས་དབྱེ་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-4-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-448]]

Distinction by way of complete dedication. First, regarding the verse beginning with "If a Shravaka," if one observes ethical discipline with a mind that desires to touch the enlightenment of a Shravaka or a Pratyekabuddha, then in relation to this Great Vehicle, that person is one who has transgressed ethical discipline, whose intention is unwise, and likewise, whose practice is also degenerate, because they have fallen from the great purpose; thus, their intention and practice are degenerate. However, if one completely dedicates that ethical discipline—for the sake of which one is observing it—to the unsurpassed enlightenment, which is the pacification of the two obscurations, then even if that person remains in a manner of striving for the five desirable qualities, they are said to be abiding in the perfection of wisdom, because they have not degenerated, having entered upon the path and the supreme fruit. Distinction by way of motivation. ^1-448

###### **ཀུན་སློང་གི་སྒོ་ནས་དབྱེ་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-4-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-449]]

The second has two parts: a brief presentation and a detailed explanation. ^1-449

###### **མདོར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-4-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-450]]

First, regarding the verse beginning with "Whatever dharma is certain," it is said that any dharma of training that is certain by way of motivation—meaning that which causes the qualities of supreme enlightenment, the goal to be attained by bodhisattvas, to arise—is the meaning of the ethical discipline of bodhisattvas who possess the dharma of qualities. Conversely, the Guide has stated that any dharma seized by an inferior motivation, which causes one to degenerate completely from great enlightenment, the fruit to be attained by beneficial bodhisattvas, is a transgression of the ethical discipline of the Great Vehicle. ^1-450

###### **རྒྱས་པར་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-4-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-451]]

The second, the detailed explanation, has two parts: the supreme to be engaged in, and the inferior to be turned away from. ^1-451

###### **འཇུག་བྱ་མཆོག** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-4-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-452]]

The first, regarding the verse beginning with "If a bodhisattva," is as follows: Even if a beginner bodhisattva acts in a way that is attached to the five desirable qualities, if they have gone for refuge from the heart to the Buddha, the Dharma, and the noble Sangha, and if they do not separate from the intention of omniscience, thinking, "I shall attain the state of buddhahood," then one who is wise regarding the meaning should understand that person to be abiding in the perfection of ethical discipline. ^1-452

###### **ལྡོག་བྱ་དམན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-4-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-453]]

The second, regarding the verse beginning with "If for eons," is as follows: Even if one has practiced the ten paths of wholesome action for ten million eons, if one generates a desire for the level of a Shravaka arhat or a Pratyekabuddha, at that moment a fault arises in the ethical discipline of the Great Vehicle, and one’s ethical discipline is truly degenerated. For a practitioner of the Great Vehicle to generate the mind directed toward the level of a Shravaka or Pratyekabuddha is a fault far graver than even the four defeats of a bhikṣu. Although one may have committed a defeat, if one has not abandoned the mind generated toward supreme enlightenment, one does not thereby lose the fortune for the path of great enlightenment; however, that generation of the mind toward an inferior state, as long as it is not abandoned, renders one without the fortune to proceed toward the path and fruit of the Great Vehicle. ^1-453

###### **རློམ་པ་ཡོད་མེད་ཀྱི་སྒོ་ནས་དབྱེ་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-4-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-454]]

The third, regarding the classification by way of the presence or absence of arrogance, consists of two parts: what is to be adopted and what is to be abandoned. ^1-454

###### **བླང་བྱ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-4-2-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-455]]

The first, regarding the verse beginning with "Guarding ethical discipline," is as follows: One guards ethical discipline and dedicates that very act toward supreme enlightenment. By not grasping at that ethical discipline as a sign, one does not become arrogant, and one does not praise oneself by way of that ethical discipline or the like. If one has completely abandoned the perception of "I" and the perception of "sentient beings," such a bodhisattva is said to be abiding in the perfection of wisdom. ^1-455

###### **སྤང་བྱ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-4-2-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-456]]

The second, regarding the actual factors to be abandoned and the teaching of their antidotes, consists of two parts. ^1-456

###### **སྤང་བྱ་དངོས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-4-2-3-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-457]]

The first, regarding the verse beginning with "If a bodhisattva," is as follows: If those bodhisattvas who are practicing the Great Vehicle, the path of the Victors, think in this way—"These beings possess ethical discipline, and these beings are immoral"—and, having entered into such diverse perceptions, possess attachment, then by that perception alone they are said to be truly immoral. Because they possess attachment and, by way of that, engage in disparaging others and the like, that defilement causes the ethical discipline of the Great Vehicle to degenerate, and that person possesses no pure ethical discipline, for it has been corrupted by that perception of grasping at those things. ^1-457

###### **དེའི་གཉེན་པོར་བསྟན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-4-2-3-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-458]]

The second, regarding the verse beginning with "For whom there is no perception of self," is as follows: For a person who has realized the selflessness of the person—in whom there is no perception of self and no perception of sentient beings—how could there be the "immorality" of possessing the perception of grasping at a self, or the "immorality" of being separated from desire, which arises due to that? Any non-virtue whatsoever has as its root the perception of grasping at a self; therefore, in the absence of that, it is impossible for the entity of sin to arise, for there is no cause. This absence of the arrogance of grasping at two things—discipline and non-discipline, or immorality—by way of knowing that all phenomena are devoid of intrinsic nature, is what the Guide has declared to be the bodhisattva’s discipline of restraint. If one possesses this way of being, one possesses the ethical discipline that is pleasing to the noble ones, which is devoid of all faulty conduct, and because it is without grasping, it is also perfectly pure. ༈ Explanation of the perfection of generosity. ^1-458

###### **སྦྱིན་པའི་ཕར་ཕྱིན་བཤད་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-5-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-459]]

The fifth, regarding the perfection of generosity, consists of two parts: first, developing joy in generosity by understanding the faults and benefits of giving and not giving, and second, the manner in which generosity should be practiced. ^1-459

###### **སྦྱིན་པ་བཏང་བ་དང་མ་བཏང་བའི་སྐྱོན་ཡོན་ཤེས་ནས་སྦྱིན་པ་ལ་དགའ་བར་བྱ་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-5-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-460]]

Developing joy in generosity by understanding the faults and benefits of giving and not giving. Regarding the first, there are three parts: the manner in which a bodhisattva practices generosity, the faults of an ordinary person not giving in that way, and the manner of cultivating an aspiration for generosity after understanding those points. ^1-460

###### **བྱང་ཆུབ་སེམས་དཔས་སྦྱིན་པ་གཏོང་བའི་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-5-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-461]]

The first, "Sentient beings purified," and so forth, refers to two verses. Regarding the one whose mental continuum is purified as just explained, because they are free from conceptualization and attachment, they possess pure morality. For such a person, there is no viewing of signs in any pleasant or unpleasant object. They give away even their head, hands, and feet to others, and at that time, they have no mind that is discouraged or incapable. In that manner, they give away everything they possess, and at all times, they have no attachment to anything they possess. Since they understand that phenomena are devoid of intrinsic nature, selfless, and merely arise from conditions, they give away even their own flesh without any discouraged mind arising. If that is the case, it goes without saying that they give away external objects. If they were to be stingy, that would not exist in any way, because they are free from attachment. ^1-461

###### **ཕལ་པས་དེ་ལྟར་མ་བཏང་བའི་སྐྱོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-5-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-462]]

The second, "Perception of a self," and so forth, refers to one verse. For foolish people who are attached because they perceive a self where there is no self in reality, and who grasp at objects such as the eyes as "mine," how could such a mind of giving exist? It does not. Those who are stingy will be reborn in the realm of hungry ghosts, and even if, by some chance, they are reborn as humans, they will be poor during that time. ^1-462

###### **དེ་ལྟར་ཤེས་ནས་སྦྱིན་པ་ལ་མོས་པར་བྱ་བའི་ཚུལ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-5-1-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-463]]

The third, "At that time, the Bodhisattva," and so forth, refers to one verse. In this way, at the time of being born in existence, Bodhisattvas, knowing that sentient beings have become poor due to the power of stinginess, subdue the mind of stinginess within themselves and become inclined toward the intention of giving. Through their practice, they always give away all their possessions freely and without obstruction. For if one were to leave these four continents, which are well-adorned by their arrangement, without giving them away, they would become conditioned things of an impermanent nature, possessing no essence to be relied upon—like the dust of spittle, which is like the droplets of spittle cast away. Knowing them to be without essence, in order to extract the essence, they give those continents away to others, and the Bodhisattva becomes joyful; however, if they were to receive those continents back, they would not be joyful in that way, for the very reason just explained. 
How the practice of giving should be performed: ^1-463

###### **ཚུལ་ཇི་ལྟར་སྦྱིན་པ་བཏང་བར་བྱ་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-5-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-464]]

The second, the manner of how to give, has two parts: the actual practice and its benefits. ^1-464

###### **དངོས།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-5-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-465]]

The first has three parts: giving for the sake of others through compassion, possessing the dedication of skillful means, and giving without attachment to maturation or the objects themselves. ^1-465

###### **སྙིང་རྗེས་གཞན་དོན་དུ་གཏོང་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-5-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-466]]

The first, "Bodhisattva," and so forth, refers to three lines. A wise and clear-minded Bodhisattva, even when giving a gift as small as a single mouthful of food, does so with a preceding intention of compassion, thinking: "May this gift be given in such a way that all sentient beings, as many as are included within the three realms of existence, may attain faultless prosperity, may the fruits of all those who have entered the three vehicles be accomplished, and may all attain the unsurpassed fruit of Buddhahood." Thus, they perform the act of giving. ^1-466

###### **ཐབས་མཁས་པས་ཡོངས་སུ་བསྔོ་བ་དང་ལྡན་པ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-5-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-467]]

The second, "That also," and so forth, refers to one line. That merit of having given a gift is also dedicated for the sake of the benefit of all realms of wandering beings, so that I and all sentient beings may fully awaken to supreme enlightenment. ^1-467

###### **རྣམ་སྨིན་དང་དངོས་པོ་ལ་མ་ཆགས་པར་གཏོང་བ།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-5-2-1-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-468]]

The third, "Having given a gift," and so forth, refers to two lines. In this way, a Bodhisattva, having given a gift, does not allow their mind to dwell in a manner of longing for or being attached to the substance to be given or the object of its return, nor do they dwell in a manner of clinging to signs. Furthermore, that Bodhisattva never has the expectation that "the maturation of that gift will become my own," for they give for the sake of sentient beings without attachment to the substance or its maturation. ^1-468

###### **ཕན་ཡོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-5-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-469]]

The second, regarding the benefits, has two parts: general and specific. ^1-469

###### **སྤྱི།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-5-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-470]]

The first, "In this way, giving," and so forth, refers to two lines. A Bodhisattva who gives in the way just explained is one who is skilled in giving through being endowed with the mental engagement of skillful means, and who gives without attachment to all of their possessions and the maturation thereof. Through such a method, even when giving a small object, it becomes the giving of an immeasurable, vast gift, and the maturation of that gift likewise becomes immeasurable in that same degree. ^1-470

###### **བྱེ་བྲག** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-5-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-471]]

The second, regarding the specific, has two parts: the benefits of generosity endowed with skillful means such as compassion, and the benefits of giving without attachment. ^1-471

###### **སྙིང་རྗེ་སོགས་ཐབས་མཁས་པ་དང་ལྡན་པའི་སྦྱིན་པའི་ཕན་ཡོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-5-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-472]]

The first, "In the three realms," and so forth, refers to three verses. Taking as an example the entirety of all sentient beings existing in the three realms, without exception, [it is said that] if all those sentient beings were to give vast offerings for an immeasurable eon to the World-Knower, the Buddha, the Blessed One, and to the Śrāvaka Arhats and Pratyekabuddhas, and were to diligently seek the fruit thereof, the Śrāvaka enlightenment—[then] a Bodhisattva who is skilled in the Great Vehicle and endowed with skillful means, in a single moment of completing the mental task, having rejoiced in the merit that constitutes the basis of those just described, ^1-472

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-473]]

If one dedicates [that merit] to the supreme and excellent state of Buddhahood for the sake of all sentient beings, then by generating even a single moment of mind dedicated in that way, one surpasses the roots of virtue of all wandering beings. For example, just as a single wish-fulfilling jewel, the king of gems, the vaidurya, is able to surpass a vast heap of many gathered trinkets, in the same way, a Bodhisattva, through the gateway of rejoicing in a marvelous intention, surpasses all the extremely vast heaps of generosity of all other wandering beings. ^1-473

###### **མ་ཆགས་པར་བཏང་བའི་ཕན་ཡོན།** ^1-3-2-2-1-2-5-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-474]]

The second, "If a Bodhisattva," and so forth, refers to one verse. If a Bodhisattva gives gifts to wandering beings, does not claim the merit of that generosity as his own, and gives in such a way that he is entirely without attachment, having no miserliness toward the objects to be given, then the roots of virtue in such giving will increase more and more, possessing great power to complete the accumulations and abandon obscurations. The opposing factor of generosity is miserliness caused by attachment, but here, because that attachment is rendered entirely without opportunity and one gives, it is like the moon in the waxing phase, which possesses clear rays and is free of clouds. ^1-474

###### ལམ་གྱི་འབྲས་བུ། ^1-3-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-475]]

The Fruition of the Path. Second, regarding the explanation of the fruition of the path, there are two parts: the fruition of the specific path and the fruition of the fully perfected path. ^1-475

###### **ལམ་བྱེ་བྲག་པའི་འབྲས་བུ།** ^1-3-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-476]]

The Fruition of the Specific Path. First, this is taught by sixteen lines or four verses, beginning with "By the Bodhisattva’s generosity." In this way, there is no aspect of the Bodhisattva’s path that is not subsumed within the six perfections; therefore, it is taught that the extensive scriptures of the Mahayana and the perfection of wisdom are also, in meaning, contained therein. Having already well explained the practices of these six perfections above, now their fruition is explained: "By the Bodhisattva’s generosity," the continuity of birth in the realms of hungry ghosts is severed, and likewise, poverty of resources and all afflictions are also severed. By giving gifts without apprehending them, one abandons all afflictions such as miserliness and ignorance, and by giving the gift of Dharma to others, one also causes all of their afflictions to be severed. When the Bodhisattva engages in these practices, in all lifetimes they obtain limitless and inexhaustible, increasing abundance of whatever resources they desire. By gathering beings through the gifts of material things and Dharma, they cause those sentient beings who dwell in the causes and results of suffering to be fully ripened. ^1-476

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-477]]

Through ethical discipline, one abandons the various and manifold natures of the animal realm, such as their different classes and forms, and furthermore, one also abandons the eight states of non-freedom; by that discipline, one always attains a fortunate basis of leisure. Through patience, one attains the supreme, vast, and excellent form, adorned with the marks and signs, up to the very body of a Buddha; it becomes beautiful like the color of gold and worthy of being looked upon by the world. Through diligence, by the power of the virtues of the white qualities not diminishing but increasing from one stage to the next, one attains the treasury of the Buddha’s limitless wisdom. Through meditative concentration, one abandons the objects of desire of the desire realm, which are worthy of being disparaged due to their great faults and meager happiness, and one accomplishes the three knowledges—knowledge of former states, knowledge of the exhaustion of defilements, and knowledge of the mind—as well as the three higher knowledges—the divine ear, the divine eye, and miraculous powers—which, when combined with the former, make six, and one also accomplishes meditative concentrations such as the "lion’s play" and so forth. Through the perfection of wisdom, one fully understands the nature of all phenomena as emptiness, abandons the grasping at things as real, and thus truly transcends the three realms without remainder; having become a Buddha, the supreme among men, the Dharma King who possesses the two bodies, one turns the precious wheel of enlightened activity that exercises sovereignty throughout all world systems without interruption, and in order to bring all suffering to a permanent end, one also reveals the supreme light of the holy Dharma to all beings. ༈ The Fruition of the Fully Perfected Path. ^1-477

###### **ལམ་ཡོངས་སུ་རྫོགས་པའི་འབྲས་བུ།** ^1-3-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-478]]

"The second [point] concerns the single verse beginning with 'Having perfected this dharma...' For anyone abiding in the lineage of the Great Vehicle, from the moment they first generate the mind for enlightenment, they practice—by way of skillful means—this entire dharma of the Great Vehicle path, the six perfections as taught, for three countless eons and so forth. What are these skillful means? It is to bind all these perfections together into a single purpose without separation; in this way, they practice them such that each serves as a companion to the others, and all are practiced in their entirety. Furthermore, they practice them in such a way that they all become the essence of the perfection of wisdom that possesses all supreme aspects. By practicing in this way, having 'perfected' the practice of all the perfections to their ultimate limit, that bodhisattva, by means of a wisdom endowed with a single moment, is able to manifest the dharma-dhātu, which possesses all supreme aspects and is devoid of any lack of purity, encompassing all undefiled phenomena. Having attained the great wisdom that is the final continuum of the ten stages, and relying upon that, they progress from the path of learning to the path of no-learning, becoming perfectly and fully awakened to the level of great enlightenment. By the power of having attained the ultimate transformation of basis, they thus 'perfectly purify' the buddha-field—the realm of Akaniṣṭha that is their own manifestation—extending to the limits of space, without being restricted to directions or parts. They 'perfectly hold' as their retinue the pure beings who are the great bodhisattvas of the ten stages. By attaining the wisdom body that is equal to all buddhas, they 'perfectly hold' the buddha-lineage, which is the nature of the three bodies, manifesting throughout the existence of the world-systems of the ten directions to the limits of space, in a manner that has no limits of direction or time. They also 'perfectly hold' the infinite lineage of the dharma-method of scripture and realization. Likewise, they 'perfectly hold' the infinite lineage of the noble saṅgha who act according to that buddha-dharma in the world-systems of the ten directions. And by means of their great enlightened activity, they 'perfectly hold' all phenomena of benefit and happiness that pertain to both existence and peace. Because the wisdom body, which pervades the dharma-dhātu, has reached the ultimate of abandonment and realization, it is permanent, stable, and unchanging, like space; and by means of great enlightened activity that is spontaneously present like a wish-fulfilling jewel, they provide benefit and happiness for as long as the limits of cyclic existence endure, having attained the ultimate of fruition." ^1-478

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-479]]

The conclusion of that. ^1-479

##### དེ་ལྟར་བསྟན་པའི་དགོས་པས་མཇུག་བསྡུ་བ། ^1-3-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-480]]

The third, the conclusion by way of the purpose of the teaching as stated in that manner, is the single verse beginning with "Healing the illnesses of beings." The Buddha is the supreme physician who acts to heal, by way of pacifying from the root, the illnesses of the body and mind of beings without exception. The root of all illnesses, which are sufferings, is the grasping at things as real, and all the afflictions that arise from that, as well as all the decline of cyclic existence and peace that arise from the obscurations of afflictions and of knowledge, are healed by the Tathāgatas through the nectar of the dharma of the perfection of wisdom. Such a king of physicians, the Buddha Bhagavān Śākya-siṃha himself, has spoken this, which contains all the profound and vast dharma of the Great Vehicle, the path of supreme enlightenment, when asked what the perfection of wisdom—the subject matter—is and what its specific qualities are. The name of this sūtra is "The Precious Qualities of the Perfection of Wisdom, the Path of Supreme Enlightenment," because it contains and gathers all the precious qualities of the path and fruit of the world and what transcends the world. The purpose of speaking in this way is for all the infinite sentient beings to attain such a path. It is the legacy of fulfilling the vow made since beginningless eons to attain supreme enlightenment oneself for the sake of protecting all sentient beings. By showing this ultimate, profound dharma, which he had realized through his great compassion that is without object, to those to be tamed, those who were the immediate disciples at that time heard it directly from his lips, and those who were distant disciples heard it through transmission. By hearing it in this way, they discerned the objects; by contemplating it, they understood the meaning; and by meditating upon it, they realized it, thereby gradually becoming Buddhas. In this manner, the Buddhas, whose wisdom-bodies have become equal in all directions and throughout infinite time, establish sentient beings on the path of unsurpassed enlightenment through these words and this meaning. ^1-480

### མཇུག་གི་དོན། ^1-4-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-481]]

The Concluding Meaning. Regarding the fourth section, the concluding meaning, there are two parts: concluding the scope with words that indicate the remaining text in its entirety, and stating the translation colophon for the purpose of showing the authenticity of the source. The first is the statement: "The Noble Verse Summary of the Perfection of Wisdom is complete." ^1-481

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-482]]

The second is the statement: "Translated, edited, and finalized by the Indian preceptor Vidyākarasiṃha and the chief editor and translator, the monk dPal brtsegs." With these, the explanation of the direct meaning of the Noble Verse Summary of the Perfection of Wisdom, Precious Qualities, has been completed. ^1-482

## ཤུགས་བསྟན་ལམ་གྱི་མངོན་རྟོགས་ལྟར་བཤད་པ། ^2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^2-1]]

The second section of the root outline, which connects to the stages of clear realization, should be understood from what is available separately. ^2-1

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^2-2]]

Here it is said: The great ocean of the Buddha’s excellent speech,
The essence of which is the perfection of wisdom,
Its precious, priceless, and ultimate heart
Is this jewel that gathers precious qualities.
Whatever dwells in the expanse of the profound and peaceful mind
Of the form body, the supreme sign of the full moon,
This kind of Dharma system, well-drawn from the throat of glory,
Is rare in all the world.
Recalling the final testament of the supreme speaker of truth,
Who said it is a pity that the perfection of wisdom,
Even a mere corner of its words, should decline
When all the systems of the Dharma are in decline.
The scripture that the Sugata directly spoke,
Which even bodhisattvas of the ten grounds, numerous as Ganges sands,
Cannot express even a fraction of its wisdom—
My joy in explaining, listening to, and composing it increases greatly.
Look here, O sons of noble family, this Dharma
Is the profound, ultimate, and vast treasury of Dharma;
Rejoice that this is the supreme causal-concordant
Of the wisdom of the Victor, which perfectly completes the two accumulations.
The stages of practice and clear realization
Are also greatly clarified by the meaning, and the excellent, stainless system
That explains the intent of the direct teaching
Is composed by way of scripture, oral instructions, and reasoning.
By the virtue of this, in all my lives,
May I never turn away from the excellent path that pleases the Victor,
Which well-realizes the meaning of the union of the profound emptiness
And dependent arising.
From now until the attainment of buddhahood, may I,
In life after life, increase my wisdom,
And with great compassion that abandons self-cherishing,
May I practice the benefit of beings through supreme skillful means.
With a mind of gratitude toward the supreme Victor,
May I uphold the entire treasury of the Victor’s Dharma,
And like the bodhisattvas Samantabhadra and Mañjuśrī,
May I spread the Victor’s enlightened activity until the end of existence.
May all who see, hear, or think of this text,
And all who have a connection with me through good or bad karma,
Swiftly clear away the darkness of ignorance
And dwell upon the stage of non-retrogression.
By the truth of this Dharma being well-explained according to reality,
May all the Victors and their sons
Generate the glory of eloquence and wisdom in those who uphold this,
And may they be victorious in all directions.
May the harmful thoughts and actions of demons, barbarians, and the like,
Who are the enemies of the teachings,
And all black-hearted humans and non-humans,
Be completely pacified in the direction where the volumes of this text reside.
May all the darkness of false appearances that mimic the Dharma
Be destroyed at the root by the blessing of this,
And by the power of the blazing light of the true Dharma,
May the benefit and happiness of beings increase more and more.
Even in this darkness where the dregs of time prevail,
May the moon-like superior intention increase,
And may the cooling rays of benefiting others, radiating millions of lights,
Rise as if satisfied with their own beauty.
May the supreme holders of the teachings live long,
May the patrons of the teachings also increase in prosperity,
And may all beings develop faith in the heart of the teachings,
So that the precious teachings may flourish supremely.
May all the oceans of sentient beings, filling the ends of space,
Be well-established by me alone on the excellent path
Of the perfection of wisdom,
And may they all become fully awakened buddhas.
In the regions where this text spreads,
May sickness, poverty, and conflict be pacified,
And may the feast of auspiciousness, where intended purposes are fulfilled according to the Dharma,
Increase more and more.
Thus, this commentary, which enters without error into the intended meaning of the Victor’s Mother, was composed because of my own deep devotion to this Dharma since my youth, and from the desire to plant good imprints of the profound Dharma in my own continuum. As for the causal conditions for this, the co-operative condition was the repeated and earnest urging of the practitioner of the Early Translation’s Great Perfection, the vidyādhara Sna tshogs rang grol, as well as the persistent urging of the great master of the Early Translation tradition, the holy lama Lung rtogs, and the urging of many Dharma speakers such as the learned one named Mdo sngags. Relying on these, I, Mi pham ’jam dpal dgyes pa’i rdo rje, whose trust in this Dharma system had become firm, relied upon the excellent explanations of the Indian and Tibetan commentaries. In my forty-seventh year, the year of the Water Dragon, I began this on the fourteenth day of the sixth Hor month at the place blessed by the Victorious Lotus-Born, known as the upper glorious Ke’u tshang of the white Stag tshang, called Padma bde ldan gling. I wrote it as time and leisure permitted, and completed it fully on the twenty-eighth day of that same month, an auspicious time associated with the constellation Puṣya, and other perfect conditions. May this, by spreading in all directions and times, cause the benefit of the teachings and all sentient beings without exception to be wondrous and uninterrupted. Sarvamaṅgalam. ^2-2
